#5004. Getting More Physical Activity May Help With Your Perimenopause Symptoms

  • A new study reports that exercising more frequently may help alleviate perimenopause symptoms. 
  • While increased physical activity was linked to improved perimenopause symptoms overall, the strongest association was with psychological symptoms like anxiety.
  • Finding exercises you enjoy and creating accountability can help increase your physical activity level.

A new study found that exercising more frequently may help alleviate perimenopause symptoms like hot flashes, anxiety, and urinary issues. 

The study results were recently presented at The Menopause Society’s 2026 Annual Conference. 

Perimenopause is the transitional period leading up to menopause. 

Due to hormonal changes that mark the end of a person’s reproductive years, symptoms like hot flashes, mood swings, brain fog, insomnia, and urinary incontinence are common.

Past studies show that lifestyle modifications, such as eating a healthy diet, managing stress, and getting enough physical activity, can help reduce some perimenopause symptoms. 

“Physical activity is low cost, accessible, and already known to benefit sleep, mood, and heart and bone health — all areas that matter a great deal during perimenopause,” said study lead and co-author Liudmila Zhaunova, PhD, director of science at Flo Health — the developer of a women’s health tracking app, which was used to collect health data for this study. “But when we looked at the research, there was surprisingly little evidence specific to perimenopause.”

“Hormone therapy isn’t suitable or desired for everyone, so women deserve a broader set of evidence-based options,” Zhaunova told Healthline. “The more we understand about accessible, lifestyle-based approaches, the better equipped women and their healthcare providers are to build a plan that works for them.”

Higher rates of exercise lower perimenopause symptoms overall

Study researchers recruited more than 1,800 women from around the globe who were using the Flo app. 

Study participants were asked to complete online surveys about their physical activity, from not being physically active to exercising more than 5 days a week. 

Participants were also asked to report on their perimenopause symptoms in three categories — somatic (body-based issues like hot flashes), psychological (mental health issues like mood changes), and urogenital (issues regarding the urinary or vaginal areas). Researchers rated the severity of these symptoms using the Menopause Rating Scale (MRS).

The researchers found that higher rates of physical activity were associated with fewer perimenopausal symptoms across all three categories. 

“Perimenopause symptoms are wide-ranging, and women often experience several at once,” Zhaunova said. “Seeing a lower symptom burden across all three domains suggests that being more active may be relevant to women’s overall experience, not just one symptom.” 

Mental health benefits of exercise were most pronounced

Additionally, the scientists discovered that the greatest exercise benefit was observed in the reduction of perimenopausal psychological symptoms. 

“Psychological symptoms, such as low mood, irritability, anxiety, and exhaustion, are among the most disruptive parts of perimenopause, and they’re often overlooked or not recognized as related to this life stage,” Zhaunova explained.  

“Our findings suggest that physical activity may matter most for mood and anxiety-related symptoms,” she continued.

“This could help women and clinicians have more informed conversations about how to support mental well-being during perimenopause. That said, women with more symptoms may find it harder to exercise, so we need further research to understand the direction of the relationship.”

How exercise may improve perimenopause symptoms

Sherry Ross, MD, a board certified OB-GYN and Women’s Health Expert at Providence Saint John’s Health Center in Santa Monica, CA, said that regular exercise could potentially help relieve perimenopause symptoms like hot flashes and mood changes. Ross wasn’t involved in this study, but noted other benefits of exercising during this life stage:

  • body flexibility and strength
  • body image and self-esteem
  • bone health
  • cardiovascular health
  • energy levels
  • immune system strength
  • mental health
  • sleep quality
  • weight management

“Exercise releases endorphins, improves blood vessel health, lowers inflammation, helps reset circadian rhythm, and improves the body’s internal cooling system,” Ross told Healthline. “Collectively, these changes ease anxiety, irritability, mood changes, sleep, and fatigue.”

“There were mixed reviews on how exercise benefits hot flashes, but exercise does help with weight control, which may benefit hot flashes,” she said. “Strengthening the pelvic floor and core may help with the urinary symptoms women many experience in perimenopause.”

Exercise tips for perimenopausal women

Prudence Hall, MD, an OB-GYN in private practice in Santa Monica, CA, and author of “Radiant Again & Forever,” said that building a sustainable exercise routine requires consistency, joy, and community. Hall wasn’t involved in the study, but shared some exercise tips for perimenopausal women.

  • Create built-in accountability: Schedule recurring exercise sessions with a friend, whether meeting in person or syncing up virtually.
  • Prioritize enjoyment over punishment: Choose activities that genuinely make you happy, whether that is Pilates, tennis, racquetball, dance, or short high intensity interval training (HIIT) circuits. “When exercise feels like play rather than a chore, consistency follows naturally,” Hall said. 
  • Remove logistical friction: Work with a trainer if possible, or build an accessible library of on-demand online classes that fit your schedule.
  • Connect movement to cellular health: Remember that exercise is not just about weight management; it literally rebuilds your body’s cellular engine. “Reconnecting with physical strength and seeing a vibrant, energized body emerge is one of the most empowering experiences a woman can have during midlife,” Hall said. 

“Too often in midlife, previously stable women suddenly plummet into deep depression and anxiety, only to be prescribed multiple medications with debilitating side effects that never address the underlying biology,” Hall said.

“While hormone optimization remains essential to correct these endocrine deficiencies, exercise serves as a powerful, non-pharmaceutical therapy that directly targets the true root causes by revitalizing our cellular health and restoring overall vitality.” 

#5005. Letter from the Editor-in-Chief: The Good and the Hard of Life on GLP-1s

  • GLP-1 medications like semaglutide and tirzepatide can lead to significant weight loss and may help reduce inflammation, improve mobility, and quiet persistent thoughts about food. Many people report life changing results after years of unsuccessful diets.
  • A Healthline story on Mayim Bialik’s GLP-1 side effects became our most engaged Facebook post of the year, as thousands of commenters shared hard side effects and life changing results. Research also increasingly links GLP-1s to benefits beyond weight loss.
  • Healthline’s Editor-in-Chief Robin Hough talks with his mom, Kitty, who lost 56 pounds on tirzepatide, and a registered nurse who has lost over 50 pounds, about the benefits, side effects, and cost of long-term treatment.

“Years of diets that worked for a couple of months, then gave all the weight back and more. Now the food noise has gone quiet,” a GLP-1 user shared.

In June, our brilliant News team published a piece about actor Mayim Bialik and her challenging experience with GLP-1 side effects. It did well, like many of the celebrity + health condition + human-interest angle features we create tend to do with our audience (that’s you all reading this!). Not a massive viral hit by any measure, but a strong performer on the traffic front.

Life in a busy newsroom went on. 

Jump forward nearly 4 months; our equally brilliant social media team posts the piece on Facebook, leading with one of Bialik’s quotes from the article: “GLP-1s have helped people in serious need. Of that I am certain. But nobody talks much about what happens when it goes wrong.” 

The fuse was lit. Nearly 12k likes, 1k reposts, and 3.5k comments later, the team had their most engaged post of the year by some margin. The irony is not lost on me that Bialik’s quote, about how rarely people talk about side effects, sparked such a deep conversation about that very thing. 

GLP-1s are a big topic for Healthline. They drive traffic, they drive revenue, and they offer a rich seam of human-centric storytelling for our teams. Even so, no one predicted the post would catch fire the way it did. Our social team’s read is that a few things stacked up: Mayim Bialik is a name our Facebook audience knows, the topic is one where nearly everyone has a stake, and a busy thread tends to get shown to more people, who then add more comments. That’s the hypothesis. What we’ve consistently seen is that posts that lead with a quote get more people talking than those that lead with a headline. You may have noticed this article takes the same approach.

What the GLP-1 conversation on Facebook revealed

We also know that the comment sections on social media aren’t always the most hospitable of environments, particularly in the health space. What stood out about the lion’s share of the comments we read was how constructive the discourse was. People were leading with deeply personal and often moving perspectives about the life changing impact (for good and bad) of GLP-1s. In turn, they were met with equally balanced and thoughtful responses. 

Many of the comments expanded on Bialik’s experience, detailing long and difficult battles with a range of side effects. But plenty told the other side of the story: years of failed diets and struggles with chronic conditions leading to despondency, side effects that went on to ease with time, and life changing results that were ultimately worth the cost.

My mom’s experience with tirzepatide

The line that opened this piece could have come from any of those commenters. It didn’t. It came from Kitty Hough, who is 75 and lives in London. Eagle-eyed readers will have spotted that she has the same surname as me – that’s because she’s my mom. She’s one of two people closer to the Healthline home who spoke to me for this piece and whose stories echo the Facebook post thread, with the good and the hard bits side by side.

Kitty was a hockey player and distance runner as a girl, the kind who could eat what she liked without it showing. Then came children, and a weight that crept up year after year, with decades of diets that “worked for a couple of months only to be back to my former weight and then some.” In late 2024, after a lot of research, she decided to try tirzepatide.

Since then, Kitty has lost 56 pounds. She says her joints hurt less, which she puts down to reduced inflammation. She is more mobile. The constant chatter about food has faded. She thinks harder before she eats now. 

“I am a strong believer that obesity is a disease and in my case certainly not the result of overindulgence or laziness,” she says. “GLP-1s have greatly improved my health and self-esteem. After years of struggling with my weight, I have finally found a way to solve the problem.”

Kitty will also tell you what it cost. Nausea. Constipation. Sulfur-tasting burps and rough nights of indigestion. Initial hair loss that has since grown back, and loose skin. Muscle loss. And she misses some of the true joy of cooking and eating food. 

But despite bumps in her journey, Kitty remains convinced that the benefits of GLP-1s for her life and health outweigh any drawbacks she’s experienced. “I am conscious that I will probably be on this drug for a long time if not for life. It is truly a miracle drug.” 

A healthcare professional’s view on the cost of GLP-1s

I also spoke to Ann Marie Maguire, AGNP-C, RN, TCTCN, a nurse practitioner specializing in transplantation and cellular therapy, the sister of Healthline’s Chief Business Officer Christine Macguire, and someone who has lost over 50lbs since starting GLP-1s in 2022. 

“I struggled with my weight since my teens and always believed I should be able to manage it on my own. But in my 30s, my knees were hurting just getting out of bed or walking to dinner. I realized how much my weight was affecting my quality of life. Starting a GLP-1 in 2022, alongside healthier eating and exercise, made a tremendous difference. My only regret is not starting sooner,” said Ann Marie. 

As a healthcare professional, Ann Marie also identified cost as a major factor that can’t be ignored regarding GLP-1s and their impact. 

“There’s understandable debate about the long-term use and cost of these medications,” she says. “But we also need to consider the health and financial burden of obesity, including cardiovascular complications, hospitalizations, surgeries and ongoing medications. The cost conversation should account for the potential benefits of preventing or reducing those complications.”

Ann Marie points to a frustration many patients will recognize: reaching a healthier weight can become the reason coverage gets cut off. “We don’t automatically stop blood pressure medication because a patient’s readings improve,” she says. The treatment may be what’s keeping the condition controlled. Obesity, she argues, deserves the same approach. “Reaching a goal weight can mean treatment is working, and decisions about continuing it should reflect the patient’s ongoing needs, benefits and side effects.”

The bigger picture on GLP-1s

It’s important to note that this article looks at the experiences of only a handful of people out of the millions who now take GLP-1s. Like all drugs, these medications are not right for everyone. Side effects are common; some of them can be serious, and both the long-term impact and what happens when people stop are still being worked out. Ultimately, your care team, with knowledge of your medical history, is a far better guide than a comment thread.

Yet the evidence now reaches well beyond the scales: large trials have linked semaglutide to fewer heart attacks and strokes in people with heart disease and slower kidney decline in people with diabetes and kidney disease, and tirzepatide became the first FDA-approved drug for moderate to severe sleep apnea in adults with obesity.

Stories like Kitty’s, Ann Marie’s, Mayim Bialik’s, and those of the 3.5k commenters on the Facebook post aren’t proof of what will happen to you one way or another. But for a lot of people, my mom included, these drugs have changed what an ordinary day looks like, and in many cases changed what their future can look like. 

One of the most significant drug developments of the century so far? Hard to argue with. 

Robin Hough is the Editor-in-Chief of Healthline, and in addition to this regular Letter from the Editor column, he can be found every other Wednesday in our Healthline Wellness Wire newsletter.

#5006. ADHD Medications May Help Ease Menopausal Brain Fog

  • A recent review found that ADHD medications like lisdexamfetamine (Vyvanse) and atomoxetine (Strattera) may help improve self-reported symptoms of brain fog in perimenopausal and postmenopausal women.
  • Menopausal brain fog — including forgetfulness, slow thinking, and difficulty concentrating — may be linked to hormonal fluctuations that affect brain function during perimenopause and menopause.
  • Experts caution it’s too early to recommend ADHD medications for menopausal brain fog due to limited research, potential risks like cardiovascular effects, and lack of FDA approval for this use.

Medications commonly used to treat attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder (ADHD) may help reduce certain symptoms of menopausal brain fog, according to a review presented at the 2026 Annual Meeting of the Menopause Society. The findings have not yet been published in a peer-reviewed journal.

The research suggests that both lisdexamfetamine (Vyvanse) and atomoxetine (Strattera) may improve self-reported executive function in perimenopausal and postmenopausal people. However, they showed little to no effect on objective cognitive tests.

While this scoping review showed promising results, experts caution that it may be too early to begin recommending these medications to females experiencing menopause. 

Sara Vaughn, MD, Chief Medical Officer at Clair Health, who wasn’t involved in the study, told Healthline that these findings are interesting and hypothesis-generating, but they do not yet establish ADHD medications as a treatment for menopausal brain fog.

Connection between ADHD-like symptoms and menopause

Many middle-aged females report experiencing forgetfulness, slow thinking, reduced concentration, and difficulty finding words. This is often referred to as “brain fog,” and is a common occurrence during various stages of menopause. 

These symptoms of brain fog also mirror the symptoms of ADHD.  

Brain fog may be due to declining or fluctuating levels of estrogen during perimenopause, which affects dopamine and norepinephrine signaling in the prefrontal cortex and the hippocampus. This can impact memory, focus, and executive functioning. 

Despite how disruptive these symptoms can be to quality of life, there is currently no medication approved by the Food and Drug Administration (FDA) to manage them. This often leaves clinicians in a position of trying to differentiate between brain fog from menopause and undiagnosed ADHD. 

The review analyzed five studies involving females ages 45–60 with their last menstrual period within the past 10 years. The studies all focused on how ADHD medications affect outcome measures of cognition and ADHD severity. 

The researchers found that with lisdexamfetamine, scores on the Brown Attention Deficit Disorder Scale (BADDS), a self-report rating scale that measures ADHD symptoms, dropped from 35.7 to 21.2, compared with 29.8 with a placebo. With atomoxetine, BADDS scores decreased from 38.6 to 25.5, compared with 30.1 with a placebo.

There was no significant benefit observed on objective cognitive tests (ex., memory and processing speed). 

These findings suggest that certain ADHD medications may improve perceived cognitive symptoms during menopause, although benefits were not demonstrated on objective cognitive tests.

Jennifer Tat, a medical student at the University of Tennessee Health Sciences Center and lead author of the study, noted, “In the population data, symptom severity, not a formal ADHD diagnosis, predicted menopausal burden. Many women were never diagnosed, so relying on a chart diagnosis misses the women most at risk.”

“We have evidence that the menopause transition is associated with measurable brain changes,” said Vaughn. “But no specific brain signature of menopausal brain fog has been established. To study it rigorously, we first need to define what the term means and measure it consistently.”

Why experts say it may be too soon to recommend these medications

Limitations of this review include the small number of studies and the small sample sizes. Most of the measures were also self-reported, which leaves room for error. 

Also, the FDA has not approved these types of medications for menopausal brain fog, meaning using them for these purposes would be off-label. 

“ADHD medications are not without risks, and I think it is too early to use them routinely for menopausal ‘brain fog,’” said Vaughn. 

ADHD medications can carry risks, including cardiovascular side effects. Stimulants such as lisdexamfetamine also have the potential for misuse and dependence. These medications should only be used with medical supervision.

“I am a strong proponent of treating the underlying disorder rather than treating isolated symptoms. ADHD and menopausal cognitive symptoms may look alike, but that does not mean they share the same underlying mechanism—or require the same treatment,” noted Vaughn.

Further research, including additional long-term prospective studies, is needed before clinical guidance can be changed

“We hope this opens up the conversations with patients and providers. We recommend clinicians to screen patients for ADHD and, for those who screen positive, refer them to our psychiatric colleagues who have the knowledge and resources to perform a diagnostic test so that those women who have gone undiagnosed with ADHD can get the treatment they need and deserve,” said Tat.  

Tips to help manage menopausal brain fog 

According to the National Council on Aging (NCOA), your brain requires exercise to function optimally, just like your muscles do. 

Ways to help keep your brain sharp and manage menopausal brain fog include: 

  • eating a nutrient-rich diet, such as the MIND diet 
  • exercising regularly
  • quitting or avoiding smoking
  • limiting your alcohol intake

The NCOA also suggests keeping your brain well-exercised by: 

  • doing a mental exercise each day, such as a crossword, sudoku, or a word search 
  • taking up a new activity, such as gardening, knitting, or anything else involving physical coordination
  • memorizing your shopping list before going to the store
  • engaging in games that stretch your brain, such as chess or bridge

You can also speak with your healthcare professional about how menopause, estrogen levels, hormone therapy, and modifiable risk factors like a sedentary lifestyle or high blood pressure can affect cognitive health. 

“I want clinicians to know that they must take brain fog seriously. Ask whether the symptoms are new or a lifelong pattern that menopause has worsened. ADHD in women often goes unrecognized for decades,” said Tat.

#5007. Why Living with Endometriosis May Double Your Odds of Migraine

  • Those with endometriosis had 2.3 times the odds of also having migraine, according to a new analysis of more than 13 million females.
  • The researchers said the two conditions may share a pain pathway.
  • A planned clinical trial will test whether migraine medication can relieve endometriosis pain.

In a new analysis, researchers found females with endometriosis were 2.3 times more likely to also have migraine, and they said the two conditions may share a pain pathway involving nerves and inflammation.

Recognizing that connection earlier could lead to coordinated care for both conditions, the researchers said.

The analysis, presented at the American Neurological Association (ANA) Annual Meeting in San Diego on October 20, included more than 13 million females. The research hasn’t yet been published in a peer-reviewed scientific journal.

Endometriosis and migraine: What’s the link?

Endometriosis occurs when tissue similar to what lines the uterus grows outside the uterus, Mallory Stuparich, MD, a gynecologic surgeon at MemorialCare Miller Children’s & Women’s Hospital in Long Beach, California, told Healthline. Stuparich was not involved in the study.

Yohannes Woubishet Woldeamanuel, MD, lead author of the study and assistant professor of neurology at Mayo Clinic Arizona, told Healthline that migraine also involves more than one part of the body.

“Migraine is more than a headache,” he said.

Headache is one symptom, Woldeamanuel said, but migraine can also cause sensory, motor, and balance symptoms.

“Migraine is a little complex. It’s a multiple-nervous system involving a condition,” he said.

Endometriosis pain and the nervous system

Stuparich said endometriosis pain typically develops in 2 ways. The first starts in the lesions themselves.

“There are inflammatory markers that are created by the endometriosis lesions themselves, and those inflammatory markers can irritate the nerves that live close by the lesions,” she said.

The second involves the nervous system, Stuparich said. Scar tissue can grow its own nerve supply, and years of chronic pain can change how the nervous system responds. That adaptation can “perpetuate pain or make people feel sensations that normally wouldn’t be painful,” she said.

Stuparich said the inflammatory and immune processes involved in endometriosis have “pretty significant overlap in the setting of migraines.”

CGRP’s role in endometriosis and migraine

One possible link between the two conditions is calcitonin gene-related peptide (CGRP), a chemical messenger that helps transmit pain signals, the researchers said.

CGRP plays a key role in migraine attacks, Woldeamanuel said, and blocking it reduces those attacks. CGRP medications work by blocking the sites where CGRP attaches or by binding to CGRP itself.

Researchers have also found CGRP in endometriosis tissue.

A 2024 study published in Science Translational Medicine found CGRP in endometriosis lesions from both people and mice. In mice, blocking CGRP signaling reduced pain and reduced lesion size.

Research on genetics, inflammation, and hormones

Other studies point to shared biology between the two conditions:

  • Genetics. A 2020 study published in Genes found overlapping genetic risk for endometriosis and migraine, including genes tied to inflammation. The same study found no evidence that one condition causes the other.
  • Inflammation. A small 2025 study of 36 females, published in Neurology: Neuroimmunology & Neuroinflammation, found higher levels of some inflammation markers in people with both conditions than in people with migraine alone.
  • Hormones. Migraine affects two to three times as many females as males, according to a 2023 study published in the European Journal of Neurology. Hormonal changes can lower the pain threshold, Woldeamanuel said.

Stuparich said she was “not surprised at all” by the finding because it matches what she sees in her own patients.

“We see this overlap of patients who have endometriosis also having migraines,” she said.

Stuparich said the science still has gaps. She noted that nothing has been proven conclusively in peer-reviewed research yet, but the way both diseases behave supports a connection.

“It makes sense that there would be overlap,” she said.

Hidden overlap between endometriosis and migraine

Endometriosis and migraine are treated by different specialists, so the overlap is easy to miss, Woldeamanuel said.

For the analysis, researchers at Mayo Clinic Arizona reviewed 35 studies published over 50 years, comparing migraine rates among 207,675 females with endometriosis and 12,963,120 females without the condition.

Short-term health records miss up to 90% of that overlap, according to the analysis. Migraine rates in females with endometriosis changed depending on how each study identified migraine.

“This data is hiding,” Woldeamanuel said.

  • 1 year of billing codes: fewer than 5%
  • longer-term insurance claims: 18% to 43%
  • doctors interviewing patients directly: 54% to 78%

“These two clinics are siloed. One clinic doesn’t ask about the other condition. They just focus on their own specialty,” Woldeamanuel said.

The findings of the analysis have not been published in a peer-reviewed journal.

Endometriosis and migraine treatment options

Some treatments already overlap, Stuparich said. Hormonal birth control “can sometimes be used to treat menstrually associated migraines,” she said.

Stuparich said she has heard anecdotally of patients whose endometriosis symptoms improved after they started CGRP medications for migraine, though long-term data are still lacking.

The researchers said the findings support studying whether existing CGRP-blocking migraine medications could also relieve endometriosis pain, potentially offering one treatment for both conditions.

Woldeamanuel leads a planned Mayo Clinic trial testing one of those medications, rimegepant (Nurtec ODT), for endometriosis pain. The trial will also track migraine days in participants who have both conditions. It’s expected to finish in 2031, according to ClinicalTrials.gov.

Talking with your doctor about endometriosis and migraine

People who suspect they have both conditions can take steps now to get them treated together, Stuparich said.

Patients can raise the connection themselves, Stuparich said. She suggested a starting point for each appointment:

  • At a gynecology visit: Mention that you’ve “come across some information that endometriosis and migraines could be associated,” then ask, “What can I do about that?”
  • At a neurology visit: Describe painful periods that “really disrupt my quality of life,” then ask, “What can we do about that?”

Patients can also ask for an early referral to the other specialist so both conditions get coordinated care, Woldeamanuel said. “The earlier it’s diagnosed, the better it’s managed,” he said.

Stuparich said doctors are moving away from viewing endometriosis as only a gynecologic disorder because it can affect the whole body.

“It is a whole body systemic neuroinflammatory neuroimmune disorder,” she said.

#5008. RFK Jr.’s Revised Vaccine Schedule May Cause 83% Surge in Hepatitis B Infections

  • A new modeling study projects that delaying or omitting the hepatitis B birth dose could increase chronic infections among children by 83% to 225%, depending on vaccine uptake rates.
  • The CDC ended its universal recommendation for hepatitis B vaccination at birth in late 2025, shifting to individual-based decision making for infants whose mothers test negative for the virus.
  • Up to 90% of infants infected with hepatitis B during their first year develop chronic infection, which can lead to cirrhosis, liver failure, or liver cancer.
  • The American Academy of Pediatrics still recommends universal vaccination within 24 hours of birth.

Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr.’s proposed changes to the childhood vaccine schedule have raised alarm among public health experts and researchers.

Concerns include a rise in preventable infectious diseases such as measles, polio, and hepatitis B. 

A new study suggests that delaying or foregoing the hepatitis B shot for newborns could increase chronic infections among children.

In December 2025, the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) ended its recommendation for universal hepatitis B vaccination at birth for infants whose mothers test negative for the virus. Instead, the federal health agency recommends individual-based clinical decision making.

After reviewing the evidence, the CDC, which is overseen by the HHS, concluded that newborns “face little risk of contracting hepatitis,” which deemed the vaccine, typically administered at birth, unnecessary. 

Experts say this shift in public health policy wasn’t based on science, and researchers warn of rising infection rates. 

“Hepatitis B immunizations have been proven to protect individuals against a serious and significant disease,” said Graham Tse, MD, pediatrician and chief medical officer of MemorialCare Miller Children’s & Women’s Hospital in Long Beach, CA.

“The hepatitis B vaccines have several decades of history and have been proven safe and effective,” Tse said. “There has been no new data or evidence presented or uncovered to support delaying the newborn dose of hepatitis B.”

Here’s what parents should know about making informed decisions with their child’s healthcare team.

Delaying hepatitis B shot may raise infection risk by 83%

A U.S. modeling study published in Annals of Internal Medicine on October 20 projected that delaying the first vaccine dose until 2 months could increase chronic hepatitis B infections among infants by 19% under one scenario. 

With shared decision making and lower vaccine uptake, projected increases ranged from 83% to 225%, compared with universal birth-dose vaccination.

The researchers also estimated that up to 1.8 million young adults could remain susceptible to hepatitis B by age 20 under shared decision making, compared with 232,000 under universal birth dosing.

Another study published in JAMA Pediatrics in late April projected hundreds of additional chronic infections annually if the hepatitis B birth dose were delayed or omitted.

However, these figures are model-based projections, rather than verified outcomes. Actual results will depend on vaccination rates and other factors.

What changed in the hepatitis B vaccine schedule?

In December 2025, the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) adopted a recommendation shifting away from universal hepatitis B vaccination at birth for infants whose mothers test negative for hepatitis B surface antigen (HBsAg). 

Under the new approach, parents and healthcare professionals can decide together whether to give the first dose at birth or defer it until the baby is 2 months old.

The change followed an 8 to 3 vote by the Advisory Committee on Immunization Practices (ACIP), marking a departure from longstanding U.S. guidance.

However, infants born to mothers who test positive for hepatitis B or whose infection status is unknown should still receive the hepatitis B vaccine and hepatitis B immune globulin (HBIG) within 12 hours of birth, according to the updated guidelines.

Why is hepatitis B dangerous for infants?

Hepatitis B is a viral infection that affects the liver. 

Although some infections resolve, newborns and infants face a higher risk of developing lifelong hepatitis B infection compared with adults. 

Up to 90% of infants infected during their first year develop chronic hepatitis B.

“We have actual world evidence that vaccinating newborns for hepatitis B prevents hepatitis B infections later in life and the serious complications of that disease,” Tse said. 

Over time, chronic infection can lead to cirrhosis, liver failure, or liver cancer. 

“The danger of delaying the newborn hepatitis B shot is that individuals do not get vaccinated and thereby do not have protection against hepatitis B,” Tse said.

Early vaccination helps protect young infants before potential exposure occurs, including situations in which maternal screening misses an infection.

“Newborns can be infected with hepatitis B from a mother who is not aware they have a hepatitis B infection or from exposure later in life,” Tse said.  

“The risk is developing a hepatitis B infection, which can potentially lead to liver cancer, fulminant hepatitis, or severe end-stage liver disease.”

What should parents know about vaccine safety?

In contrast to the CDC, the American Academy of Pediatrics (AAP) still recommends a universal hepatitis B vaccine dose within 24 hours of birth, followed by additional doses during infancy. 

The leading pediatric medical organization has raised concerns about the potential consequences of reduced vaccination rates.

The hepatitis B vaccine has decades of safety and effectiveness data. 

Tse said the AAP’s vaccine schedule and recommendations are based on decades of study, use, and expert input, and provide the best protection against vaccine-preventable diseases. 

Parents who have questions about potential side effects, timing, or their baby’s individual risk should discuss them with a qualified healthcare professional.

“Parents are confused, given the recommendations from CDC, HHS, and Kennedy differ from established and respected groups such as the American Academy of Pediatrics, the Infectious Disease Society of America, and several states,” Tse said. 

“Parents desire to keep their children safe, and having differing opinions and confusion will lead to reduced vaccination rates as parents experience doubt as to whom to listen to or believe.”

Should your newborn get vaccinated against hepatitis B?

While the updated CDC policy offers more flexibility for some families, lower vaccination uptake could lead to an increase in chronic hepatitis B infections. 

Ask your pediatrician about the benefits of vaccination against hepatitis B at birth, the risks of delaying the first dose, and the recommended schedule for completing the series. 

You can also ask how maternal screening results affect your baby’s protection.

An informed conversation with your healthcare professional can help you weigh the evidence and choose a vaccination plan for your baby.

“I see no argument whatsoever for changing the established vaccination schedule for hepatitis B,” Tse said.

“There have been no new studies, no data, nor evidence to justify Kennedy’s proposed changes to the childhood immunization schedule. Delaying vaccinations or skipping vaccinations puts your child at risk for serious and significant diseases, which are all preventable,” Tse said.

#5009. Christina Applegate ‘Not Happy at All’ After Learning She Was Misdiagnosed With MS

  • Christina Applegate revealed that she was misdiagnosed with multiple sclerosis (MS) after sharing her diagnosis publicly in 2021.
  • While her doctor now says she doesn’t have MS, her symptoms have worsened, and the underlying cause remains under investigation.
  • Research suggests that around 15% of people diagnosed with MS may have another condition, with females twice as likely to be misdiagnosed.
  • Conditions often mistaken for MS include migraine, peripheral neuropathy, fibromyalgia, and radiologically isolated syndrome.
  • MS can be challenging to diagnose because no single test confirms it in every case. Diagnosis relies on a combination of symptoms, neurological examination, and MRI findings.

Christina Applegate shared an unexpected health update on her return to the MeSsy podcast with co-host Jamie-Lynn Sigler. 

The two actors have detailed their experiences with multiple sclerosis (MS), a neurological disorder that can lead to debilitating symptoms affecting daily functioning and quality of life. 

Applegate, 54, has been raising awareness about the condition since her initial diagnosis in 2021.

In two podcast episodes released October 20, Applegate revealed that doctors had told her she was misdiagnosed with MS.

“She came into my room and said, ‘Congratulations, you don’t have MS,’ ” Applegate said as she fought back tears. “I was, like, not happy at all. Everybody else was so happy.”

The “Anchorman” and “Dead to Me” actor explained that her symptoms have continued to worsen, leaving her without an explanation for what she’s experiencing as doctors investigate the cause.

Being misdiagnosed with MS is relatively common. Applegate’s revelation highlights a difficult reality for people living with unexplained symptoms resembling MS. Her experience raises questions about how MS is diagnosed, why misdiagnoses may occur,  and what people can do when their symptoms don’t match their diagnosis.

While MS can be difficult to diagnose, an accurate diagnosis can help people with the condition gain access to appropriate treatment. A misdiagnosis, on the other hand, may lead to unnecessary interventions and medication, and take an emotional toll over time.

“MS can be challenging to diagnose because there is no single test that confirms it in every case,” said Steve Allder, MD, consultant neurologist at Re:Cognition Health. 

“An incorrect diagnosis can have significant consequences. Someone incorrectly diagnosed with MS may experience considerable anxiety, undergo unnecessary investigations, or receive treatments with potential risks and side effects. Equally, if MS is missed, treatment may be delayed, potentially allowing further neurological damage,” Allder told Healthline.

How common is MS misdiagnosis?

A 2024 systematic review and meta-analysis underscores that MS misdiagnoses remain a concern. 

Research suggests that around 15% of people diagnosed with MS may actually have another condition.

However, estimates vary across studies, with some reporting rates ranging from 5–41%.

While an accurate diagnosis can guide patients toward appropriate treatment, a mistaken one may lead to unnecessary medication, emotional distress, and years of uncertainty.

New diagnostic guidelines offer hope for greater accuracy, but they also raise important questions about how doctors can adequately distinguish MS from other conditions. 

“Research has shown that MS can be both overdiagnosed and underdiagnosed,” Allder said.

“Diagnosis relies on a combination of symptoms, neurological examination, MRI findings, and sometimes, additional tests. It is also important to recognize that diagnoses can evolve as new evidence emerges. As neurologists, we must remain open to revisiting a diagnosis when symptoms or test results do not fit, ensuring patients receive appropriate treatment and support.”

What health conditions are mistaken for MS?

MS symptoms may vary widely and overlap with several other conditions. These may include: 

  • Migraine — may cause sensory disturbances, visual symptoms, and MRI abnormalities.
  • Radiologically isolated syndrome (RIS) — MS-like lesions appear on brain or spinal imaging, but the person has no symptoms typical of MS.
  • Peripheral neuropathy — nerve damage outside the brain and spinal cord can cause numbness and tingling as well as weakness.
  • Fibromyalgia — may cause widespread pain, fatigue, and cognitive difficulties that overlap with symptoms reported by people with MS.

Because these conditions share certain features with MS, healthcare professionals should consider a patient’s full clinical profile rather than relying on a single symptom or scan.

“MS can cause numbness, tingling, muscle weakness, visual disturbances, balance problems, and fatigue. However, these symptoms are not unique to the condition,” Allder said.

“For example, numbness and tingling may indicate peripheral neuropathy, sometimes associated with diabetes or vitamin B12 deficiency. Weakness or altered sensation may result from a trapped nerve or spinal cord compression. Migraines can cause visual disturbances and sensory symptoms, while inner-ear disorders may affect balance,” he continued.

Allder noted that fatigue is particularly nonspecific and may be associated with:

  • anemia
  • thyroid disorders
  • sleep problems
  • other medical conditions

He explained that, as a neurologist, he considers the pattern, duration, and progression of symptoms alongside examination findings. 

“No single symptom automatically indicates MS. A thorough assessment is essential to identify the underlying cause and ensure the patient receives appropriate treatment,” he said.

Why does MS misdiagnosis occur?

There is no single test that definitively confirms every case of MS. 

Diagnosis typically involves reviewing symptoms, conducting neurological examinations, and evaluating MRI scans and laboratory results.

MRI findings may be tricky to interpret. Some white-matter spots resemble MS lesions but may result from migraine, aging, or other conditions. Misapplying diagnostic criteria or overlooking alternative explanations can also lead to mistakes.

“MRI abnormalities alone do not confirm MS,” Allder said. “The diagnosis must be based on the overall clinical picture, with alternative explanations carefully considered.”

Research also suggests that MS misdiagnosis rates may differ by sex.

Research shows females are 2.1 times more likely to be misdiagnosed with MS than males, highlighting the potential for diagnostic bias and differences in how symptoms are evaluated.

What changes have been made in diagnostic criteria?

The updated MS diagnostic criteria published in 2025 offer a framework for improving diagnosis. 

The updated guidelines introduce several important changes.

For instance, the optic nerve (the nerve connecting the eye to the brain) is now included as a fifth anatomical location for assessing evidence of MS.

The revised criteria also include new MRI biomarkers, such as the central vein sign and paramagnetic rim lesions. These features can help doctors distinguish MS-related abnormalities from other conditions.

These changes may help doctors diagnose MS earlier and more accurately. 

If you experience persistent or recurring neurological symptoms, seek medical advice. Your doctor can assess your symptoms and, if applicable, refer you to a neurologist. 

Sudden weakness, vision loss, or difficulty speaking requires urgent medical assessment, Allder said.

“Depending on the findings, investigations may include MRI scans of the brain and spinal cord, blood tests, and, in some cases, a lumbar puncture,” Allder noted. 

“It is important that results are interpreted alongside symptoms and examination findings, as no single test should determine the diagnosis in isolation.”

Allder said that if you’re unsure about a diagnosis, you can ask your neurologist what evidence supports it and whether alternative health conditions have been considered. 

“A second opinion or follow-up assessment may be appropriate. The priority is an accurate diagnosis that leads to the right treatment and support,” Allder said.

#5010. ACOG Strongly Recommends Hormone Therapy for Menopause Symptoms

  • The American College of Obstetricians and Gynecologists (ACOG) released expanded menopause management guidelines strongly recommending menopause hormone therapy (MHT) for vasomotor symptoms (VMS) and related sleep disturbances in eligible individuals.
  • For individuals who can’t or don’t want to use MHT, ACOG strongly recommends nonhormonal options, including selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors (SSRIs), serotonin and norepinephrine reuptake inhibitors (SNRIs), and neurokinin receptor antagonists, to manage VMS.
  • The guidelines also conditionally recommend nonpharmacologic approaches like cognitive behavioral therapy (CBT), mindfulness-based interventions, regular exercise, clinical hypnosis, and acupuncture, typically used alongside medications to manage menopause symptoms.

The American College of Obstetricians and Gynecologists (ACOG) published updated recommendations for menopause management in Obstetrics & Gynecology on October 15. 

These expanded recommendations are the most comprehensive guidance to date. They include new clinical guidelines for menopause hormone therapy (MHT), nonhormonal medications, and nonpharmacological treatment of vasomotor symptoms (VMS), which include hot flashes and night sweats, and sleep and mood changes. 

According to the ACOG guidance, 50%–92% of females experiencing menopause report VMS. 

Around 16%–66% of females in the United States report menopause-related sleep issues, and more than one-third of perimenopausal and postmenopausal females report experiencing depressive symptoms. 

“In recent years, misconceptions about the risks of hormone therapy have been widespread, which has made some clinicians reluctant to prescribe it and some patients hesitant to use it,” Bruce Pier, MD, clinical professor and associate fellowship director at the University of North Carolina and collaborator on the ACOG clinical practice guidelines, told Healthline.   

“This guidance offers a comprehensive review of the evidence to support individualized counseling and shared decision making. Our hope is that it clears up those misconceptions and expands access to treatment.”

The ACOG is also developing companion guidance for genitourinary syndrome of menopause, expected in 2027. 

ACOG hormone therapy recommendations for VMS and sleep

The ACOG strongly recommends the use of MHT with estrogen and progestogens (or estrogen alone for people without a uterus) for both VMS and vasomotor-related sleep disturbance, in individuals who can use hormones. 

It also conditionally recommends conjugated estrogen plus bazedoxifene (Duavee) as an alternative for these issues. 

According to a meta-analysis from 2021 that was cited by the guidelines, estrogen therapy, with or without progestogens, significantly reduced VMS frequency and severity compared to a placebo or no treatment. A 2017 meta-analysis ranked transdermal estradiol plus a progestogen as the most effective option for reducing VMS frequency. 

A 2022 systematic review and meta-analysis, also cited by the guidelines, found that MHT was associated with improved self-reported sleep quality. Once again, transdermal therapies showed some of the largest effects. 

ACOG recommendations for starting MHT

The ACOG recommends initiating MHT before age 60 or within 10 years of the onset of natural menopause.. 

It also suggests that the decision to start MHT for VMS and vasomotor-related sleep disturbances should be based on a shared decision making discussion about an individual’s symptoms and the benefits and risks of therapy. Consideration should also be taken regarding an individual’s: 

  • age 
  • medical history
  • baseline risks of cardiovascular disease, breast cancer, and venous thromboembolism (VTE)
  • individualized treatment priorities and goals

Inequities in menopause care

Research shows that factors such as race, culture, socioeconomic status, gender identity, and sexual orientation can influence how individuals experience menopause and the care they receive.

According to the SWAN study, Black women reported more menopause symptoms than white women but were less likely to be treated for them. 

“This updated document shines a light on these inequities. Clinicians can proactively help right now by asking every patient in midlife about symptoms, rather than waiting for them to bring it up. Long term, we hope this document drives more research funding to understand these gaps and begin closing them,” said Pier. 

ACOG MHT recommendations for depressive symptoms

The ACOG stated in the guidelines that, “The available evidence is insufficient to recommend for or against the use of estrogen–progestogen or estrogen-only MHT for the treatment of menopause-related depressive symptoms.”

It recommends that perimenopausal and postmenopausal individuals who meet the diagnostic criteria for major depressive disorder (MDD) or those whose most noticeable symptom is depression should be treated according to depression management guidelines. 

Current treatment guidelines for depression include therapy, medication, or a combination of the two.

Psychological treatments for depression include psychotherapy, such as cognitive behavioral therapy (CBT), while antidepressants are among the most commonly prescribed medications.

ACOG recommendations for nonhormonal treatments

The ACOG strongly recommends the use of selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors (SSRIs) and serotonin and norepinephrine reuptake inhibitors (SNRIs) for the management of menopausal symptoms like VMS and depression in individuals who don’t want to use MHT or can’t use it. 

Currently, paroxetine is the only antidepressant that is approved by the Food and Drug Administration (FDA) for the management of VMS. 

The ACOG also strongly recommends the use of neurokinin receptor antagonists to manage VMS and vasomotor-related sleep disturbances in people who don’t desire hormone therapy or can’t use it. 

There are currently two neurokinin receptor antagonists approved by the FDA to manage menopause symptoms. Fezolinetant (Veozah) was approved in 2023 to help manage hot flashes. In 2025, the FDA approved elinzanetant (LYNKUET) for the management of moderate to severe VMS due to menopause. 

Another nonhormonal treatment for VMS that the ACOG conditionally recommends is gabapentin. 

ACOG recommendations for nonpharmacologic treatments

There are various nonpharmacologic treatments that the ACOG conditionally recommends for the management of VMS, vasomotor-related sleep disturbances, and mood changes. These are treatments that don’t involve the use of medications. 

The nonpharmacologic treatments conditionally recommended include: 

  • CBT
  • mindfulness-based interventions
  • regular exercise
  • clinical hypnosis
  • acupuncture 

It is generally recommended that these interventions are used alongside hormonal or nonhormonal medications. 

The ACOG noted that there is not enough evidence to support the efficacy or safety of phytoestrogens, such as soy isoflavones, or herbal supplements, which are not regulated by the FDA in the same way medications are for safety, purity, or efficacy. 

“Menopause is finally getting the attention it deserves, and this document shows what evidence-based care should look like in this space. It lays out a range of safe, effective options for managing menopause symptoms. My message to anyone struggling with these symptoms is simple: talk to your clinician. You don’t have to suffer through them,” said Pier.

#5011. A Handful of Pistachios May Help You Sleep Better, Review Says

  • A narrative review of current evidence suggests that pistachios may help support sleep.
  • Pistachios contain nutrients associated with sleep health, including tryptophan, vitamin B6, magnesium, phytonutrient melatonin, fiber, and polyphenols, which may influence sleep-related biological processes.
  • Studies of tryptophan supplements and dietary polyphenols suggest potential sleep benefits, but these findings do not establish that eating pistachios improves sleep.
  • More human clinical trials are needed before pistachios can be recommended as a sleep aid.

Could a handful of pistachios before you go to bed really help you sleep better? 

A review of studies published in Nutrition Research Reviews examines how the nutrients and plant compounds in pistachios might influence sleep health. 

Pistachios contain several nutrients linked to sleep-related processes, prompting an investigation into whether they might offer benefits beyond their nutritional value.

While the findings suggest that pistachios may support sleep, the evidence is inconclusive and warrants further study.

“Pistachios are compelling for sleep research because they bring together several nutrients and plant compounds connected to sleep physiology within one whole food,” said study author Marie-Pierre St-Onge, PhD, professor of nutritional medicine and director of the Center of Excellence for Sleep & Circadian Research in the Department of Medicine at Columbia University Institute of Nutrition, in a news release. 

“Rather than focusing on a single ‘sleep nutrient,’ this review provides a rationale for studying how the complete pistachio food matrix may affect sleep-related pathways,” she continued.

Key nutrients in pistachios may impact sleep

The narrative review examined existing research on pistachios and sleep-related biology.

The researchers explored whether the combination of nutrients in pistachios could promote quality sleep.

Rather than identifying pistachios as a sleep remedy, the researchers outlined biological pathways through which their nutrients and bioactive compounds might influence sleep.

“We were particularly interested in pistachios because they are particularly high in phytomelatonin, polyphenols, and carotenoids, which we noted were plant-derived components that had been shown to have sleep benefits in preclinical studies,” St-Onge told Healthline.

“We also noted their high content of tryptophan and vitamin B6, which are relevant to melatonin production in humans,” she continued.

Pistachios contain several nutrients associated with sleep, including melatonin, which helps regulate the sleep-wake cycle. 

  • Tryptophan: A 1-ounce serving of pistachios contains about 71 milligrams (mg) of this essential amino acid, which the body uses to produce serotonin and melatonin.
  • Vitamin B6: This vitamin helps the body process amino acids and supports pathways involved in neurotransmitter production.
  • Magnesium: Pistachios provide about 31 mg per ounce. Magnesium can help support several bodily functions, including nervous system regulation.
  • Phytomelatonin: Pistachios contain plant-based melatonin, but its absorption and effects in humans remain uncertain.
  • Fiber and polyphenols: These compounds may influence gut microbes and the gut-brain axis, potentially affecting sleep regulation.

Other studies examining the effects of some of these nutrients on sleep offer further context.

A 2021 systematic review and meta-analysis of tryptophan supplementation found that it may reduce wakefulness after sleep onset, particularly at doses of at least 1 gram (g) daily. However, an ounce of pistachios supplies only about 71 mg, which is much less than the doses examined by the researchers. 

Additionally, a 2023 review of dietary polyphenols led by St-Onge identified potential links between plant compounds and sleep outcomes, although results varied across studies. 

Neither review established that polyphenols or tryptophan from pistachios specifically affected sleep quality. 

“I was not surprised that pistachios may help support sleep, given their combination of nutrients involved in sleep regulation,” said Michelle Routhenstein, MS, a preventive cardiology dietitian at EntirelyNourished, who wasn’t involved in the study.

“Pistachios provide tryptophan, an amino acid the body uses to produce serotonin and melatonin; vitamin B6, which supports these pathways; and magnesium, which plays a role in nervous system function and sleep regulation,” she told Healthline.

Combined effects of nutrients may support sleep

The new review highlights the concept of the “food matrix,” or how nutrients and bioactive compounds interact within a whole food.

Instead of investigating tryptophan, magnesium, or melatonin individually, researchers were curious about the combined effects of consuming them together in pistachios.

Laboratory and simulated-digestion research offers clues about how fiber and plant compounds in pistachios and peanuts interact with gut microbes. However, these findings do not establish that eating pistachios changes sleep quality in real-world conditions.

Broader nutrition research also points to a relationship between diet and sleep.

A 2021 review in the Annual Review of Nutrition examined evidence linking dietary patterns and individual nutrients to sleep outcomes. The findings suggest a relationship between sleep and diet, but note that more research is needed to determine cause and effect.

In a nutshell, the current evidence builds a compelling case for pistachios as a sleep aid.

However, it’s too soon to recommend pistachios as a sleep remedy, as many factors may affect sleep quality and insomnia.

“Sleep difficulties can have different underlying causes,” Routhenstein noted. “If inadequate intake of these nutrients is a contributing factor, pistachios may help, but they may not address sleep issues related to other nutrient deficiencies or other causes.”

Pistachios are a healthy snack, not yet a sleep aid

Much of the supporting evidence cited in the review comes from studies of individual nutrients, other foods, laboratory experiments, or biological mechanisms rather than from trials involving whole pistachios. 

Researchers have also reported significant variations in the melatonin content of pistachios, making the amount in a typical serving difficult to pinpoint.

Additionally, the review received support from American Pistachio Growers, and the corresponding author, St-Onge, serves on its advisory board. Larger studies and clinical trials are needed, ideally without industry funding, to ensure well-rounded results.

Indeed, the authors call for randomized controlled trials examining whether eating pistachios affects sleep outcomes, including sleep quality, duration, and melatonin levels. 

“This was a review; we noted that the complete profile of pistachios would make them well-suited to support healthful sleep, but research is needed to evaluate this,” St-Onge told Healthline. 

“This is the next step, and something we are actively working on. We also need to better understand what dosage and frequency of consumption would be optimal.”

Regardless, a handful of pistachios is still considered a healthy choice as part of a balanced diet.

“A small handful, about 1 ounce of shelled pistachios, is an appropriate portion,” Routhenstein said. “Enjoy them during the day or as a light afternoon or evening snack when you’re hungry.”

Other ways nutrition may help promote sleep

If nutrients like tryptophan help promote sleep, it’s possible that other nuts could yield similar results.

“Other nuts, such as almonds and walnuts, may offer similar benefits because they also contain nutrients involved in sleep regulation,” Routhenstein said.

“Early research is encouraging, but it’s too soon to say how much they might help.”

Other foods, like turkey, also contain tryptophan.

“For someone allergic to nuts, foods such as tofu, beans, oats, eggs, dairy, and fish can provide tryptophan, which the body uses to make serotonin and melatonin,” Routhenstein said. “The goal is to meet their nutrient needs rather than rely on one food to improve sleep.”

Sleep health depends on multiple factors. As such, Routhenstein recommends a personalized approach to getting better sleep, from what you eat and when you eat to addressing any nutritional gaps.

“I would also encourage regular, balanced meals throughout the day to support blood sugar management, especially for those with diabetes, while avoiding large meals close to bedtime,” Routhenstein said. “Caffeine later in the day and alcohol in the evening are also worth considering as possible sleep disruptors.”

#5012. Ultra-Processed Food Intake May Increase Dementia, Stroke Risk

  • Healthy adults who consume high amounts of ultra-processed foods (UPFs) may be more likely to show signs of vascular brain injury, which has been linked to stroke risk and cognitive decline.
  • UPFs account for more than 50% of daily energy intake in high-income countries such as the United States.
  • People with the highest UPF intake had higher body fat levels and greater odds of vascular brain injury than those with the lowest intake.
  • You can reduce UPF intake by making simple swaps toward whole foods, such as fruits, vegetables, and grains prepared at home.

Healthy adults who eat high levels of ultra-processed foods (UPFs) may be more likely to show signs of damage to the brain that could increase the likelihood of stroke and future memory problems, according to a study published October 15 in JAMA Network Open. 

UPFs are often defined using the NOVA classification: These foods typically have 5 or more ingredients and include substances not typically found in culinary preparations. This category of foods includes packaged snack foods, sugary cereals, soft drinks, and frozen ready-to-eat meals. 

UPFs account for more than 50% of daily energy intake in high-income countries, including the United States, Canada, and the United Kingdom. 

Previous research has linked these foods to adverse cardiometabolic outcomes. This new study adds to growing evidence linking high UPF intake to adverse health outcomes.

High UPF intake may increase the risk of vascular brain injury

This was a cross-sectional analysis of 8,783 adults between the ages of 35 and 69 without cardiovascular disease. The participants were all enrolled in the Canadian Alliance for Healthy Hearts and Minds (CAHHM) and Prospective Urban and Rural Epidemiology-MIND (PURE-MIND) cohorts. 

The researchers used MRI scans to evaluate the participants’ brains. They also set out to analyze the cross-sectional relationship between UPF consumption, body fat, vascular brain injury (VBI), and cognitive performance. 

They measured the participants’ habitual UPF intake using a food frequency questionnaire. Intake was expressed as servings based on a 2,000-calorie-per-day diet. 

UPFs accounted for an average of 25% of participants’ daily diets. For the individuals with the highest intake, this increased to 45% of everything on their plates, compared to 16% for those who ate the least. 

The researchers also noted that UPFs were not just an added part of individual diets. These foods appeared to replace whole foods.

Among people with the highest UPF intake, fruits, vegetables, grains, and other minimally processed foods accounted for 41% of their daily food intake. Among those with the lowest UPF intake, minimally processed foods made up 68% of their daily intake. 

When the researchers compared the highest intake to the lowest, they found that those with a higher intake of UPFs had 1.7% more body fat. They also found that people with a higher UPF intake had higher odds of VBI. 

“These findings are concerning, but it is important to remember that an association does not prove cause and effect,” said Michelle Routhenstein, Preventive Cardiology Dietitian at Entirely Nourished, who wasn’t involved in the study.

“From my perspective as a preventive cardiology dietitian, these findings reinforce the importance of nourishing our blood vessels to support both cardiovascular and brain health.” 

Although researchers found no significant differences in cognitive performance, the signs of vascular brain injury observed in the study have been associated with an increased risk of stroke and cognitive decline.

These findings suggest a possible link between UPF intake and signs of brain changes. However, participants were only assessed once and weren’t followed over time. This means the researchers were unable to determine whether UPFs directly caused the changes. 

“While we can’t conclude from this study that limiting UPFs would improve brain health, we have decades of literature demonstrating that higher intakes of green leafy vegetables, brightly colored fruits, whole grains, and lean protein sources are good for brain health,” said Suzanne Judd, PhD, Director of the UC College of Medicine’s Center for Public Health, who wasn’t involved in the study.  

“So focusing more on what can be brought into the diet rather than what should be eliminated might be a good way to start,” she added. 

Why might UPFs affect the brain?

The preservatives [in UPFs] may have a direct impact on the vascular system, while the loss of antioxidants results in a less robust response to inflammation in the body. 

“Additionally, sugar or salt is often added to make the food a bit tastier so people consume it in higher quantities. Over-consumption of food can lead to weight gain and diabetes, which can negatively impact brain health,” Judd said. 

Tips for reducing your UPF intake

Judd told Healthline that it is virtually impossible to completely avoid UPFs. Even some “health” foods, like protein powder and some yogurts, are considered ultra-processed. 

It is more important to look at diet overall and emphasize consuming foods as close to their natural state as possible, such as fruits, vegetables, grains, and meats purchased raw or frozen and then cooked at home.

Routhenstein recommended starting simply with one manageable swap rather than trying to overhaul your entire diet. 

She said that you should identify an ultra-processed food you eat regularly and consider a more nutrient-rich alternative. It is important to account for convenience, food preferences, budget, and lifestyle so that the change feels realistic and is sustainable over the long term.

“I want people to feel empowered, not fearful, when making food choices,” said Routhenstein. “Healthy eating does not mean having to cook everything from scratch. What matters most is building consistent, nourishing habits that support both heart and brain health.”

#5013. Walking, Yoga, or Zumba? Which Exercises Help Relieve Menopause Mood Symptoms

  • Regular physical activity may help reduce depressive symptoms and improve quality of life during menopause, according to a review of 12 clinical trials involving over 1,000 people ages 40 to 65.
  • Aerobic exercises like walking, Zumba, and dance showed the strongest effects on mood and overall well-being, while mind-body activities like yoga and tai chi may help improve emotional regulation.
  • Physical activity also supports cardiovascular health, helps maintain muscle mass and bone density, and aids in managing weight during the menopause transition.
  • If you’re experiencing persistent depression or symptoms that interfere with daily life, talk with a healthcare professional about evidence-based treatment options.

Physical activity may help support mood and improve quality of life during the menopause transition, according to a study published on October 13 in Menopause, the journal of the Menopause Society.  

This review of 12 clinical trials involving over 1,000 people ages 40 to 65 found that both aerobic and mind-body exercises may be associated with reduced depressive symptoms and improved well-being.

What type of exercise helps improve menopause symptoms?

It is common for individuals to experience psychological symptoms during menopause, including depression, anxiety, low mood, stress, and difficulty with thinking and memory. These symptoms can have a significant effect on quality of life, with an estimated 1 in 10 women leaving work due to menopause symptoms. 

This study found that regular physical activity may help improve certain psychological symptoms and overall quality of life during menopause. 

The types of physical activity in the review were broken into two categories: aerobic-based exercise (ABIs) and mind-body activities (MBAs). 

ABIs included: 

  • walking
  • Zumba
  • aerobic exercise
  • dance
  • Taekwondo 

MBAs included: 

  • yoga
  • tai-chi
  • Pilates
  • stretching

The researchers found that ABIs showed the strongest effects on reducing depressive symptoms and improving overall quality of life. This may be particularly relevant during perimenopause when women may be more vulnerable to mood instability due to estrogen fluctuations. 

How aerobic exercise can affect the body

Sherry Ross, MD, board certified OB/GYN and Women’s Health Expert at Providence Saint John’s Health Center in Santa Monica, CA, who wasn’t involved in the study, pointed out that there are various ways that ABIs can have positive effects. 

Ross explained that aerobic exercise may help improve menopausal symptoms through several mechanisms, including the release of endorphins, reduced inflammation, improved cholesterol levels, and better regulation of body temperature and sleep cycles.

MBAs were shown to have a greater effect on improving psychological quality of life. These exercises involve mindfulness, body awareness, and breathwork. They may help improve emotional regulation. 

ABIs tended to require fewer sessions. This may mean these types of exercises are easier to fit in, especially if you are facing time or energy constraints. For example, in one trial, participants were instructed to walk for 30 minutes, three times per week. In another trial, participants attended three hour-long Zumba classes per week. 

In contrast, one trial using MBAs instructed participants to attend two 75-minute yoga classes each week, and another had participants attend daily one-hour yoga classes. 

Stephanie Faubion, MD, Director for the Mayo Clinic Center for Women’s Health and Medical Director for The Menopause Society, who wasn’t involved in the study, told Healthline that these findings are encouraging. 

However, she also cautioned that this was a relatively small body of evidence. “While physical activity is an important component of a healthy lifestyle during midlife, I would be cautious about interpreting these findings to mean that exercise is a treatment for depression related specifically to menopause,” she said. 

How does exercise help improve mood during menopause? 

Research has shown that physical activity can positively affect mental health. Studies have also found that regular exercise can help improve symptoms of depression and anxiety. 

Faubion told Healthline that, broadly speaking, regular physical activity may support mood through multiple pathways, including better sleep, improved physical function, and overall well-being. 

However, she also noted that this study cannot tell us precisely why the improvements occurred or whether aerobic exercise is superior to other forms of activity.

What other ways can exercise help with menopause? 

Faubion said that recommending physical activity during and after menopause extends well beyond mood. 

“Physical activity is important for cardiovascular and metabolic health, maintaining muscle mass and physical function, and supporting bone health,” she said.  

“These become particularly important at midlife as women experience age- and menopause-related changes in body composition, bone, and cardiometabolic health,” Faubion added. 

Ross agreed that physical activity is important for more than just mood and mental health improvement. She also noted that there are other types of exercise beyond ABIs and MBAs that can benefit people during menopause:

  • Weight-bearing exercises, where your feet and legs support your body weight, and muscle-strengthening exercises build bones. 
  • Low impact exercises, such as brisk walking, using elliptical machines, and stair stepping, can help strengthen muscles and support bone health.
  • High impact exercises include running/jogging, jumping rope, and high intensity aerobics.  Strong muscles increase blood flow and send key nutrients to help maintain healthy bone density.

“These types of activities help with weight loss or allow women to maintain a healthy weight. If you are not ready for aerobic exercises, stretching and simple exercises help with balance and stability,” Ross said. 

Faubion told Healthline that physical activity is an important part of healthy aging and should be encouraged during the menopause transition, but it should not be suggested that women can simply exercise their way out of significant depression or other bothersome menopause symptoms. 

“Women experiencing persistent depression, anxiety, or symptoms that interfere with their quality of life should seek evaluation and discuss the full range of evidence-based treatment options with their healthcare clinician,” Faubion said.

#5014. Women With Autoimmune Diseases More Likely to Be Misdiagnosed Than Men

  • A new study found that women with autoimmune diseases often take longer to get a correct diagnosis.
  • The researchers added that women are more likely to be misdiagnosed with mental health conditions than men.
  • Factors such as disease presentation, research gaps, fragmented care, and unconscious medical bias may contribute to these disparities.
  • The researchers suggest that doctors should carefully consider autoimmune diseases, especially in women.
  • It’s also important for people with autoimmune diseases to advocate for themselves.

A new international study found that women with autoimmune diseases often experience longer delays in getting a correct diagnosis compared to men.

The research, published in eClinicalMedicine on October 12, also shows that females report higher rates of being misdiagnosed, especially with mental health or psychosomatic conditions.

These findings highlight challenges in diagnosing autoimmune diseases and suggest that sex differences affect patients’ experiences in healthcare.

This is significant given that autoimmune diseases, conditions in which the body’s immune system attacks its own healthy cells, are estimated to affect around 4.6% of the U.S. population. Additionally, despite the observed disparities in diagnoses, women are almost two times as likely as men to be diagnosed with an autoimmune disease.

Diagnostic experiences among people with autoimmune diseases

Researchers conducted an online survey between December 7, 2024, and March 7, 2025, inviting adults ages 18 and older who had a confirmed autoimmune or rheumatic disease.

The survey was carefully designed and tested with input from patients, charities, and medical experts to ensure it captured important information about patients’ diagnostic journeys.

Participants were asked about the time it took from when they first noticed symptoms to when they received their autoimmune diagnosis.

They also reported how many healthcare visits they made before diagnosis and whether they felt their symptoms were ever misdiagnosed as mental health issues, psychosomatic problems (physical symptoms caused or worsened by psychological factors), or another physical disease.

The survey was widely shared on social media, patient support groups, and community organizations to reach a broad audience.

Special efforts were made to include underrepresented groups such as males, younger individuals, and people from minority ethnic backgrounds.

The survey was available in five languages, and participants could complete it with help from researchers if needed.

To analyze the data, the team used statistical methods, adjusting for factors like age, type of autoimmune disease, continent of residence, and financial situation.

These methods helped compare the experiences of males and females while accounting for differences in disease types and other factors

Women took longer to be diagnosed, and were often misdiagnosed

The study included 6,607 participants, with 91.2% identifying as female and most living in the United Kingdom. On average, women were younger than men in the study.

Autoimmune thyroid disease was the most common condition reported. Non-autoimmune conditions like anxiety were more commonly reported by women, while men reported higher rates of hypertension.

One of the main findings was that women reported longer delays before getting a diagnosis. Nearly 32% of females experienced delays of four years or more from symptom onset to diagnosis, compared to about 26% of males.

On the other hand, men were more likely to be diagnosed within a year of symptom onset (nearly 50% of males versus 40% of females).

Women also made more visits to healthcare providers before diagnosis, with 26.2% having 11 or more visits compared to 18.4% of men.

When it came to misdiagnoses, women were significantly more likely to report that their symptoms had been mistaken for mental health issues (43.3% of females vs. 32.7% of males) or psychosomatic causes (33.9% of females vs. 21.5% of males).

However, there was no significant difference between men and women in being misdiagnosed with another physical disease.

The type of autoimmune disease also influenced misdiagnosis rates. Diseases with clear physical symptoms or definitive lab tests, like type 1 diabetes or rheumatoid arthritis, had lower rates of mental health and psychosomatic misdiagnoses.

In contrast, conditions such as autoimmune encephalitis or undifferentiated connective tissue disease had higher rates of these misdiagnoses (over 50%).

After adjusting for disease type and other factors, women still had higher odds of reporting mental health or psychosomatic misdiagnoses compared to men.

Other factors affected misdiagnosis rates, too. People living in continents outside the United Kingdom, those with lower household income, and certain disease groups were more likely to report psychiatric or psychosomatic misdiagnoses. Age, however, was not linked to the risk of being misdiagnosed.

More physician awareness, patient advocacy needed

Muhammad Qasim, MD, a pulmonologist and medical writer with MySleepApneaTeam, told Healthline that autoimmune diseases are often difficult to diagnose. Qasim wasn’t involved in the study.

“Early symptoms can be vague, fluctuate over time, and affect several parts of the body,” he said. “Fatigue, pain, sleep problems, brain fog, and mood changes do not immediately point to one diagnosis.”

Qasim echoed the study’s findings, explaining that similar symptoms may be interpreted differently in women versus men, with complaints being attributed to anxiety, stress, or psychosomatic causes.

However, there are probably multiple factors contributing to this phenomenon, he said.
