“Differences in disease presentation, gaps in research, fragmented care, and unconscious bias can all contribute,” said Qasim. “Once a psychiatric explanation enters the medical record, there is also a risk that later clinicians view new symptoms through that same lens.”

Regarding unconscious bias, the study’s authors noted that women have historically been treated by doctors as “less credible” and more “emotional” in reporting their symptoms, which can influence how doctors interpret them.

These disparities in diagnosis are important, however, because longer waits and erroneous diagnoses can delay proper treatment and increase women’s frustration, leading them to stop asking for help.

The researchers suggest that healthcare providers need to be aware of these concerns and consider autoimmune diseases carefully when patients, especially females, present with more complicated symptoms.

Adam Carewe, MD, a physician in Boulder, CO, and a clinical informaticist with General Medicine who wasn’t involved in the study, offered guidance to those experiencing symptoms on how to obtain a proper diagnosis.

“Patients can keep an accurate symptom timeline, bring in family history, ask what else could be explaining these symptoms, ask for a plan for next steps if initial testing/treatment, do not change anything, and lastly get a second opinion,” he advised.

Carewe emphasized the value of not giving up on finding answers. “The most important thing for anyone with persistent symptoms of any kind is to continue to work with your primary care clinician and continue to advocate for yourself,” he said.

#5015. Letter from the Editor-in-Chief: What Breast Cancer Patients Need When Treatment Ends

  • Breast cancer awareness has never been higher, but public knowledge and support after treatment haven’t kept pace, said Marisa Weiss, MD, founder and Chief Medical Officer at Breastcancer.org.
  • The widest gap is the “quiet phase” after initial treatment ends, when patients are left with questions about side effects, work, and family. Some turn to GLP-1s, supplements, and cannabis without telling their doctors, often fearing judgment, even though some can interact with cancer medications.
  • To mark this Breast Cancer Awareness Month, Breastcancer.org has released a Breast Health Playbook providing 9 ways to lower your risk of breast cancer.
  • Healthline’s Editor-in-Chief Robin Hough sits down with Dr. Weiss, a breast cancer survivor herself, to talk advocacy, awareness, and care.

Breast cancer awareness has never been higher. Every October, the world turns pink and the key messages—that early diagnosis saves lives and that knowing the risk factors reduces the risk—are hard to miss. 

Breast cancer remains the leading cause of cancer death amongst women in most countries, with an estimated 694,000 deaths annually and around 8.2 million women globally living with a breast cancer diagnosis, according to 2024 data from the World Health Organization (WHO)’s Global Health Observatory. 

But when October ends, and the wall-to-wall campaigns move on, what has awareness actually changed for those impacted by breast cancer?

For Marisa Weiss, MD, the answer is that there is always more to be done, and she’s uniquely placed to hold that view. 

Dr. Weiss has treated breast cancer patients for more than 30 years. In 2000, she founded Breastcancer.org, a not-for-profit that provides expert-reviewed medical and lifestyle information on breast health and breast cancer to millions of people globally.

Sixteen years ago, she was diagnosed with stage 1 breast cancer, becoming, in her own words, a “dual citizen” in that moment, with lived experience on both sides of the doctor’s desk. 

I had the pleasure of sitting down with Dr. Weiss as part of Healthline’s Breast Cancer Awareness Month coverage to talk about how awareness has changed care for the better for millions of people around the world, but why critical gaps remain, and how access to trusted information and community can help close those gaps. 

“It’s good that awareness is high, but knowledge remains low,” she said. The misconception she sees most is the belief that breast cancer is something that happens to other families.

“Most people think they’re off the hook because it doesn’t run in their family,” Dr. Weiss explained. In fact, 85%-90% of people who get breast cancer have no known family history and no known gene in the family, “so we’re all at risk,” she warns. 

For most, a diagnosis arrives as a shock. “They’re completely surprised, flattened by it. Their life is upside down, and they scramble to find what they need to know,” Dr. Weiss noted.

Breast cancer care: Be your own advocate

That aftershock caused by a diagnosis is at the core of why Breastcancer.org exists. Because the person whose life is at stake is increasingly expected to direct their own care and advocate for their needs, access to accurate and empowering information is critical in that process. “Decisions aren’t being made around the patient and for the patient in a bubble,” Dr. Weiss said. “Decision-making is supposed to be shared.”

With appointments hard to get and clinicians stretched, she said patients have to do more of the work.

In practice, Dr. Weiss says that means preparing by writing down your family history on both sides and making sure your care team knows who you are. “What’s important to you? Who’s in your life? Do you want more children? Are you planning a vacation? All those decisions can have an impact on the care you receive.”

Gaps remain even with the best care

Where Dr. Weiss sees the widest gap is after treatment ends. Speaking from personal experience, she said: “I had the best care possible, and there were still gaps. You can find yourself alone with lots of questions and fears.”

Understandably, a great deal of focus and attention is centered on diagnosis and treatment, she said. Then it stops. “When your life is supposed to go back to normal, it never does. You have to find a new normal. That quiet phase can be really lonely and difficult.”

For Dr. Weiss, the questions that fill that silence are often as practical as they are emotional: “How do I know my treatment is working? How do I tell leftover side effects from new ones? How do I go back to work, and who do I tell? How do I talk to my kids? What about that new treatment I heard about on TV last night?”

That post-treatment period can also be the first time anyone has really had a chance to stop, take stock, and start asking “what next?” 

“You might think, what happened to me? Who do I want to be moving forward? How do I have a long life full of wonderful things, and remain as independent as possible?” 

What breast cancer patients don’t say in the clinic

Some of what patients do post-care never reaches their doctor at all, with Dr. Weiss pointing to a growing amount of personal care happening outside the hospital: GLP-1s, peptides, hormonal therapies, supplements, medical cannabis, to name a few. Breastcancer.org’s annual Pulse survey shows that people reported being interested in all of those things and more, and that they tend to learn about them online rather than risk the stigma of bringing them up with their doctors.

“They don’t really trust that their doctor knows much about these things,” she said, “and they don’t want a conversation that makes them feel judged. Some patients worry about what ends up in their medical record and then everyone’s going to know.”

Dr. Weiss says the stakes are real, because some of those choices can interact with cancer medications with potential for adverse or unexpected side effects. “That is why people turn to Breastcancer.org with questions that are personal, private, and important, including “what do I need to share with my doctor, and who’s on a need-to-know basis?”

The power of community, and its limitations

Peer communities fill part of that gap. “Breastcancer.org’s members share medical information, tips, and practical advice with people who have just had the call, or are about to start treatment and don’t know what to expect,” Dr. Weiss shared, and said that while she values that kind of support system hugely, it does come with a caveat:

The same community can also be intrusive. Dr. Weiss describes standing at a party, drink in hand, when someone asks what kind of cancer you had, what you did, and whether it came back. Her advice to patients, which she took herself: “Put up your hand and say, ‘thank you so much for caring, but I cannot hear another story right now.’”

Information you can trust in the age of AI 

On the topic of AI and its impact on treatment and care, Dr. Weiss is cautiously optimistic. On the treatment front, she points to tools that help radiologists read mammograms more thoroughly and assess future risk as game changers in the fight against breast cancer. 

Breastcancer.org also uses AI for tasks such as finding citations and interpreting pathology reports. But like many, she is wary of misinformation. “When you’re fearful, you look for reassurance, and you don’t want to be reassured by misinformation. When it comes to health information, every word matters.”

Her final advice is for the appointment itself, which she says she has even struggled with. “When they start speaking quickly in complex medical language, you can get lost really fast. The risk is you kind of fake it. That must not happen.” 

“Do your research in advance. Write things down. Bring someone you trust as a second set of ears. Ask direct questions. Don’t worry if it sounds funny or silly. It’s always a smart question when you are dealing with your health.” Just be prepared for the questions to multiply from there. “Once you get a certain set of questions answered, that raises more questions. So just write it all down.”

If breast cancer has touched your life, know that you are not alone. Healthline’s Bezzy Breast Cancer Community is a space for peer support, feel-good stories, and advice in a welcoming, stigma-free environment when your lived experience is seen, heard and valued.

Robin Hough is the Editor-in-Chief of Healthline, and in addition to this regular Letter from the Editor column, he can be found every other Saturday in our Healthline Daily newsletter. He’ll soon be launching a dedicated Letter from the Editor newsletter coming to an inbox near you later in the fall.

#5016. Ultra-Processed Food Intake May Not Be Linked to Colorectal Cancer

  • Researchers report that consumption of ultra-processed foods doesn’t necessarily increase a person’s risk of developing colorectal cancer.
  • They note that there is a wide range of foods considered ultra-processed, and each may carry different risks.
  • Experts say people should adopt a healthy diet rich in fruits, vegetables, whole grains, and calcium-rich foods to lower their risk of colorectal cancer.

Ultra-processed foods as a group do not increase the risk of colorectal cancer, according to a new study.

Researchers from the National Cancer Institute (NCI) report that they found no association between overall intake of ultra-processed foods and colorectal cancer incidence among the more than 400,000 adults they studied.

They said their findings are important because ultra-processed foods now account for more than half of adults’ energy intake in the United States. They published their research on October 15 in PLOS Medicine.

The study comes amid recent research that has connected ultra-processed foods to an increased risk of heart disease, type 2 diabetes, and obesity.

However, the link between colorectal cancer and ultra-processed foods has been less clear.

A 2025 study reported that ultra-processed foods may increase the risk of colorectal cancer in females under 50.

A 2022 study found that males who consumed high rates of ultra-processed foods had a higher risk of developing colorectal cancer.

NCI scientists noted in their new study that ultra-processed products represent a wide range of foods.

They pointed out that foods such as whole grains and dairy products were associated with a lower risk of colorectal cancer among study participants, while foods such as processed meats were associated with a higher risk.

“We did not observe an association between total ultra-processed food (UPF) intake and colorectal cancer incidence,” said Erikka Loftfield, PhD, a former investigator with the NCI, in a news release. “However, when we looked at food groups, like whole grains, dairy, and meat, by UPF or non-UPF source, we found that associations were in line with current dietary guidance for colorectal cancer prevention.”

Experts not involved in the study told Healthline that the NCI scientists appear to be on the right track.

“While the study did not find that overall ultra-processed food intake was associated with colorectal cancer risk, the findings suggest that simply classifying foods by their level of processing may not tell the whole story,” said Marissa Shams-White, PhD, a senior principal scientist in population science at the American Cancer Society.

“One of the challenges with studying ultra-processed foods is that the category includes a very wide range of foods and beverages, and we don’t yet know whether they all relate to colorectal cancer risk in the same way,” she added.

Ketan Thanki, MD, a colorectal surgeon specializing in benign and malignant diseases of the colon, rectum, and anus at the MemorialCare Todd Cancer Institute at Long Beach Medical Center in California, agrees.

“Just because it is ultra-processed doesn’t mean it is inherently bad or good for you,” he said. “The source of the food and the additives like preservatives, flavorings, sugars, and pigments that are added to increase palatability probably play a much larger role in increasing cancer risk.”

“A whole grain cereal and a hot dog are both UPF, but they push risk in opposite directions,” Thanki noted.

Ultra-processed foods and colorectal cancer risk

In their study, the National Cancer Institute researchers analyzed data from 461,682 adults aged 50 to 71 years who enrolled in the NIH-AARP Diet and Health Study between 1995 and 1996.

The participants recorded their eating habits using a food frequency questionnaire. Researchers then used that data to estimate each person’s intake of ultra-processed foods. The median consumption of ultra-processed foods among participants was 43% of daily median intake.

The participants were then followed until 2018, for a median of nearly 19 years. The scientists reported that 10,075 cases of colorectal adenocarcinoma were diagnosed during that period.

The researchers reported no association between overall intake of ultra-processed foods and the incidence of colorectal cancer.

However, they said their findings varied by food type. Higher intake of whole grains, fiber, dairy products, and calcium derived from UPF sources was associated with lower colorectal cancer incidence, while meat from UPF sources was associated with higher incidence.

Researchers also found that replacing 10% of gram weight from UPF intake with minimally processed foods was associated with a 2% lower incidence of CRC.

The researchers acknowledged limitations to their study.

They noted that the participants were primarily older, white Americans. In addition, UPF intake was only measured once at the beginning of the study period, so the findings cannot account for changes in eating habits or the increasing availability of ultra-processed foods.

Nonetheless, Ashkan Farhadi, MD, a gastroenterologist at MemorialCare Orange Coast Medical Center in California, who wasn’t involved in the study, said he found the results “very interesting.”

“The definition of ultra-processed foods is so broad,” he told Healthline. “It’s important to designate what type of ultra-processed foods you’re talking about.”

“You shouldn’t just go by whether a food is designated as ultra-processed,” Farhadi added. “You should go by what’s on the [ingredient] label.”

Shams-White shared a similar approach. “When it comes to diet, it is important to follow an overall healthy dietary pattern,” she told Healthline. “Emphasize a variety of fruits and vegetables and whole grains and limit red and processed meats, sugar-sweetened beverages, ultra-processed foods, and refined grain products.”

“Eating a diet rich in fiber and whole grains, dairy and calcium, and limiting processed and red meat and alcohol is what really reduces your risk of developing cancer,” added Thanki.

Risk factors for colorectal cancer

The American Cancer Society estimates there will be about 108,000 new cases of colon cancer and nearly 50,000 new cases of rectal cancer in the United States in 2026.

The incidence rate is slightly higher in men than in females.

The organization reports that the rate of colorectal cancer cases in the United States has declined since the mid-1980s, but they note that most of that decrease has occurred in older adults.

The incidence rate among people under 50 increased by nearly 3% from 2013 to 2022. Colorectal cancer is now the leading cause of death from cancer for U.S. adults under the age of 50.

Colorectal cancer occurs when cells in the large intestine grow out of control. About 70% of cases begin in the colon.

There are a number of symptoms of colorectal cancer. Some of the common ones include:

  • constipation
  • diarrhea
  • blood in the stool
  • bleeding in the rectum
  • abdominal pain

Diet is considered one of the risk factors for colorectal cancer. However, there are other considerations.

Farhadi said genetics, obesity, smoking, alcohol consumption, and a sedentary lifestyle can also increase a person’s risk of developing colorectal cancer.

Shams-White noted that the American Cancer Society Guideline for Diet and Physical Activity for Cancer Prevention recommended achieving and maintaining a healthy weight, being physically active, following a healthy eating pattern, and avoiding alcohol and tobacco.

Thanki points out that there are risk factors for colorectal cancer that can be modified and others that can’t.

“Among non-modifiable factors, the biggest are age, a personal or family history of colorectal cancer or advanced polyps, inherited syndromes such as Lynch syndrome, and longstanding inflammatory bowel disease (ulcerative colitis or Crohn’s colitis),” he told Healthline.

“On the modifiable side, the evidence is strongest for obesity, physical inactivity, smoking, alcohol, and diets high in red and processed meat and low in fiber, whole grains, and calcium,” he added.

Colorectal cancer treatment outcomes improve if the disease is caught at an early stage. The 5-year relative survival for localized colorectal cancer is 91.3%. The 5-year relative survival rate for colorectal cancer caught at various stages, including cancer that has spread, is 65.4%.

Shams-White points out that the American Cancer Society Colorectal Cancer Guidelines recommend that people at average risk of colorectal cancer start getting screened at age 45.

“Screening can help prevent cancer by finding abnormal cells and polyps so they can be removed before they can become colorectal cancer, as well as detect cancer at an earlier stage,” she said.

Thanki had similar advice. “Get screened starting at 45, or earlier if you have a family history or other risk factors, because colonoscopies can prevent cancer by removing polyps,” he said. “And never ignore symptoms like rectal bleeding or a change in bowel habits at any age.”

#5017. Pope Leo XIV Diagnosed With Lung Cancer After Surgery to Remove Part of Lung

  • Pope Leo XIV has been diagnosed with lung cancer, according to the Vatican.
  • The diagnosis followed a successful surgery on October 13, in which the doctors removed the lower lobe of the Pope’s right lung.
  • The Vatican says the Pope had a calm first night and is expected to spend a week in the hospital while exams are conducted on the removed tissue.
  • At this time, there have been no updates as to whether Pope Leo will require any further treatment.

Pope Leo XIV has been diagnosed with lung cancer following a successful surgery, the Vatican said. 

The Pope, 71, underwent surgery at Agostino Gemelli University Policlinic in Rome on October 13 to remove a nodule from his right lung. This was determined to be pulmonary adenocarcinoma, the most common type of lung cancer. 

The surgeons removed the lower lobe of the Pope’s lung to “ensure a radical procedure; the lower lobe of the right lung was removed using a video-assisted technique,” said Dr. Luigi Carbone, the Director of Vatican Healthcare Services, and Professor Stephano Margaritora, the lead surgeon, in a press release. 

“An intraoperative histological examination confirmed the diagnosis of lung adenocarcinoma, as suspected from the preoperative examinations,” they said. 

Pope Leo was in the operating room for 3 hours, while the operation itself lasted around 90 minutes. 

The doctors noted that it was a successful operation with no complications. 

According to Matteo Bruni, the Director of the Holy See Press Office, the Pope asked for dinner after the surgery and spent the evening with his secretaries and other Vatican staff.

In a press release on Wednesday, the Vatican said that the Pope’s postoperative recovery is progressing well.

Pope Leo will take a week off to recover

According to Vatican News, Pope Leo had a calm first night following his surgery. 

In the coming days, exams will be conducted on the removed tissue. There has currently been no change in the initially announced week of hospitalization. 

On October 14, the Pope underwent blood tests and chest X-rays that were scheduled following his surgery. The results of these tests confirm that his postoperative recovery is progressing as expected, and the values are within a normal range for the type of operation performed.

Pope Leo has also undergone some respiratory physiotherapy and has begun walking around his room, the Vatican said.

It has not yet been announced whether he will require any further treatment.

What is adenocarcinoma lung cancer? 

Adenocarcinoma is the most common subtype of lung cancer globally. It accounts for around 40% of all lung cancer diagnoses. 

Adenocarcinoma is a type of non-small cell lung cancer (NSCLC), which accounts for about 80–85% of all lung cancers. 

Lung adenocarcinoma begins in the epithelial cells that produce mucus. It is the most common type of NSCLC. 

Adenocarcinoma can be classified further by the genetic changes driving the tumor. For example, EGFR-positive lung cancer is found more often in people who have never smoked and can often be treated with targeted therapies, which is why biomarker testing of tumor tissue helps guide treatment decisions.

Treatment for NSCLC is generally determined by the stage. However, it typically involves one or a combination of:  

  • surgery 
  • radiation therapy 
  • chemotherapy
  • targeted therapy 
  • immunotherapy

Surgery is generally the first step in treatment for this type of lung cancer. The right lung has three lobes, or sections, and the left has two.

A doctor will often remove the entire lobe that has the tumor in order to remove the tumor and improve the outlook.

The body eventually adapts in order to compensate for the loss of function.

According to the American Cancer Society (ACS), for NSCLC that hasn’t spread outside of the lung, the 5-year relative survival rate is 67%. 

#5018. RJ Mitte's Death Highlights Mental Health Challenges for People With Cerebral Palsy

  • “Breaking Bad” actor RJ Mitte has reportedly died by suicide at age 34, according to his representative. Authorities have not yet confirmed the official cause of death.
  • Mitte lived with cerebral palsy and was a prominent advocate for disability representation and inclusion.
  • Research shows that adults with cerebral palsy have higher rates of depression and anxiety, although there is no evidence linking Mitte’s death to his disability.
  • Improved access to mental healthcare, inclusive communities, and employment opportunities can help support the well-being of people with cerebral palsy and other disabilities.

“Breaking Bad” actor and disability advocate RJ Mitte has died at age 34.

Mitte reportedly died by suicide, his representative told People on October 12. However, local authorities have said the official cause and manner of his death remain under investigation.

Mitte, who was best-known for playing Walter White Jr. on the Emmy Award-winning television series “Breaking Bad,” lived with cerebral palsy and advocated for disability inclusion.

There is currently no evidence to suggest that cerebral palsy caused the actor’s death. Still, Mitte’s untimely passing highlights the mental health challenges that people with cerebral palsy may face.

Research has demonstrated elevated risks of anxiety and depression among those with the condition, alongside barriers to diagnosis, social participation, and employment.

“Living with cerebral palsy can affect mental health through several interacting pathways,” said Shannel Elhelou, neuropsychologist and founder of Brain Centric Neuropsychology in Westlake Village, CA.

“Chronic pain, fatigue, disrupted sleep, and the effort required to navigate daily activities can take an emotional toll. Experiences of bullying, discrimination, social exclusion, and difficulty accessing care can add to that burden. Also, the early brain injury underlying cerebral palsy may contribute to difficulties with emotional regulation, although each person’s experience is different,” Elhelou told Healthline.

Help is out there

If you or someone you know is in crisis and considering suicide or self-harm, please seek support:

  • Call or text the 988 Suicide and Crisis Lifeline at 988 or chat at 988lifeline.org. Caring counselors are available to listen and provide free and confidential support 24/7.
  • Text HOME to the Crisis Text Line at 741741 to connect with a volunteer crisis counselor for free and confidential support 24/7.
  • Not in the United States? Find a helpline in your country with Befrienders Worldwide.
  • Call 911 or your local emergency services number if you feel safe to do so.

If you’re calling on behalf of someone else, stay with them until help arrives. You may remove weapons or substances that can cause harm if you can do so safely.

If you’re not in the same household, stay on the phone with them until help arrives.

RJ Mitte leaves behind a legacy of advocacy

Mitte played a key role in challenging stereotypes by portraying a teenager with cerebral palsy on “Breaking Bad.”

Outside of acting, Mitte advocated for people with disabilities through organizations including United Cerebral Palsy (UCP) and Shriners Children’s.

He also led the Mitte Foundation, a non-profit corporation founded in 1994 by his grandparents, Roy F. and Joann Cole Mitte, that improves the quality of life for people in Central Texas.

Mitte’s advocacy emphasized representation, inclusion, and opportunities for people with disabilities.

The legacy he leaves behind underscores the need to recognize mental health as an essential part of care for individuals with cerebral palsy and other disabilities.

Adults with cerebral palsy face higher rates of depression, anxiety

Cerebral palsy (CP) is a group of conditions affecting movement, posture, and coordination.

Most people with cerebral palsy have typical intelligence, although some also have intellectual disabilities or other comorbid physical and mental health conditions.

A 2018 study published in JAMA Neurology found that adults with cerebral palsy had a 28% higher risk of developing depression and a 40% higher risk of anxiety than adults without the condition.

A 2024 study in JAMA Network Open found higher recorded rates of anxiety and attention deficit hyperactivity disorder (ADHD) among children and young adults with cerebral palsy.

The researchers also identified comparatively low rates of depression and suicidal ideation, highlighting concerns that these conditions may be underdiagnosed.

“Research shows that adults with cerebral palsy have an increased risk of depression and anxiety,” Elhelou said.

“However, these conditions should never be viewed as an inevitable part of having a disability. One concern is ‘diagnostic overshadowing,’ when symptoms of depression, anxiety, or other mental health conditions are attributed to cerebral palsy itself, leading those conditions to go unrecognized and untreated. Someone’s fatigue, withdrawal, or changes in behavior deserve careful assessment rather than the assumption that ‘this is just their cerebral palsy,'” she continued.

“While these findings help us understand mental health risks among people with cerebral palsy, they cannot tell us what any one person was experiencing. Many interacting factors may contribute to someone dying by suicide, and it would be inappropriate to attribute an individual’s death to their disability.”

Young people with disabilities experience bullying, other barriers

A 2019 study published in Developmental Medicine & Child Neurology found that children with cerebral palsy had higher odds of anxiety disorders and behavioral challenges than children without the condition.

Difficulties with friendships and bullying were associated with some of these differences.

Factors that may affect well-being include:

  • bullying, stigma, and social exclusion
  • limited access to recreational and community activities
  • physical barriers that restrict participation and independence
  • difficulty accessing appropriate mental health assessments

The researchers also found that supportive friendships may help reduce some of these disparities, reinforcing the importance of inclusive schools and communities.

Suicidal ideation among those with disabilities

A 2021 study published in the American Journal of Preventive Medicine found that U.S. adults with disabilities faced increased risks of suicidal thoughts, planning, and attempts compared with adults without disabilities.

The 988 Lifeline received more than 8 million contacts through calls, texts, chats, and American Sign Language videophone in 2025, according to the Substance Abuse and Mental Health Services Administration.

Federal funding for the Lifeline’s dedicated LGBTQ+ youth service ended in July 2025, removing the option to press 3 for specialized support.

After pressure from Congress, the service was recently restored at the end of September, according to the Kaiser Family Foundation (KFF).

Support for those with cerebral palsy

Employment and financial independence can influence well-being for anyone, particularly those with cerebral palsy.

However, a 2024 study found that just 30.4% of young adults with cerebral palsy who participated in vocational rehabilitation programs were employed when they exited those programs.

Workplace accommodations, accessible hiring, vocational rehabilitation, and inclusive social opportunities can help address these barriers.

“Depression and anxiety are treatable, and they should never be dismissed as an inevitable part of living with a disability,” Elhelou said.

“If you notice persistent sadness, anxiety, hopelessness, or withdrawal, reach out to someone you trust and a mental health professional. You deserve a clinician who takes your concerns seriously and adapts treatment to your communication, mobility, or other access needs. Care should also address pain, fatigue, and sleep difficulties, which can affect emotional well-being.”

Organizations such as United Cerebral Palsy offer information and support for people with cerebral palsy and their families.

Healthcare professionals can also improve outcomes by screening for mental health concerns, using appropriate assessment tools, and connecting patients with accessible treatment.

Improved access to care, inclusive communities, and meaningful opportunities can help people with cerebral palsy and other disabilities thrive.

“Asking for help does not diminish your resilience or your contributions. If you are experiencing thoughts of suicide or feel overwhelmed, you do not have to wait until things feel unbearable to reach out,” Elhelou said.

“Supportive relationships, peer groups, and disability communities can offer connection and understanding. Staying engaged in activities that bring enjoyment and meaning can also help.”

#5019. PMOS, Formerly PCOS, May Be Associated with Alzheimer's Disease Risk

  • Polyendocrine metabolic ovarian syndrome (PMOS), formerly known as polycystic ovary syndrome (PCOS), has been linked to changes in brain function and cognition that may overlap with Alzheimer’s disease.
  • A new review suggests that metabolic conditions associated with PMOS, rather than PMOS itself, may help explain a potential link to Alzheimer’s disease and dementia risk, although more research is needed.
  • Regular exercise, a healthy diet, and managing metabolic risk factors such as high blood sugar and cholesterol may help support long-term brain health.

Polyendocrine metabolic ovarian syndrome (PMOS), formerly known as polycystic ovary syndrome (PCOS), has been linked to changes in brain function and cognition that may overlap with those seen in Alzheimer’s disease.

However, a review published October 13 in the Journal of the Endocrine Society suggests that metabolic conditions commonly associated with PMOS, rather than PMOS itself, may help explain a potential link to Alzheimer’s disease and dementia risk.

“Researchers are exploring whether some metabolic conditions commonly seen in women with PMOS, such as insulin resistance, obesity, type 2 diabetes, [and] elevated cholesterol, could play a role in dementia, since these are already known risk factors for cognitive decline,” Yesika Garcia, MD, board certified endocrinologist with Medical Offices of Manhattan, who was not involved in the study, told Healthline.  

“In other words, any potential connection may be related to these underlying metabolic issues rather than PMOS itself. I think it’s important to clarify that an association does not mean causation,” she added. 

PMOS does not independently increase the risk of dementia

The researchers searched PubMed for studies published through 2025 examining brain structure and function, cognitive performance, biomarkers associated with neurodegenerative diseases, and dementia risk in people with PMOS.

Because PMOS is a newer term, the researchers searched for studies using both PMOS and PCOS.

The review identified studies suggesting that people with PMOS may experience mild differences in certain cognitive abilities, including:

  • Executive function, such as planning and decision making
  • Working memory, or the ability to temporarily hold and use information
  • Processing speed
  • Word recognition

Some brain imaging studies using MRI and PET scans identified differences in brain structure and function among people with PMOS. Researchers also observed hormonal changes associated with differences in gray matter.

Some studies identified elevated biomarkers associated with Alzheimer’s disease in people with PMOS. These findings appeared to overlap with metabolic changes seen in type 2 diabetes, particularly those associated with obesity and insulin resistance.

The researchers also found that neurological differences were less pronounced when people with PMOS were compared with metabolically healthy control groups, suggesting that associated metabolic conditions may play an important role in these findings.

Overall, the review suggests that metabolic conditions associated with PMOS may contribute to changes in brain health, although more research is needed to determine whether PMOS independently affects Alzheimer’s disease or dementia risk.

“I think we need to be careful about drawing conclusions too early. The research raises some good questions, but it doesn’t prove that PCOS [PMOS] causes Alzheimer’s disease,” said Dung Trinh, MD, internist, of MemorialCare Medical Group and Chief Medical Officer of Healthy Brain Clinic in Irvine, CA, who wasn’t involved in the study. 

“What it does reinforce is how important metabolic health may be when it comes to protecting our brains as we get older,” he added. 

Sherry Ross, MD, board certified OB/GYN and Women’s Health Expert at Providence Saint John’s Health Center in Santa Monica, CA, who also wasn’t involved in the study, agreed. She also added that more attention and research need to be given to PMOS, brain changes, and future risks of dementia.

How PMOS and dementia may overlap

There are various ways that PMOS and dementia may share pathways and overlap, according to the researchers. Some of these shared pathways include: 

  • obesity and insulin resistance
  • dyslipidemia
  • gut dysbiosis, or an imbalance of microorganisms in your digestive tract
  • sleep disturbances
  • sex hormone alterations

Trinh explained that insulin resistance may be one of the most important connections between PMOS and dementia.

Although insulin is commonly associated with diabetes and blood sugar regulation, it also plays an important role in brain function. When insulin signaling doesn’t work properly, it may interfere with how brain cells use energy, potentially contributing to problems with memory and thinking over time.

Chronic inflammation, which can occur in people with PMOS, may also play a role in Alzheimer’s disease, Trinh told Healthline.

“Researchers are looking at how these metabolic changes may affect amyloid and tau, two proteins associated with Alzheimer’s. Some brain imaging studies have even found differences in brain structure and function among women with PCOS [PMOS],” he said. 

Ross added that elevated androgen levels, which are common in people with PMOS, may also play a role in dementia risk.

“An important distinction is that these risk factors are not present in every woman with PMOS, and having PMOS does not automatically mean someone will experience cognitive decline,” cautioned Garcia. 

How can people lower their risk of cognitive decline? 

Garcia noted that many of the dementia risk factors associated with PMOS are modifiable. 

“Regular exercise, including both cardiovascular activity and strength training, can improve insulin sensitivity, help with weight management, and support cardiovascular and brain health,” she added. 

Trinh emphasized the importance of diet.

“A Mediterranean-style diet, with plenty of vegetables, fruits, whole grains, healthy fats, and lean sources of protein, is a reasonable place to start,” he said.

Managing blood sugar, cholesterol, and blood pressure is also important, Trinh and Garcia noted.

“Medication, like metformin, can also improve insulin resistance, which may further reduce the risk of dementia,” Ross added. 

Getting adequate sleep, avoiding smoking, and staying mentally and socially active are additional ways to support long-term brain health, Garcia told Healthline. 

“For someone with PCOS [PMOS], I would encourage regular visits with their healthcare provider to monitor metabolic health and address problems early,” said Trinh. 

“While we cannot say that these interventions specifically prevent dementia in women with PMOS, we know they benefit overall metabolic and cardiovascular health, which are closely connected to brain health,” explained Garcia.

#5020. Viola Davis Says Shingles Pain Was a ‘9 Out of 10’: How It Changed Her Life

  • Award-winning actress and producer Viola Davis is opening up about her painful experience with shingles and how it changed her approach to her health.
  • Davis said the pain reached a 9 out of 10, prompting her to take her health more seriously and prioritize stress management.
  • Shingles risk increases with age, particularly after 50, but vaccination is more than 90% effective at preventing the disease.

Emmy, Grammy, Oscar, and Tony Award-winning actress and producer Viola Davis was 50 years old and at the height of her career when she developed shingles.

“All of a sudden, I got a rash in the middle of traveling and juggling [back-to-back] movies that started in the middle of my stomach [and] wrapped around my back,” she told Healthline. “And the pain was definitely at a 9 out of 10.”

Focused on work, Davis didn’t see a doctor right away. Her symptoms eventually subsided but later returned on her upper back.

“That is when I was like, ‘Oh, my goodness, I just got bitten by a poisonous spider,” said Davis. “It felt like really bad nerve pain…then someone mentioned the word shingles.”

After seeing a doctor, Davis learned she had shingles, which is caused by the same virus responsible for chickenpox. She also learned that the risk increases with age as the immune system naturally weakens.

“It can also lead to herpetic neuralgia, lifelong pain that can result from a shingles outbreak,” Robyn Liu, MD, member of the board of directors at the American Academy of Family Physicians, told Healthline.

Shingles can affect daily life, including work and sleep.

“Many people don’t realize that shingles can be associated with nerve pain, and for some, that pain can last for months or even years after the rash has resolved due to potential long-term complications,” Jennifer Caudle, DO, family medicine physician and associate professor at Rowan University School of Osteopathic Medicine, told Healthline.

Nearly one in three people in the U.S. develops shingles in their lifetime.

“Anyone who has had chickenpox can get it, but the risk goes up as we age,” said Liu.

How Shingles Changed Viola Davis’s Approach to Her Health

After learning about shingles, Davis said she realized that she could no longer ignore her body.

“You just cannot. I mean, I know that people say it at nauseam, but, for me, it meant slowing down,” she said. “But then the shingles slowed me down…even though I was getting up, I was working out, [the] pain and the sluggishness was awful.”

Now 61, Davis has teamed up with GSK for its “Shingles, actually” campaign to raise awareness about shingles.

“[I’m] sending the message out there for women to really honor themselves and honor their bodies,” she said. “Their bodies literally speak. They’re the other character in their lives.”

After her experience with shingles, Davis began scheduling regular annual checkups.

“Before that time, I did absolutely go to my doctor, but only when it was absolutely necessary, you know, to get my colonoscopy, get my mammogram, but I never went if something felt odd,” said Davis.

Now, whenever she feels off, she doesn’t hesitate to see her doctor. She makes it a point to be honest with them about what’s going on with her body, and she is not shy about asking questions.

“I feel that it’s my job. It’s what I owe myself, and not only [do] I owe myself, it’s what I owe my daughter, it’s what I owe my husband, it’s what I owe my elderly mom, who is in advanced dementia [and] I take care of her,” Davis said.

Caudle stressed the importance of bringing questions to your doctor.

“That’s what we’re here for. Sometimes patients ​tell me they don’t realize they can ask questions or introduce a topic that their healthcare provider didn’t bring up first,” she said. “But a simple question like, ‘Am I at risk for shingles?’ can be a great place to begin.”

How Viola Davis Prioritizes Stress Management After Shingles

Davis has made stress reduction a priority. When she developed shingles, she was juggling an intense workload and adjusting to life as a new mom after adopting her daughter.

“I was someone who just ignored stress,” said Davis. “[When] we meet each other, it becomes a contest: Who can work through their stress the best? I felt like I had to strike while the iron was hot.”

Today, Davis makes stress reduction a priority through activities such as spraying her pillows with lavender, taking Epsom salt baths, meditating, going to therapy, exercising, spending time outdoors, using vagus nerve stimulation, and connecting with family and friends.

“It’s like I overdo it now because I feel like I have to overdo it in order to compete with my schedule. But that’s what I owe myself,” Davis said.

Still, her profession can be intense and time consuming due to shooting and promotional schedules. She still makes time to de-stress and focuses on projects that bring her joy.

“Your career is only as important as how much you enjoy it; how much it adds to your life,” said Davis. “[For] me, it used to be something that I felt defined my worth. I don’t feel like it defines my worth now…I feel that I’m already worthy… I was born worthy. That’s how I see it.”

How to protect yourself from shingles

Vaccination is the best way to protect against shingles, Liu said. The shingles vaccine is more than 90% effective at preventing the disease.

“For children, that protection begins with the chickenpox vaccine, which can be given along with other routine childhood immunizations. Preventing chickenpox also lowers the risk of developing shingles later in life,” she said.

Liu recommends two doses of the shingles vaccine for adults 50 and older, as well as adults 19 and older with weakened immune systems.

“Some patients may have more questions because side effects can be more noticeable than with other vaccines, so it’s important to understand the tradeoff: short-term discomfort from the vaccine versus the risk of lasting complications from shingles,” said Liu.

Side effects may include headache or redness, swelling, itching, and soreness where the shot was given.

“I tell my patients that these reactions are common and usually clear up within a day or two. They’re a normal sign that your body is building protection,” said Liu.

#5021. Heart Failure Is Becoming More Common in Adults Under 65, Report Finds

  • Heart failure hospitalization and mortality rates are increasing among adults under 65, highlighting a growing concern for younger adults.
  • The global burden of heart failure has grown significantly since 1990, with researchers pointing to population growth and aging as major contributing factors.
  • While the underlying causes of heart failure vary by age and region, managing risk factors such as high blood pressure, diabetes, and obesity may help prevent the condition.

An estimated 56 million people worldwide were living with heart failure in 2023, more than double the number in 1990, according to a new Global Burden of Disease (GBD) analysis published September 30 in JAMA Cardiology.

Heart failure is a chronic condition in which the heart cannot pump enough blood to meet the body’s needs. Despite its name, heart failure doesn’t mean the heart has stopped beating.

A separate 2026 report from the Heart Failure Society of America (HFSA) found that heart failure contributed to more than 450,000 deaths in the United States in 2023, with particularly sharp increases in mortality among adults under 65.

Approximately 7.7 million U.S. adults (3%) are living with heart failure, according to the HFSA report. That number is projected to rise to 8.7 million by 2030, 10.3 million by 2040, and 11.4 million by 2050.

The GBD researchers said the findings highlight the growing burden of heart failure associated with population growth and aging, as well as the need for prevention and treatment strategies targeting common causes such as ischemic heart disease and hypertension (high blood pressure).

Heart failure hospitalizations and deaths rising among younger adults

Between 2010 and 2022, heart failure hospitalization rates increased among adults under 65 while declining among those 65 and older. Between 2001 and 2020, estimated heart failure mortality rates more than doubled among adults ages 25 to 44 and nearly doubled among those ages 45 to 64.

“I think we’re seeing the impact of cardiovascular risk factors developing earlier in life. Conditions such as obesity, diabetes, hypertension, and sleep apnea are affecting younger adults at higher rates than in previous generations,” Kevin Shah, MD, Program Director of Heart Failure Outreach at MemorialCare Heart & Vascular Institute at Long Beach Medical Center in Long Beach, CA, who wasn’t involved in the analysis, told Healthline. 

“When those risk factors are present for decades, they can eventually lead to heart failure at a younger age,” he said. 

Amit Blumfield, MD, a cardiologist at Manhattan Cardiology in New York City who was also not involved in the analysis, noted that improvements in early detection may also play a role.

“Our tools to detect early heart failure are also improving with time, and as a result, patients may receive an earlier diagnosis and can hopefully start medical therapy to prevent progression or possibly even reverse the process entirely.”

Heart failure deaths continue to rise in the United States

Heart failure contributed to 452,573 deaths in the United States in 2023, accounting for approximately 14.6% of all deaths that year, according to the HFSA report.

Age-adjusted heart failure mortality rates increased by approximately 37% between 2010 and 2023, with preliminary 2024 data suggesting the upward trend is continuing.

This increase appears to have reversed more than a decade of previous progress in reducing heart failure mortality.

What are the most common causes of heart failure?

Ischemic heart disease and hypertension were among the most common causes of heart failure in older adults, according to the GBD report. However, the underlying causes varied considerably by age group and region.

Among children and young adults, congenital heart abnormalities and rheumatic heart disease (RHD) were common causes of heart failure. Heart failure associated with alcohol use was most prevalent during middle adulthood.

The analysis also identified regional differences in the underlying causes of heart failure:

  • Rheumatic heart disease: South Asia, East Asia, Oceania, Southeast Asia, and parts of sub-Saharan Africa
  • Alcohol use: Eastern Europe, Australasia, the Caribbean, and Central Europe
  • Chagas disease: Southern, Andean, tropical, and central Latin America

The global burden of heart failure is expected to grow as populations age.

Can you prevent heart failure? 

While not all cases of heart failure can be prevented, managing certain risk factors can help reduce your risk. These include:

  • High blood pressure
  • Excessive alcohol consumption
  • Tobacco use

According to Shah, other important steps to help prevent heart failure include maintaining a healthy weight, staying physically active, and managing conditions such as diabetes and high cholesterol.

He added that regular medical care is important because many of these risk factors can be identified and treated long before heart failure develops.

“Heart failure is often treatable and may even be reversible with early diagnosis and treatment. It is important for patients to seek help early if they have concerning symptoms, such as exertional shortness of breath, chest pain, leg swelling, or anything else they’re concerned about. Talk with your doctors early and often,” said Blumfield.

#5022. Hormone Therapy May Help Improve Menopause Brain Fog, Mood Changes

  • Hormone therapy may affect changes in the brain that occur during menopause. 
  • A recent study has shown that hormone therapy may help improve “brain fog” symptoms during menopause.
  • Another study found that hormone therapy may improve mood changes that are often associated with menopause. 
  • Other research has alleviated long-standing concerns over a possible link between menopause hormone therapy and dementia. 

Menopause hormone therapy (MHT), also known as hormone replacement therapy (HRT), helps manage various menopause symptoms. 

Currently, MHT is approved by the Food and Drug Administration (FDA) to help relieve hot flashes, night sweats, vaginal dryness, and dyspareunia (pain during sex). 

However, recent research has found that MHT may also help improve mental symptoms like “brain fog” and mood changes. 

Other research also found that MHT does not affect dementia risk. This research followed the FDA’s removal of the black box warning on these products, which suggested they may increase the risk.  

Together, these studies show that MHT may help people experiencing menopause in ways not previously realized and that it is generally safe to use. 

Hormone therapy may ease  menopause ‘brain fog’ 

Research published in Scientific Reports on September 1 suggests that an estrogen hormone treatment that combines estriol and progesterone may help ease the symptoms of brain fog during menopause. 

Brain fog may involve memory lapses, difficulty concentrating, and slower thinking. 

Just about two-thirds of women experience cognitive difficulties, like brain fog, during the menopausal transition. However, there is currently no Food and Drug Administration (FDA)- approved treatment for these symptoms. 

This study was an observational case series that followed 20 menopausal women with an average age of 53. Each participant was given a baseline assessment for cognitive symptoms prior to treatment. 

They were then treated for 12 months with a customized regimen of estriol and progesterone.

Estriol is a natural form of estrogen that occurs during pregnancy and has been used in Europe and Asia to treat other symptoms of menopause. 

After 12 months, the participants reported significant reductions in: 

  • brain fog
  • concentration 
  • working memory 
  • verbal memory
  • processing speed
  • problem solving

“Estrogen plays a well-documented role in protecting the brain, yet there are still no approved type and dose of treatment that specifically targets the cognitive symptoms so many women experience during menopause,” Rhonda Voskuhl, MD, neurologist, member of the Comprehensive Menopause Center at UCLA Health, and senior study author, said in a press release. 

“Women are often told to simply live with brain fog, when in fact there is a neuroscience basis for it, and potentially a way to address it. That gap in care is what motivated us to look more closely at estriol,” Voskuhl said. 

This was a small, preliminary case series without a placebo group or randomization. More research is needed to confirm the benefits for cognitive symptoms. 

Key points

  • Brain fog is a common symptom of menopause with no approved treatment. 
  • A recent study found that hormone therapy with estriol and progesterone may help improve cognitive symptoms during menopause. 
  • More research is needed to confirm the effects of hormone therapy on cognitive symptoms. 

Hormone therapy may help with menopause mood changes

A study published September 9 in Menopause, the journal of the Menopause Society, showed that hormone therapy may help improve mood changes in women in midlife. 

The researchers noted that mood disturbances, such as depression, irritability, anxiety, and mental exhaustion, affect up to 68% of women during menopause. 

“The erratic changes and loss of estrogen associated with this hormonal transition can lower serotonin and dopamine levels, which [are] associated with depression and cognitive changes, including poor concentration, brain fog, and memory loss,” said Sherry Ross, MD, board certified OB/GYN and Women’s Health Expert at Providence Saint John’s Health Center in Santa Monica, CA, who wasn’t involved in the study. 

The study included 260 individuals with an average age of 52 years. Just over half of the participants had a history of anxiety or depression, and 24% were using an antidepressant medication at the start of the study. 

Each participant completed the Menopause Rating Scale (MRS) both before and after initiating hormone therapy for FDA-approved indications, such as hot flashes and night sweats. 

The average follow-up during the study was 4.5 months. Between the baseline and the follow-up, there was a significant reduction in the MRS psychological scores. The percentage of participants with severe mood symptoms decreased from 62.3% to 24.6%. 

Treatment response didn’t appear to differ by age, menopausal stage, psychiatric history, or antidepressant use. These findings suggest that hormone therapy may help improve mood symptoms in midlife, especially for those with higher severity of symptoms before treatment. 

“This study supports the evidence that starting hormone therapy for the typical disruptive symptoms of menopause may bring collateral benefits, including improvement in mood,” Ross said. “Communicating with your healthcare provider is always best to manage your symptoms and prepare yourself properly for this next stage of life.” 

Key points

  • A recent study showed that hormone therapy may help improve mood changes associated with menopause. 
  • The biggest improvement was seen in those with more severe symptoms before beginning treatment. 
  • Improvement with hormone therapy was seen despite age, menopause stage, and psychiatric history. 

Menopause hormone therapy not linked to dementia

For decades, women have been warned about the serious potential risks associated with menopause hormone therapy (MHT). This included an FDA “black box” warning citing complications like cardiovascular disease, cancer, and dementia. 

However, a recent meta-analysis commissioned by the World Health Organization (WHO) that included data from more than a million women, and was published in The Lancet Healthy Longevity, found that MHT neither increased nor decreased dementia risk. 

“The World Health Organisation currently provides no guidance on MHT and dementia risk, leaving a critical gap for women, clinicians, and policymakers. Our work will help inform upcoming WHO guidelines on reducing the risk of cognitive decline and dementia,” Melissa Melville, a PhD candidate and aging researcher in the Department of Clinical Health Psychology at the University College London, and first author of the study, told Healthline in a previous interview. 

In the same interview, Melville stressed that the importance of this work is twofold: for clinicians, there is an urgent need for higher-quality research, and for patients, decisions about MHT should be guided by symptoms and well-established benefits and risks.

Key points

  • A new meta-analysis has helped to ease long-standing fears regarding the association between menopause hormone therapy and dementia. 
  • The analysis reviewed data from more than a million women and found that MHT neither raises nor decreases the risk of dementia. 
  • The researchers noted that decisions about MHT should be guided by symptoms and well-established benefits and risks.

#5023. Michael Strahan Shares the Words That Helped His Daughter Through Brain Cancer Treatment

  • Michael Strahan opened up to Healthline about his daughter Isabella’s brain cancer diagnosis and how the experience strengthened their relationship.
  • After undergoing surgery, radiation therapy, and chemotherapy for medulloblastoma, Isabella has returned to college and is embracing life after treatment.
  • Strahan shared how he helped his daughter maintain hope during treatment and why they’re speaking out to support other families affected by cancer.

For years, Michael Strahan appeared in commercials for St. Jude Children’s Research Hospital, delivering a message he never imagined would become so personal: “No parent should have to hear that their child has cancer.”

But in 2023, the beloved TV host and former NFL player faced that reality when his daughter, Isabella, was diagnosed with brain cancer at 19 years old.

“[That] line that I used to deliver, usually at the end of the commercial…here I am actually filling that line in my life in real time,” Strahan told Healthline.

Strahan believes his longtime connection with St. Jude was more than a coincidence. After years of advocating for families affected by childhood cancer, he found himself on the other side of that experience.

“When I needed that family…they were there for me,” he said. “I’m just appreciative, and I think things come back to you; give out good, good comes back.”

Facing brain cancer at 19 years old

In October 2023, Isabella began experiencing vomiting, headaches, and balance issues during her freshman year at the University of Southern California (USC). Tests revealed that she had medulloblastoma, a fast-growing cancerous brain tumor located in the cerebellum. She underwent emergency brain surgery, radiation therapy, and four rounds of chemotherapy.

While medulloblastoma typically occurs in children between the ages of 3 and 9, it can also affect young adults like Isabella.

“[The] biology in adolescents and young adults can differ meaningfully from pediatric cases, requiring specialized expertise at centers of excellence for this disease,” Arnab Chakravarti, MD, director of the Brain Tumor Program at The Ohio State University, told Healthline.

Medulloblastoma is sometimes confused with glioblastoma, the most common type of malignant primary brain tumor in adults. Glioblastoma is particularly aggressive, with a median overall survival of just 15 months and a 5-year survival rate of less than 10%.

However, Chakravarti explained that medulloblastoma has a significantly higher cure rate than glioblastoma.

“[This is] a point of genuine hope that deserves to be communicated clearly,” he said.

Radiation therapy is a cornerstone of medulloblastoma treatment. Isabella received proton therapy, which uses precisely targeted radiation to treat tumors while limiting exposure to surrounding healthy tissue.

“For medulloblastoma, this physical precision is transformative,” Chakravarti said. “Proton craniospinal irradiation dramatically reduces [collateral] exposure — preserving cochlear function and hearing, reducing cardiovascular risk, protecting the thyroid, and most critically, sparing developing brain tissue in ways that translate into measurably better neurocognitive outcomes years after treatment.”

He added that multiple prospective studies have shown that children and young adults treated with proton craniospinal irradiation experience less cognitive decline and better quality of life compared to those treated with conventional radiation.

Today, Isabella is back at USC, embracing the everyday routines she once had to put on hold.

“I am back at USC, so…it’s been great, in the midst of exams, so it’s a little stressful, but I actually [am] inviting the stress in because at one point I didn’t have exams, so I’m happy to be back,” Isabella told Healthline.

Along with attending classes, Isabella is enjoying activities such as working out and cooking. She continues to undergo MRI scans of her head and spine every three months, but said that after another year, the scans will become less frequent, occurring every six months.

“But really…I don’t have a lot of doctor’s appointments,” she said.

Her outlook on life has also changed.

“It feels like a brand-new life, but it’s a good one. And that’s wonderful,” Isabella said.

Cancer strengthened their father-daughter bond

Isabella said her family’s support played an important role in helping her through cancer treatment. Spending so much time together in the hospital brought them closer as they watched movies and played cards and board games to pass the time.

“[It] really, really bonds you to people, and just having my dad and my whole family there was something that [you] can’t beat,” she told Healthline.

Michael said that despite the difficult circumstances, he cherishes the time he spent with his daughter. Before her diagnosis, Isabella was a typical teenager who spent much of her time at school or with friends.

“They say you spend the most time with your kid for the first few years of their life, and then once school starts, you don’t get a chance to spend as much time as you would like with them,” he said.

While Michael said he and Isabella already had a close relationship, her diagnosis brought the family even closer and helped them reflect on what mattered most.

“[What] was really important was family, and relationships, and the love that you have for each other, and the support you have to give each other,” he said. “I think that our relationship now is better than ever.”

Today, Michael makes an effort to visit Isabella at USC whenever he’s in Los Angeles during the NFL season.

“Some weekends she [has time for me]. Some she doesn’t,” he said. “But just to have that relationship with your child, where they want to hang out with you, is a good feeling, and that’s the relationship that we have.”

Turning experience into advocacy

Michael and Isabella are sharing their story to raise awareness about brain cancer and offer hope to other families facing similar challenges.

They recently participated in a fireside chat in New York City hosted by Real Chemistry and SMAC, where they discussed their family’s experience navigating Isabella’s diagnosis, mental health, caregiving, and the impact of sharing their story.

Throughout her treatment, Isabella also documented her experience through a series of YouTube vlogs and the ABC primetime documentary, “Life Interrupted: Isabella Strahan’s Fight to Beat Cancer.”

For Michael, helping others is a value he learned from his parents. He recalled how his father used his transportation business in Germany to support people in need after retiring from the military.

“[He] would take donations, and use his money and his trucks, and drive stuff to Yugoslavia and Czechoslovakia, and drop it off there at local churches and the shelters for people, who he had no connection with,” said Michael. “So for me, I think I do what I feel is on my heart and what’s important to me.”

Why Michael Strahan believes hope matters when facing cancer

For parents caring for children with cancer, Michael emphasized that it’s OK to feel overwhelmed or emotional. He encouraged them to show their children love and support while helping them maintain hope for the future.

“I think positivity is the main thing because it was so tough on her at the time when she was [saying], ‘I can’t do it. I can’t do this. I can’t do that,’” said Michael.

Michael said he made a conscious effort to replace words like “can’t” and “if” with “when” while encouraging Isabella during treatment.

“Anytime she got down on herself, I’d say, ‘when you go back to college, when you do this, when you do that,’” said Michael. “And I just think the positive attitude and the love that you can give and the confidence that you can display towards your loved one [or] your child who’s going through something like this is the only thing you can do because you’re at the mercy of medicine.”

Now in her senior year at USC, Isabella is looking ahead to life after graduation.

“[There’s] a lot of interesting opportunities and things that I want to pursue,” she said.

Michael has no doubt she will find her way.

“She’s so talented, and this experience, in my opinion, has opened up my eyes to more talent that she has that I didn’t realize…the talent to connect people, and be in front of a crowd. I always thought she’d be nervous because I get nervous, but she’s better than me,” he said.

#5024. Wellness Apps, Medically Tailored Meals Help Slow Weight Gain After GLP-1s

  • Most people regain a significant portion of the weight they lost within 1 year of stopping GLP-1 medications such as semaglutide (Wegovy) or tirzepatide (Zepbound).
  • A small study found that structured support, including a digital weight management app or medically tailored meals, may help slow weight regain after stopping GLP-1 medications.
  • Healthy eating habits, regular exercise, and ongoing medical support may help people maintain weight loss, while some may benefit from continuing GLP-1 medications long-term.

Weight regain is common after stopping GLP-1 medications such as semaglutide (Wegovy, Ozempic) and tirzepatide (Mounjaro, Zepbound).

In fact, previous research has shown that most people regain at least half of their lost weight within 1 year of stopping treatment, with weight regain often accelerating during the first 3–4 months.

However, a new study suggests that structured support, including a weight management app or medically tailored meals, may help slow weight regain after discontinuing GLP-1 medications.

The small study, published October 7 in Obesity Science & Practice, found that participants who received either intervention regained significantly less weight over four months than those receiving usual care.

How researchers tested ways to limit weight regain after stopping GLP-1s

The study included 39 adults with an average age of 50, an average weight of 189 pounds, and an average body mass index (BMI) of 31.4.

All participants had lost more than 10% of their body weight while taking GLP-1 medications and had stopped treatment within the previous 30 days.

All participants also completed questionnaires at the beginning and end of the study and used Bluetooth scales to track their weight at home throughout the four-month period.

Researchers randomly assigned participants to one of the following three groups:

Usual care

Participants continued to receive care from their healthcare providers, which may include nutrition, exercise, and lifestyle counseling from a clinician or registered dietitian. The type and amount of support varied depending on the provider’s recommendations and available resources. The researchers did not provide any additional intervention.

Digital weight management app

Participants received a subscription to Noom, an app that uses behavioral strategies to support weight loss and maintenance. These include tracking food intake, exercise, and weight, as well as educational lessons on the psychological, physiological, social, and environmental factors that influence eating behaviors.

The app also provides tools to help users recognize and manage triggers that may lead to overeating. Participants were encouraged to use the app for at least 15 minutes daily, including completing lessons, logging food, and tracking their weight.

Medically tailored meals

These are typically nutrient-dense meals developed by registered dietitians to meet the nutritional needs of people with specific health conditions, such as chronic kidney disease or uncontrolled type 2 diabetes.

For this study, participants in this group selected 10 portion-controlled, “weight wellness” meals each week from more than 70 options, which were delivered to their homes.

Medically tailored meals and wellness app slowed weight regain

After four months, participants who received either medically tailored meals or access to the digital weight management app experienced significantly less weight regain than those receiving usual care.

The estimated weight regain was 10.75% of body weight in the usual-care group, compared with 3.6% in the Noom group and 3.58% in the medically tailored meals group.

Although participants in all three groups regained weight, the findings suggest that structured nutritional or behavioral support may help limit weight regain after stopping GLP-1 medications.

“These findings highlight the benefits of ongoing support for patients on a weight loss journey,” said Mir Ali, MD, medical director of MemorialCare Surgical Weight Loss Center at Orange Coast Medical Center in Fountain Valley, CA. Ali wasn’t involved in the study.

Denise Pate, MD, a board certified internist with Medical Offices of Manhattan who was also not involved in the study, told Healthline that the findings are significant “because they show that obesity management is multifactorial, not only medication, but the combination of types of meals and behavioral change all play a role.”

However, because this was a small, four-month study, larger, longer-term trials are needed to determine whether these approaches can help people maintain weight loss over time after stopping GLP-1 medications.

Should you stay on GLP-1 medications long term?

Ali noted that research suggests GLP-1 medications are most effective for weight management when taken long-term.

“Obesity needs to be recognized and treated as a chronic condition, similar to diabetes or hypertension,” he said.

Pate agreed, emphasizing that obesity is not always recognized as a chronic condition that may require ongoing treatment.

“I feel that the medical community, insurance, and the population as a whole have yet to fully accept obesity as a chronic medical condition, which it most definitely is for a large part of patients. For this reason, I think patients should stay on these medications long term if needed,” she said.

“GLP-1 medications are highly effective tools for transitioning to a healthier diet and lifestyle, which for most people may require long-term use,” Ali added.

Ways to maintain weight loss after stopping GLP-1 medications

Pate explained that one of the goals of GLP-1 medications is to help people eat smaller portions by recognizing when they feel full and stopping when they do.

“I tell my patients from the start, this is the goal,” she said, adding that she hopes patients will retain those habits after stopping treatment.

Ali recommended maintaining a balanced diet rich in protein and vegetables while limiting added sugars and refined carbohydrates.

He added that tracking apps and regular follow-up appointments with a healthcare professional or registered dietitian may also help people maintain their progress.

“Patients need to understand that the GLP medication is not a miracle drug, although with the dramatic weight loss, it can be perceived as such. It is only a tool to be used in conjunction with an exercise routine as well as a healthy diet,” Pate said.

#5025. Letter from the Editor-in-Chief: Lung Cancer Can Look Like Anyone

  • Up to 1 in 5 lung cancer cases in the United States occur in people who’ve never smoked, but our picture of the disease hasn’t caught up.
  • Healthline Editor-in-Chief Robin Hough talks with Gini Harrison, a psychologist and his friend of nearly 30 years, whose only symptom was a sore shoulder blamed on breastfeeding. Her stage 4 EGFR-positive lung cancer diagnosis took nearly nine months.
  • They discuss testing, catching lung cancer sooner, and the documentary film, Our Lungs: Time to Breathe, which follows filmmaker Sarah Li and the family of Alex, a 24-year-old who died weeks after diagnosis.

Picture someone with lung cancer. Now picture a 24-year-old soccer player in the prime of his life. A filmmaker in her early 40s with a camera in her hand, doing what she loves best. A busy mom of two, with a shoulder so sore she can’t pick up her newborn, yet struggling to get a diagnosis. 

The gap between the picture in our heads and the people actually being diagnosed is costing time. And time is the one thing with lung cancer you cannot get back. 

We know that lung cancer is the most common cancer worldwide and the leading cause of cancer death. And while lung cancer incidence rates are falling in many countries as fewer people smoke, lung cancer in people who have never smoked is on the rise globally, both as a proportion and in absolute numbers. 

According to the CDC, as many as 20% of lung cancers diagnosed in the US are in people who have never smoked. Data suggests that non-smoking lung cancer is now among the most common causes of cancer-related deaths worldwide.

The numbers are stark. Lung cancer can affect anyone. And that includes the young – like Alex, one of the subjects of a powerful new short documentary commissioned by the charity EGFR Positive Lung Cancer UK for their Our Lungs: Time to Breathe campaign. 

Alex was a 24-year-old soccer player who died just weeks after receiving a diagnosis of Epidermal growth factor receptor (EGFR) positive lung cancer, following several misdiagnoses born of the fact that no one suspected lung cancer in one so young, fit, and with no smoking history. The film was made by filmmaker Sarah Li, who herself was diagnosed with Stage 4 EGFR+ lung cancer in April 2024, just a month after Alex died from the same mutation of the disease, and charts her journey with survivor’s guilt, what an earlier diagnosis may have meant for Alex, and what that fact means for her own future. 

Ahead of Lung Cancer Awareness Month in November, I sat down with my friend, Gini Harrison, to talk about the film, her own journey, and why catching lung cancer sooner matters so much. Gini is a Professor of Psychology at The Open University, a trustee of EGFR Positive Lung Cancer UK, and is living with stage 4 EGFR-positive lung cancer. She’s also the mom at the top of this piece, whose sore shoulder was initially attributed to breastfeeding posture.

This interview has been lightly edited for clarity and length.

EGFR-positive lung cancer: Warning signs

Take me back to the beginning. What were your initial symptoms, and what were you told?

Gini Harrison: I’d had my son, Michael, in February 2021 and was on maternity leave. My daughter, Emily, was five. I think the shoulder pain started around March, but that was it. No cough, no breathlessness… nothing that made me think about my lungs.

Initially, they gave me painkillers, which helped a bit. But the pain kept getting worse. Eventually, I started losing movement and strength in my arm, and it got to the point where I couldn’t pick up my son anymore.

This was all happening during COVID, so my appointments were over the telephone. Eventually, after months without being examined face-to-face, I paid to see a shoulder specialist. As soon as he touched my shoulder blade and I practically leaped out of the chair, he knew something wasn’t right. He ordered an MRI, which showed a tumor in my shoulder blade and another in my upper right lung. In December 2021, I was diagnosed with stage IV lung cancer.

What do you remember about that period, and how did it feel to be reassured when your instinct said otherwise?

Gini Harrison: I wasn’t thinking, “This must be cancer.” I was just thinking, “Why isn’t this getting better?”

And I think that distinction matters. Patients shouldn’t have to identify their own disease before they can get help. You should be able to say that something is wrong and be listened to.

What went through your head when you heard “lung cancer”? Did you have any of the assumptions you now push back against?

Gini Harrison: It was devastating. So many of my first questions were about the children: “How are we going to tell them?” “Am I going to be here to see them grow up?” “Will they remember me?” Suddenly, there was a question mark over a future I’d taken for granted.

I was also completely shocked by the type of cancer. If someone had said breast cancer, I don’t think I would have been anywhere near as surprised. But lung cancer?! I’d always associated it with smoking, and I’d never smoked.

I hadn’t imagined that this particular cancer could ever happen to me. So yes, I had some assumptions that I now challenge. I understand how deeply rooted they are because I had them too.

What lung cancer ‘looks like’

When people learn you have lung cancer, what do they say? What’s the first question you get asked?

Gini Harrison: People are often shocked, and one of the first questions is whether I smoked.

And they often look healthy. That doesn’t mean everything is easy, or that there aren’t side effects. It just means you can’t tell what someone is dealing with by looking at them.

I think we’ve given lung cancer such a narrow public image that people outside it can become almost invisible.

Has anyone ever asked if you smoked? How do you answer, and how should it land?
