Gini Harrison: Oh, yes! Once, I spoke to a doctor about a breathing problem after radiotherapy. He asked, “How much did you smoke?” When I said I’d never smoked, he replied, “Well, how did you get lung cancer then?”

On another occasion, I went to a hospice for help managing side effects, and someone there asked, “How are you still alive?” That’s quite a thing to hear when you’ve come looking for support!

When people ask about smoking, I try to explain that I’ve never smoked and I’m certainly not alone. Never smoking doesn’t make you immune. But I’m also very clear that nobody deserves lung cancer, whether they’ve smoked or not. We shouldn’t be creating a hierarchy of who deserves compassion.

Smoking history matters medically. But in a personal conversation, “How are you doing?” is a much kinder place to start.

Genetics and biomarker testing 

Explain biomarker or genetic testing as if I’m a friend (which I am!) who’s never heard of it. Why does it matter?

Gini Harrison: Lung cancer isn’t one single disease. Two people can both have lung cancer, but different changes in their cells may be driving it.

Genes contain the instructions that tell our cells how to work, including when to grow and divide. Changes in those genes (called mutations) can cause cells to keep dividing when they shouldn’t, forming a tumor. In my case, there’s a mutation in a gene called EGFR.

EGFR-positive lung cancer is a type of non-small cell lung cancer caused by a genetic mutation in the EGFR gene, which keeps the EGFR receptor switched “on,” leading to uncontrolled cell growth and enhanced cancer cell survival.

Biomarker testing looks for these changes, as well as other features of the cancer that can guide treatment. If we know what’s helping the cancer grow, there may be a medication that specifically targets it.

The word “genetic” can sound scary, particularly when you have children. But these changes usually develop in cancer cells over a person’s lifetime. They aren’t necessarily inherited.

Why early diagnosis is so important 

What would you say to someone reading this who has a symptom that won’t go away?

Gini Harrison: Please get it checked. And if it persists, worsens or doesn’t improve with treatment, go back. Explain what’s changed and how it’s affecting your life. Ask, “What happens next if this doesn’t get better?”

The symptoms don’t always look like lung symptoms. Mine was shoulder pain.

Most symptoms will turn out to be something other than cancer. But when a symptom continues to worsen, the original explanation needs to be reconsidered. Lower risk doesn’t mean no risk.

And I don’t want all the responsibility to fall on patients to keep pushing. Reassurance should come with a next step.

Our Lungs: Time to Breathe – Alex and Sarah’s stories 

How did you get involved in advocacy, and what was the hardest thing about being part of it?

Gini Harrison: Once I had recovered from my initial treatment, I realized that I wanted to help others in my situation, so I became a trustee of the charity EGFR Positive Lung Cancer UK. Through this work, we produced a film, Our Lungs: Time to Breathe. At the heart of it, it challenges the perception of what someone with lung cancer looks like.

The hardest part is how close to home it feels. Alex was so young, and that worked against him. Watching his family talk about what happened is heartbreaking. It’s just so unfair.

What did you hope people would see in Sarah’s and Alex’s stories?

Gini Harrison: Sarah’s story shows life continuing alongside lung cancer. She’s a filmmaker with ambitions, people she loves, and things she wants to do.

Through Alex’s family, you see the enormity of the loss. I hope people feel that, rather than simply hearing another statistic.

Lung cancer and the bigger picture

What’s changed since you were diagnosed, and what hasn’t?

Gini Harrison: For me, the biggest thing is that my children are growing up and I’m here to see it. Michael is now five (the age Emily was when I was diagnosed), and Emily is about to turn ten. I can’t tell you how grateful I am to be here with them.

It wasn’t about big bucket-list things. It was about being here every day. Seeing Michael start school. Seeing Emily reach double figures. Those milestones are enormous when you’ve wondered whether you’d get to see them.

After chemotherapy and radiotherapy, I’ve had no evidence of disease since May 2022, and my scans remain stable. But a big change has been seeing the progress in available treatment. It gives me more hope about what might be available if I need treatment again.

The public image of lung cancer still needs to catch up. We need better recognition of symptoms, access to appropriate testing and treatment, and support for the whole person. As a psychologist as well as a patient, I’m particularly aware that psychological well-being and family life need attention alongside the cancer itself.

What do you want people to do after reading this?

Gini Harrison: I’d love for people to share the film and help get it in front of those who wouldn’t normally think lung cancer has anything to do with them. And take something from it into everyday life: book the appointment you’ve been putting off, ask your doctor about testing, or respond with kindness when someone tells you about their diagnosis.

Just being willing to rethink your assumptions could make a difference.

What do you wish more people would say to someone in your position?

Gini Harrison: “You don’t have to put a brave face on with me. How are you really doing?”

When you look well, or your scans are good, people can assume you’re “fine now”. But I’m not sure the fear ever completely goes away. Every scan is terrifying. It feels like there’s a sword of Damocles hanging over my head, and part of me is waiting for the bad scan. I also still have lasting side effects from treatment, and probably always will.

My life has fundamentally changed. Some of that has brought good things, but it doesn’t make the difficult parts disappear. It helps when someone gives you room to be honest about both, without expecting you to reassure them or turn everything into a positive story.

Gini and I have been friends for nearly 30 years, and, while she’d probably cringe to hear me say it, I remain in awe of the way she approaches life with cancer. I am so grateful for her sharing her experiences with me for this column. What Gini asks of us is simple: look again, ask the question, listen, and act on your instincts. Our Lungs: Time to Breathe is where that starts for her. You can learn more about the Our Lungs: Time to Breathe campaign and the wider work of EGFR Positive Lung Cancer UK by clicking the links. — Robin Hough

Robin Hough is the Editor-in-Chief of Healthline, and in addition to this regular Letter from the Editor column, he can be found every other Saturday in our Healthline Daily newsletter. He’ll soon be launching a dedicated Letter from the Editor newsletter coming to an inbox near you later in the fall.

#5026. Taking Blood Pressure Meds? DASH Diet Likely Safe for Potassium Levels

  • A new study reports that the DASH diet does not significantly increase potassium levels in people taking high blood pressure medication.
  • Researchers said that the diet doesn’t appear to cause significant changes in kidney function.
  • Experts say the DASH diet offers many health benefits, but they recommend that people gradually integrate it into their daily lives.

People who adhere to the Dietary Approaches to Stop Hypertension (DASH) diet do not experience significant changes in potassium levels while taking blood pressure medications or nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory (NSAID) drugs, according to a new study.

Researchers also reported that people taking these medications do not experience significant changes in their kidney function.

Both types of medications are known to increase potassium levels. High potassium levels can lead to hyperkalemia, a condition that can cause issues with the heart rhythm. People with impaired kidney function due to chronic kidney disease are at increased risk of hyperkalemia.

“The DASH diet is a well-known, proven nutrition pattern that is known to improve blood pressure,” said Marian Budu, MD, a clinical research coordinator on the GoFresh Trials at Beth Israel Deaconess Medical Center in Boston and the presenting author of the study, in a news release.

“Knowing that the DASH diet did not increase blood potassium levels in this population with normal baseline potassium levels provides assurance that the DASH diet can be prescribed to a wide range of people,” she continued.

The preliminary findings are being presented at the American Heart Association’s Hypertension Scientific Sessions 2026 meeting this week in Virginia. The research has not been published yet in a peer-reviewed journal.

Experts not involved in the study told Healthline that the research provides medical professionals with important information.

“While we have traditionally exercised caution before recommending the DASH diet in patients who are prescribed ACE inhibitors, angiotensin receptor blockers, or potassium-sparing diuretics, these results show that those patients could likely adopt the DASH diet without significant concern for harm,” said Cheng-Han Chen, MD, an interventional cardiologist and medical director of the Structural Heart Program at MemorialCare Saddleback Medical Center in Laguna Hills, CA.

“DASH diets do not appear to increase potassium levels to any significant degree in patients taking medications that are known to potentially raise serum potassium,” added Kevin Volpp, MD, the founding director of the Penn Center for Health Incentives and Behavioral Economics in Pennsylvania and the scientific lead of the American Heart Association’s Health Care by Food initiative.

“This is important because it is a theoretical concern that diets rich in potassium could cause high levels in potassium in some patients.”

DASH diet didn’t raise potassium levels with blood pressure meds

The new study was a secondary analysis of the GoFresh and GoFresh Rx randomized trials that enrolled Black adults in the greater Boston area with high blood pressure.

Some of the participants were taking medications to lower their blood pressure, and others were not taking any hypertension drugs.

The study focused on four classes of blood pressure medications: angiotensin-converting enzyme inhibitors (ACEi), angiotensin receptor blockers (ARBs), and potassium-sparing diuretics and beta-blockers, as well as NSAIDs.

The analysis took place in 2026 and included 356 Black adults with an average age of 53. About 70% were women.

At the start of the study, the participants had systolic blood pressure readings between 120 mm Hg and less than 150 mm Hg. They also had a diastolic blood pressure reading of less than 100 mm Hg.

The participants also had varying degrees of kidney function. In addition, they had limited access to grocery stores.

Half of the participants received three months of weekly DASH-patterned groceries and weekly nutrition counseling sessions. The other half received a cash stipend every four weeks to buy groceries, with no nutrition counseling.

The participants’ blood potassium levels were measured at the beginning of the study and again after 12 weeks.

The researchers reported that eating more potassium-rich foods in the DASH diet did not increase potassium levels to dangerous levels among participants taking blood pressure medications.

They noted no significant increase in potassium levels, regardless of whether participants were assigned to the dietary intervention or the monetary stipend group.

In a news release, the researchers acknowledged that their study did not include people with stage 4 or stage 5 kidney disease or those with higher-than-normal potassium levels.

They added that the study population comprised only Black individuals living in Boston, so the results may not generalize to the broader population.

The study authors said that future research should focus on participants with suboptimal kidney function and those with higher potassium levels.

Volpp agreed that more research is needed.

“This study was conducted in patients with non-elevated potassium levels at baseline and does not answer the question of whether they are safe in patients with elevated potassium levels or significant impairments in renal function,” he told Healthline.

Chen noted that potassium levels should be taken seriously.

“Certain classes of blood pressure medications can potentially cause high potassium levels, especially in people with kidney disease,” he told Healthline. “If potassium levels become too high, this can lead to symptoms such as fatigue, nausea, and muscle pain, or even serious heart problems such as arrhythmias.”

Get started with the DASH diet

The DASH diet is a dietary plan that emphasizes fruits, vegetables, and lean proteins such as chicken, fish, and beans.

The plan also includes whole grains, low fat dairy products, and cooking with vegetable oils. The diet discourages the intake of red meat, salt, fat, and added sugars.

Previous research has indicated that the DASH diet can provide numerous health benefits.

A preliminary 2022 study concluded that the dietary plan can help lower blood pressure and improve heart health, but those results haven’t been published in a scientific journal. Still, the findings were consistent with a 2019 study that found the DASH diet can help lower the risk of heart disease.

In addition, a 2020 study reported that adults aged 65 and older with obesity lost body fat while following the DASH Diet. A 2023 study suggested the diet can help reduce belly fat.

A 2019 meta-analysis suggested that the DASH diet may also help lower the risk of certain cancers.

Many of these protective effects are attributed to the diet’s high fruit and vegetable content.

Chen said the DASH diet has many benefits, but the eating plan might not be for everybody.

“The DASH diet has been shown to be beneficial in lowering blood pressure, losing weight, and controlling blood sugar and cholesterol levels,” he said. “However, the amount of planning and portion tracking required to follow the diet may be too much for some people.”

Chen recommended that people ease into the DASH diet.

“The best way to start on the DASH diet is to slowly introduce the changes to your meals,” he said. “This can be as simple as including a few extra servings of fruits and vegetables each day as well as swapping some whole grain products for refined grains. Over time, more of the diet can be incorporated.”

Volpp agreed that introducing the DASH diet in increments is a good strategy.

“For many people, it is easiest to make small incremental changes sequentially,” he said. “One way to get started is to increase fruit by a couple of servings a day. Another is to pay attention to the amount of sodium in packaged foods and consciously choose products lower in sodium. A third is to make a conscious effort to eat more vegetables.”

“Make changes first that are easiest for you to make; it will be easier to make those changes stick,” Volpp added.

#5027. Pain Prevalence Higher in Women, Increases Most Rapidly Before 55

  • A large global study found that pain prevalence increases most rapidly before age 55 and is consistently higher in women than men across 11 body sites.
  • Back pain was the most commonly reported type of pain, affecting 40% of participants.
  • The likelihood of experiencing any bodily pain increased with age, while high intensity and generalized pain peaked in mid-to-late adulthood before declining.
  • Smoking, obesity, and lower household income were associated with a higher burden of pain.

Women experience a higher prevalence of pain than men across different areas of the body, according to a large global study published October 5 in Nature Medicine.

The analysis, which included more than 6 million people, also found that pain prevalence generally increased with age, with the most rapid increases occurring before age 55.

The findings come as lower back pain, neck pain, and osteoarthritis — all common causes of pain — are projected to become more prevalent globally by 2050.

Pain is more prevalent in women than men

Pain, especially lower back pain, is the leading cause of disability worldwide. However, researchers have lacked a standardized way to examine how self-reported pain varies across the life span and different regions of the world.

This new study is one of the largest efforts to date to examine self-reported pain using a standardized approach.

The researchers analyzed data from 6,125,459 individuals spanning 118 countries from 1990 to 2025. Participants ranged in age from 5 to more than 100, and 55% were female.

They used self-reported data on pain across 11 body sites: 

  • head 
  • face
  • neck or shoulder 
  • foot or ankle 
  • hand or wrist
  • elbow 
  • chest 
  • back 
  • stomach or abdomen 
  • hip 
  • knee

The researchers found that women consistently reported a higher prevalence of pain across all 11 sites than men.

The largest differences between women and men were in facial pain, headache, and stomach or abdominal pain.

Pain prevalence also increased most rapidly before age 55, especially at musculoskeletal sites such as the hips, back, and knees.

Back pain is the most common pain condition

The researchers found that the prevalence of pain varied considerably by body region and differed by age.

Back pain was the most commonly reported, affecting 40% of participants, while facial pain was the least common at 2%.

Neel Anand, MD, a board certified orthopedic spine surgeon and director of the Cedars-Sinai Spine Center in Los Angeles, who wasn’t involved in the study, explained some factors that may contribute to the high prevalence of back pain.

“Our modern lifestyles involve more prolonged sitting, less physical activity, and more time spent looking down at phones, tablets, and computers, all of which can contribute to poor posture and increased stress on the spine,” he said. 

“As we get older, the discs between our vertebrae naturally lose moisture and elasticity, making them more vulnerable to degeneration, bulging, and herniation. Excess weight and smoking can accelerate this process,” Anand added. 

In addition to pain at individual body sites, the researchers examined three broader measures: any bodily pain, high intensity pain, and generalized pain.

Any bodily pain was defined as pain reported at any site. High intensity pain was defined as an average pain intensity of 7 or higher on a 0–10 scale. Generalized pain was defined as pain occurring in 4 of 5 body regions according to the 2016 fibromyalgia spatial criteria.

The prevalence of any bodily pain increased progressively across the life span, from around 30% at age 5 to 70% among people ages 100 and older. All three pain measures were consistently higher in women than men. 

While any bodily pain increased with age, high intensity and generalized pain peaked in mid-to-late life before declining.

High intensity pain peaked around age 50, while generalized pain peaked around age 70.

These different patterns could help identify when prevention and pain management efforts may be most beneficial across the life span.

When to speak with your doctor about pain

Vernon Williams, MD, a sports neurologist and founding director of the Center for Sports Neurology and Pain Medicine at Cedars-Sinai Orthopedics and Sports Medicine in Los Angeles, who wasn’t involved in the study, told Healthline there are several signs that pain should be evaluated by a doctor.

“People shouldn’t feel that they simply have to live with pain. I especially recommend talking with a doctor when pain persists, interferes with sleep or daily activities, limits exercise or function, or is accompanied by symptoms such as numbness, tingling, or weakness,” Williams said.

“Persistent pain deserves an evaluation to identify the underlying cause and develop an appropriate treatment plan.”

Smoking, obesity, and lower income linked to higher pain burden

The researchers found that smoking, obesity, and lower household income together accounted for 18.3% of the global pain burden across all body sites.

People who currently smoked had a modest but consistently higher prevalence of pain across body sites from adolescence through age 65. The strongest association was seen with facial pain.

Obesity was increasingly associated with pain beginning in early adulthood, particularly in weight-bearing joints such as the knees, hips, feet, and ankles.

Lower household income was also associated with a higher prevalence of pain across all body sites beginning in early adulthood. The strongest association was seen with chest pain.

People with the lowest household incomes were more than twice as likely to report chest pain as those with the highest incomes. This difference was greatest in midlife and narrowed at older ages.

Williams noted that medication isn’t the only option for managing pain.

“Regular, appropriately paced exercise can help change how the brain experiences pain, while mindfulness, breathing exercises, adequate sleep, and other behavioral strategies can help the nervous system better regulate the pain response,” he said. “When medication is needed, I encourage patients to discuss non-opioid and non-pharmacologic options with their doctor as part of a comprehensive approach.”

Anand added that, “The best strategy remains prevention and preservation of function: maintaining a healthy weight, strengthening the muscles that support the joints, and avoiding smoking can all help reduce the strain placed on the musculoskeletal system.”

#5028. Detached Nails More Than 4 Times Higher Among GLP-1 Users, Study Finds

  • GLP-1 drugs like Ozempic have been linked to unusual nail changes in a new study.
  • GLP-1 users reported nail detachment more than four times as often as nonusers, and nail discoloration affected 46% of GLP-1 users, compared with 17% of nonusers.
  • The differences held up among people who had lost weight, suggesting weight loss alone may not explain them.
  • A companion study also notes that hair loss is a common side effect of GLP-1s.
  • Experts share tips for preserving skin and hair health while taking GLP-1 medications.

GLP-1 users have reported various unusual side effects like “Ozempic feet,” “Ozempic butt,” and “Ozempic breasts.” These changes have been largely tied to rapid weight loss.

Now, fingernails have entered the conversation. Nails detaching from the nail bed or changing color may be more common among people taking GLP-1 drugs, according to a new study.

In the study, which researchers presented in October at the European Academy of Dermatology and Venereology (EADV) Congress 2026 in Vienna, people taking GLP-1 drugs such as semaglutide — Ozempic, Wegovy — and tirzepatide — Mounjaro, Zepbound — reported nail detachment and nail discoloration more often than people who didn’t take the drugs.

When the researchers counted only nail changes that started after treatment began, the link between GLP-1 use and nail detachment nearly disappeared, study author Charles Taïeb, MD, PhD, told Healthline.

“This is why we insist that a substantial part of the nail excess reflects the patients’ overall health rather than the drug itself, while a modest drug-specific effect remains plausible,” Taïeb said.

Earlier research has also linked GLP-1 use to hair loss. In the new study, the researchers explained why they examined nails, noting that nails and hair both grow from cells that divide quickly.

However, the researchers cautioned in a press release that the study can’t establish that the drugs caused the nail changes. Both the nail study and a companion hair study captured self-reported symptoms at a single point in time, a design that can identify associations only, they said.

Nail changes reported among GLP-1 users

For the study, researchers examined a representative sample of people in France, the United States, Brazil, and Mexico.

Participants included 575 people taking GLP-1 drugs and 1,329 people with type 2 diabetes, obesity, or both conditions, who had never taken GLP-1 drugs.

The average treatment duration was 18.5 months, and researchers tracked weight loss over three months.

They also looked separately at people who had lost weight, to help tell whether the drugs or the weight loss explained the changes. However, the abstract didn’t specify which GLP-1 drugs participants took.

The researchers have submitted the findings to peer-reviewed dermatology journals, where they are under review, Taïeb told Healthline. Here’s what they found.

Nail separation found in 39% of GLP-1 users

According to the American Academy of Dermatology (AAD), nail detachment, or onycholysis, occurs when a nail lifts, and the lifted area often appears white.

Taïeb told Healthline that the study asked about “nail detachment” because patients, not doctors, filled out the questionnaire.

Nail detachment was the strongest signal among the five nail changes the researchers asked about, Taïeb said.

According to the abstract, 39% of GLP-1 users reported nail separation or detachment (onycholysis), compared with 9% of nonusers.

Nail discoloration, other nail changes found in 17% of GLP-1 users

Nail discoloration is a change in a nail’s usual color, such as white, yellow, or greenish-black, according to the AAD.

According to the abstract, 46% of GLP-1 users reported discoloration, compared with 17% of nonusers. Ridges or grooves and fragile nails were also more common among GLP-1 users.

Mir B. Ali, MD, a bariatric surgeon and medical director of the MemorialCare Surgical Weight Loss Center at Orange Coast Medical Center in Los Angeles, said that while he prescribes GLP-1s, nail detachment hasn’t come up in his practice. Ali wasn’t involved in the research.

“I haven’t had anybody complain of that. It’s not something we really see,” he told Healthline. He said his patients more often mention brittle nails or minor changes in nail color.

Weight loss alone didn’t explain GLP-1 nail changes

Over a three-month period, 76% of GLP-1 users reported weight loss, compared with 47% of nonusers, according to the abstract.

Weight loss alone didn’t appear to explain the nail findings, the researchers said in the press release. The differences between users and nonusers held up when they looked only at people who had lost weight.

The researchers also cautioned in the press release that the study can’t show the drugs caused the changes. They noted that about half of GLP-1 users had nail problems before starting treatment.

Adjusting for skin conditions and nutritional deficiencies weakened some of the associations, they said, though nail detachment and discoloration stayed about two to three times as common among users.

Those adjustments relied on conditions participants reported, including iron, vitamin B12, zinc, and vitamin D deficiencies, as well as psoriasis, eczema, and other skin conditions, Taïeb said.

“GLP-1 medications may affect the nails indirectly rather than directly,” said Tanya Evans, MD, a board certified dermatologist and medical director of the Skin Cancer Program at the Melanoma Clinic at MemorialCare Saddleback Medical Center in Laguna Hills, CA, who wasn’t involved in the research.

Because the drugs reduce appetite, some people may eat less protein, iron, and zinc, which can contribute to brittle nails, discoloration, or nail detachment, Evans told Healthline. Rapid weight loss may also temporarily disrupt nail growth, much as it can trigger hair shedding, she said.

Ali said the total amount of weight a person loses plays a bigger role in hair and nail changes than the rate at which they lose it.

GLP-1 hair loss and scalp changes

The U.S. labels for Wegovy and Zepbound list hair loss as a side effect reported in clinical trials.

The labels for the Ozempic, both injectable and pill form, as well as Mounjaro, list hair loss as a side effect, based on reports received after the drugs entered the market. Those reports don’t establish how often it happens or whether the drugs caused it.

As such, researchers have been examining GLP-1-related hair loss.

A companion study of the same participants found hair and scalp changes were also more common among GLP-1 users, according to that study’s abstract, also provided to Healthline:

  • Unusual hair loss: 50% of users versus 34% of nonusers
  • Loss of hair volume or density: 42% versus 19%
  • Hair loss that started after treatment began: 26% versus 12%

Scalp redness was about three times as common among users, and most users with hair loss also had scalp symptoms such as itching, redness, or scaling.

Scalp inflammation may play a role in hair changes among GLP-1 users, in addition to the shedding that often follows rapid weight loss, one of the study’s authors stated in the news release.

“This raises the possibility that inflammation may be contributing in some patients, rather than the problem being solely the classic temporary hair shedding associated with rapid weight loss,” Evans said.

Experts have told Healthline that hair loss on GLP-1 drugs is often temporary and tied to weight loss.

“Hair loss, we see commonly, not only in patients who are on GLP-1 medications, but also in our surgical weight loss patients,” Ali said.

“When you lose weight, especially in women, it releases hormones. You’re getting metabolic changes, and all these things can lead to hair loss and some changes in the nails.” Ali said he tells patients that “as the weight stabilizes at a healthy weight, the hormones stabilize and the hair tends to come back to where it was before.”

How to protect your hair and nails while using GLP-1s

Nail detachment and discoloration don’t appear as adverse reactions in the current U.S. prescribing information for Ozempic, Wegovy, Mounjaro, or Zepbound.

Ali said the best thing patients can do is get the right nutrition, including enough protein. He said he also advises patients to take a multivitamin along with B-complex, vitamin D, and calcium.

“I would not recommend stopping the GLP-1 medication simply because nail detachment occurs,” Evans said. She advised people to tell their doctor about any new changes in hair, scalp, or nails.

Treatment for nail detachment or discoloration depends on the cause, Evans said. A dermatologist can check for an infection, an inflammatory nail disorder, a nutritional problem, an injury, or another explanation, she said.

A detached nail recovers as a new nail grows out from its base, Evans said. That takes about 4 to 6 months for a fingernail and 9 to 12 months for a toenail.

Lastly, the AAD recommends having a board certified dermatologist examine any nail that’s lifting.

The AAD also recommends seeing a dermatologist if skin, hair, or nail side effects don’t improve, as they can help determine whether symptoms are due to the medication or another condition.

“The important message is don’t panic, but don’t ignore persistent nail changes either,” Evans said.

#5029. Switching from GLP-1 Injections to Wegovy Pill Shows Additional Weight Loss of 4.1%

  • People with overweight or obesity who switched from an injectable GLP-1 weight loss medication to the Wegovy (semaglutide) pill lost an average of 4.1% of their body weight over 3 months.
  • After 3 months, about 2 in 5 participants had lost at least 5% of their body weight.
  • The proportion of participants with obesity decreased by 21.6 percentage points, from 87.1% at the start of the study to 65.5% after 3 months.

People with overweight or obesity who switch from an injectable GLP-1 medication to the Wegovy (semaglutide) pill may continue to lose weight, according to findings from the OCTANE study presented at the 2026 Meeting of the European Association for the Study of Diabetes (EASD) in Milan, Italy. 

The study is among the first real-world analyses to examine weight loss and treatment experiences among people who switched from an injectable weight loss medication to oral semaglutide (Wegovy).

However, while real-world studies like this can provide useful insights into how treatments work in everyday life, these findings do not prove that switching to the Wegovy pill caused the weight loss.

The results have not yet been published in a peer-reviewed scientific journal.

Wegovy pill linked to 4.1% weight loss after switching

This was a retrospective cohort analysis evaluating weight change, treatment adherence, and patient satisfaction after people switched from injectable semaglutide (Wegovy) or tirzepatide (Zepbound) to oral semaglutide for weight management.

The study included a random sample of 194 adults with overweight or obesity and at least one obesity-related complication, such as hypertension, cardiovascular disease, obstructive sleep apnea, and dyslipidemia.

Participants had no history of diabetes or bariatric surgery and had an average baseline weight of 221.6 pounds (100.5 kg) and an average body mass index (BMI) of 34.5 kg/m2. 

Participants switched from an injectable GLP-1 medication to oral semaglutide between January 5 and February 7, 2026. There was no requirement for how long they had used an injectable medication or how much weight they had lost before switching.

Participants were required to use oral semaglutide regularly for 3 months and self-report their weight within 30 days of starting treatment and again at the end of the study period.

After 3 months of taking oral semaglutide, participants had lost an average of 4.1% of their body weight, or 8.8 pounds.

At the 3-month mark, 2 in 5 participants (40.7%) had lost 5% or more of their body weight.

The proportion of participants with a BMI of 30 or greater also decreased by 21.6 percentage points, from 87.1% at baseline to 65.5% after 3 months.

Most participants (82.4%) reported at least one treatment improvement. Around 69% reported better-fitting clothes, and around 51% reported eating healthier.

Betul Hatipoglu, MD, Medical Director, Diabetes & Metabolic Center at University Hospitals, Cleveland, OH, who wasn’t involved in the study, told Healthline that, “These findings are encouraging because they suggest that some people can continue losing weight after switching to oral treatment.” 

However, she also noted, “I would view this as promising early evidence supporting another treatment option, rather than proof that switching improves results.” 

What to consider when choosing Wegovy pill vs. injection

Mir Ali, MD, medical director of MemorialCare Surgical Weight Loss Center at Orange Coast Medical Center in Fountain Valley, CA, who wasn’t involved in the study, noted that because the oral medication is absorbed through the gastrointestinal tract, less of it reaches the bloodstream compared to the injectable form. 

However, oral semaglutide can still be effective for weight loss.

“While studies show that the injectable form is more efficacious, current findings demonstrate that the oral version is also effective for weight loss,” said Ali. 

Some people may prefer the oral form for ease of use and transportation because it does not require refrigeration. However, it must be taken in the morning on an empty stomach, followed by a 30-minute wait before eating, Ali told Healthline.

“Both formulations can cause gastrointestinal side effects, including nausea, vomiting, diarrhea, and constipation. Taking a pill does not eliminate those risks,” Hatipoglu said.

Hatipoglu also said factors to consider include how well your current treatment is working, side effects, your comfort with injections, your daily routine, cost, and insurance coverage.

She added that the decision should be made with a prescribing clinician, who can help plan the transition and monitor your response.

“Both options can be effective, so patients should discuss the risks and benefits of each with their provider. Ultimately, these medications are tools to support a healthier diet and lifestyle, and patients who make these fundamental changes achieve the best results,” said Ali.

#5030. Oral GLP-1 Orforglipron (Foundayo) Achieves Weight Loss for Women of All Ages

  • A new analysis of previous studies found that orforglipron promoted weight loss in women regardless of their menopausal status.
  • This is important since the decline in estrogen after menopause can make weight loss more difficult.
  • Menopausal women also tend to gain weight around their midsection, which is associated with health risks.
  • The fact that the new GLP-1 comes in a pill form offers a convenient option for many women.
  • However, doctors stress that it is also important to select the treatment that is the most effective for you.

A new GLP-1 pill called orforglipron (Foundayo) has been shown to help women lose weight, regardless of menopausal status, according to new research.

A group of researchers, including senior author Hunter Thomas Hoffman, PharmD, a clinical research scientist affiliated with the drug’s manufacturer, Eli Lilly, analyzed data from two prior trials, ATTAIN-1 and ATTAIN-2, to arrive at this conclusion.

These studies reveal that this medication works equally well for women who have not yet gone through menopause, those who are currently experiencing it, and those who are postmenopausal. 

The findings were presented at the Annual Meeting of the European Association for the Study of Diabetes (EASD), held in Milan, Italy, from September 28 to October 2.

This is important because menopause often causes weight gain due to lower estrogen levels, making weight loss more difficult for many women. 

Orforglipron offers a promising new option for managing weight in women with obesity or overweight, especially those with type 2 diabetes.

In April of this year, Eli Lilly’s Foundayo received FDA approval as a weight-loss drug, making it the second GLP-1 pill for weight loss to enter the U.S. market after Novo Nordisk’s Wegovy.

Examining menopause and weight loss with orforglipron

The ATTAIN-1 and ATTAIN-2 studies involved women who were either obese or overweight and who had at least one medical condition related to their weight. 

Obesity was defined as a body mass index (BMI) of 30 or higher, and overweight as a BMI of 27 or higher. 

The participants were divided into groups based on their menopausal status: premenopausal (before menopause), perimenopausal (during the menopause transition), and postmenopausal (after menopause). 

Each group included between 142 and 225 females who either received orforglipron or placebo, an inactive pill with none of the drug in it.

The ATTAIN-1 study focused on people who were overweight or obese but did not have diabetes. ATTAIN-2 included participants who had both overweight or obesity and type 2 diabetes. 

The purpose of analyzing these two groups separately was to understand how orforglipron works in people with different health conditions.

The researchers measured several outcomes over a period of 72 weeks. They examined changes in body weight, waist circumference (the distance around the waist), and the waist-to-height ratio, a measure used to assess body fat distribution. 

They also tracked how many women reached specific weight loss goals, such as losing at least 5%, 10%, 15%, or 20% of their body weight, compared to those who took the inactive pill.

By studying these factors, the researchers were able to determine not only whether orforglipron helped women lose weight but also how much weight loss was achieved and how it affected their body shape.

Up to 14% weight loss with orforglipron regardless of menopause status

The results showed that women who took orforglipron experienced significant weight loss regardless of their menopausal status. 

In the ATTAIN-1 and ATTAIN-2 studies, premenopausal, perimenopausal, and postmenopausal women all lost an average of up to 14% of their body weight. 

This means that the medication was effective for women at different life stages, including those experiencing menopause, when weight gain is often a challenge.

In addition to losing body weight, women in the orforglipron groups also saw meaningful reductions in waist circumference. Waist measurements decreased by up to 12.5 centimeters (about 5 inches) across all menopausal groups in both studies. 

This reduction is important because greater visceral fat is associated with higher risks of heart disease and diabetes.

The waist-to-height ratio also improved for many women taking orforglipron. More participants treated with the medication moved into lower waist-to-height ratio categories than those who took the placebo. 

This indicates a healthier distribution of body fat and suggests a lower risk of heart disease.

When looking at the number of women who reached specific weight loss milestones, orforglipron performed much better than the placebo. Up to 83% of women on orforglipron lost at least 5% of their body weight, while 30% of women on placebo reached that same level of weight loss. 

Losing 5% or more of body weight is often considered clinically meaningful, as it can lead to improvements in blood pressure, blood sugar, and cholesterol levels.

Overall, these findings suggest that orforglipron is a promising new medication for weight management in women with obesity or overweight, including those who also have type 2 diabetes. 

This can be especially helpful for those navigating the challenges of menopause while trying to manage their weight.

Oral GLP-1 offers convenient weight loss option for women

James J. Chao, MD, FACS, Chief Medical Officer and co-founder of VedaNu Wellness, wasn’t involved in the research, but said the significance of these findings for postmenopausal women.

“The 14% is what’s grabbing headlines,” he told Healthline, “but up to 12.5 cm from the waistline is what I’m interested in.”

According to Chao, as females enter postmenopause, they tend to store more fat around their midsection.

“This is the type of fat that can cause heart disease,” he said. “So a pill that can help a 38-year-old woman lose the same amount as a 62-year-old breaks that myth.”

Additionally, in contrast to most GLP-1 drugs, which are injectable, it’s a once-a-day pill. You can also take it any time, Chao explained, making it more convenient.

“No needle, no refrigerator, and up to 83% of participants lost at least 5%,” he said. “Sometimes it’s the simple things that make a difference.”

Luca Moretti, MD, a medical doctor at Sapienza University of Rome, who wasn’t involved in the studies, added that convenience isn’t the only thing to consider when deciding whether to use orforglipron.

Obesity management should begin with an individualized plan that includes nutrition, physical activity, and the management of other risk factors.

“When these measures are not sufficient, adding pharmacological treatment can be considered together with a physician,” he told Healthline.

Moretti further advised speaking with your physician about any potential side effects, your existing medical conditions, other medications you are taking, and your treatment preferences.

“So the most useful question to ask a doctor is not simply, ‘Is Foundayo more convenient?’ but rather: ‘Among the available treatments, which one offers the best balance of effectiveness, tolerability, and practicality for me?'” he said.

#5031. Increasing Ozempic Dose Linked to Lower Heart Risk Than Switching to Mounjaro

  • A real-world study found that people with type 2 diabetes who increased their Ozempic dose rather than switching to Mounjaro had a lower risk of major adverse cardiovascular events (MACE).
  • Those who increased their Ozempic dose to 2 mg had a 6% lower risk of MACE than those who switched to Mounjaro.
  • Because the study was observational, it showed an association and does not prove that increasing the Ozempic dose caused the lower cardiovascular risk.

Increasing the dose of Ozempic rather than switching to Mounjaro may be associated with a lower risk of major adverse cardiovascular events (MACE), according to a 2026 insurance claims analysis presented at the European Association for the Study of Diabetes (EASD) Annual Meeting 2026 in Milan, Italy.

GLP-1 medications like semaglutide (Ozempic) and tirzepatide (Mounjaro) have reshaped care for people with type 2 diabetes, but recent real-world evidence suggests these drugs may not offer identical heart protection.

It is important to keep in mind that this was a retrospective, observational study that showed an association, not causation.

More research is needed to fully compare the cardiovascular benefits of semaglutide versus tirzepatide.

Higher Ozempic dose linked to 6% lower risk of heart events

The COMPETE SWITCH Cardiovascular (CV) analysis used Komodo Health’s Healthcare Map, a large US healthcare claims database with linked laboratory results, to examine real-world data from 636,525 adults with type 2 diabetes.

The analysis focused on 185,705 people who increased their semaglutide dose and 23,104 who switched to tirzepatide.

All participants included in the analysis were already receiving 1 mg of semaglutide (Ozempic) weekly.

  • 67.2% remained on 1 mg of Ozempic
  • 29.2% escalated to 2 mg of Ozempic
  • 3.6% switched to tirzepatide (Mounjaro) within 1 year of starting the 1 mg Ozempic dose

At the 720-day follow-up: 

  • 57.4% remained on the 1 mg of Ozempic
  • 36.9% escalated to 2 mg of Ozempic
  • 5.7% switched to Mounjaro

The researchers found that people who increased their Ozempic dose to 2 mg had a 6% lower risk of MACE — including all-cause death, myocardial infarction (heart attack), and stroke — compared with those who switched to Mounjaro at doses up to 15 mg.

“This is an interesting study, but the findings may partly reflect medication adherence. When patients switch medications, it can take time to adjust the dose and find the right treatment level, which may temporarily affect how well their condition is controlled,” said Mir Ali, MD, medical director of MemorialCare Surgical Weight Loss Center at Orange Coast Medical Center in Fountain Valley, CA, who wasn’t involved in the study. 

He also noted that the study did not determine what might explain the difference in cardiovascular risk.

“It may be related to a combination of weight loss and direct effects on hormones involved in metabolism and appetite regulation,” Ali said. 

How do Ozempic and Mounjaro differ?

Semaglutide and tirzepatide are both GLP-1 medications. Ozempic (semaglutide) and Mounjaro (tirzepatide) are approved to help manage type 2 diabetes, while each medication also has a different brand approved for weight loss.

The semaglutide approved for weight loss in the U.S. is sold as Wegovy, and the tirzepatide approved for weight loss is sold as Zepbound. 

“Semaglutide and tirzepatide work in a similar way. While semaglutide is a direct GLP-1 receptor agonist, tirzepatide is a dual agonist of both the GIP and GLP-1 receptors,” Amit Blumfield, MD, with Manhattan Cardiology in NYC, who wasn’t involved in the study, told Healthline. 

“Because they work through somewhat different pathways, they can have different effects in individual patients,” added Ali. 

What to consider before switching from Ozempic to Mounjaro

While both semaglutide (Ozemipc) and tirzepatide (Mounjaro) work in similar ways, you may find that you tolerate one better than the other. 

Ali noted that you should discuss any potential benefits and drawbacks with your healthcare professional. 

“I would only recommend switching medications if there is an issue with tolerance of one or the other. If one medication is well-tolerated at increasingly higher doses and is effective in the desired weight-loss and blood sugar management, I wouldn’t necessarily switch,” added Blumfield. 

GLP-1s can have side effects, including: 

  • nausea and vomiting
  • diarrhea 
  • constipation
  • reactions at the injection site

These side effects are generally mild and may lessen over time. However, if side effects become difficult to tolerate, you should speak with your healthcare professional.

“GLP-1 medications can be highly effective for managing diabetes and promoting weight loss, but there is no one-size-fits-all treatment. The best medication depends on an individual’s health needs, goals, response to treatment, and potential side effects,” said Ali.

#5032. Can the BPC-157 ‘Wolverine’ Peptide Really Help With Pain and Recovery?

  • Proponents of BPC-157, the “Wolverine” peptide, tout injury recovery and pain relief despite limited human evidence to establish safety and effectiveness.
  • Until the potential for adverse reactions is better understood, particularly with long-term use, experts advise against unapproved peptides.
  • There are other proven treatment options for pain relief and recovery recommended by medical professionals.

Wellness seekers are increasingly turning to BPC-157, the wildly popular “Wolverine” peptide for injury recovery and pain relief, despite scant human evidence.

The Food and Drug Administration (FDA) advisory panel voted in July to ease restrictions on it and several other peptides, recommending the injectable substances be made eligible for pharmacy compounding.

But before restrictions were eased, preliminary research suggests a rise in the use of the Wolverine peptide in recent years.

A September 2026 preprint analyzed more than 15 million medical records and identified 1,039 people with documented BPC-157 use. 

The researchers found a roughly 33-fold increase in new users between 2020 and 2026. 

The results, which were based on clinical records, have not yet been published in a peer-reviewed scientific journal.

“The study found that BPC-157 use is largely hidden in clinical narrative rather than conventional prescription data,” said lead study author Venky Soundararajan, PhD, co-founder and Chief Scientific Officer of nference.

“The proportion of newly documented BPC-157 users who were male rose from 57% in 2020 to 70% in 2026, a statistically significant trend,” he told Healthline. 

“The user population for BPC-157 peptide is increasingly male in proportion and increasingly younger with each passing year.”

What is BPC-157, and why is it so popular?

BPC-157 is a synthetic 15-amino-acid peptide marketed and sold online for wellness. 

Health claims about it and other peptides have spread on social media and in fitness circles and wellness communities, with high-profile advocates like Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy Jr. touting their benefits.

Proponents of BPC-157 claim the injectable peptide promotes injury recovery, pain relief, and tissue healing. Some advocates claim that “stacking” the Wolverine peptide, particularly with TB-500, may enhance its effects.

The “Wolverine” peptide got its nickname from claims that it facilitates rapid healing, echoing the regenerative abilities of the fictional X-Men character of the same name.

This promise of pain relief and recovery may be what gives the Wolverine peptide its appeal.

Soundararajan’s study found that nearly 33% of patients reported using BPC-157 for pain relief. By comparison, just 4% of patients reported using BPC-157 for performance enhancement. 

Across the cohort of 1,039 patients, 27% reported improvements. 

“Pain relief was the dominant reported benefit, documented in 183 patients,” Soundarajan said. “Wound or tissue healing was reported in 38 patients and functional return in 34 patients.”

“Part of BPC-157’s appeal appears to be to patients who remain symptomatic after chronic, complex or previously treated musculoskeletal problems, signaling a clear clinical unmet need,” he said.

Bert Mandelbaum, MD, a sports medicine specialist, orthopedic surgeon, and co-director of the Regenerative Orthobiologic Center at Cedars-Sinai Orthopaedics and Sports Medicine in Los Angeles, said that everyone experiences pain, but warned about the risks of using the Wolverine peptide to treat it.

“There is zero indication that BPC-157 has any efficacy for pain,” he told Healthline. “There’s zero evidence that shows that it’s good for clinical healing, and we don’t really understand the safety profile of it.”

What does the research say about BPC-157?

Human evidence of the possible benefits and safety of the Wolverine peptide is limited. 

A 2025 systematic review of current BPC-157 literature found that it was dominated by preclinical research and low-grade human evidence. The review authors said there was insufficient clinical safety data to determine the peptide’s risks. 

A small 2021 retrospective study on the effects of injectable BPC-157 on knee pain suggests the peptide could help repair tissue and regrow cartilage. However, the study followed just 16 participants, lacked a control group, and did not assess impacts on function or quality of life. 

An even smaller 2025 intravenous safety pilot on BPC-157 use involved 2 participants. While no adverse effects were reported during the short study, the researchers acknowledged that larger studies are needed to confirm safety. 

What is BPC-157’s regulatory status?

The July 23–24 FDA advisory vote added BPC-157 to the Section 503A bulk drug substances list, allowing pharmacies to include it in compounded medications.

The FDA’s 8-6 vote, with one abstention, greenlit BPC-157 in both free-base and acetate forms.

The recommendation did not make BPC-157 an FDA-approved drug, nor is it a component of an approved product. The federal health agency has yet to make final regulatory decisions on peptides.

“RFK Jr. policies are hurting Americans,” Mandelbaum said. 

“This decision is ridiculous. It’s another step in the wrong direction. There’s no basis for any of this stuff.”

Is the ‘Wolverine’ peptide safe?

The safety profile of the Wolverine peptide for use in humans is largely unknown.

As such, BPC-157 is banned by the World Anti-Doping Agency as an unapproved substance for athletes both in and out of competition. 

The FDA notes that peptides like BPC-157 (free base) may be sensitive to product formulation, processing, environmental conditions, and impurities, which could degrade the products and reduce their effectiveness.

The health agency adds that it currently has limited safety information for how peptides like BPC-157 are administered. 

Soundararajan explained that his study was unable to estimate the incidence or overall safety of BPC-157 because adverse events were captured retrospectively from routine clinical notes and are likely underreported. 

“That said, the medical records examined contain both positive and negative patient experiences,” he said. 

“Mood, sleep, skin, injection-site, and gastrointestinal symptoms are concrete safety outcomes among the few examined patient records with available evidence that deserve prospective measurement and discussions by healthcare providers with their patients who report using BPC-157.”

Some experts warn of short-term risks like infection at the injection site, but the long-term effects are not known.

“We don’t have publications of infections yet, but there are anecdotes of this,” Mandelbaum said. 

“People are injecting their joints with this stuff, and you have no idea what it is. It’s not standardized. It’s not being regulated by the FDA, and I think people should recognize that. And although we haven’t proven it’s a safety hazard, we know it has no efficacy. So why do it? Why waste your money?”

What to know before using unapproved peptides

Experts urge anyone considering BPC-157 or another peptide treatment to discuss it with their doctor first.

You might ask for evidence supporting its use, information on its quality, and whether there are alternatives with established safety and effectiveness profiles.

Mandelbaum noted there are many scientifically proven and safe alternatives to consider.

“We have spent the last 20 years developing an arsenal of biologics from PRP to bone marrow aspirate to adipose tissue to understanding glucosamine and hyaluronic acid,” he said. “We’ve done an amazing job at studying these things. We’re getting to the point where we are understanding the complexities of biologics. It’s exciting, and it’s impactful.” 

Mandelbaum vehemently opposed the use of unapproved peptides like BPC-157.

He likened its rise in popularity to a “Ponzi scheme” promoted by “gym rats” and “internet influencers” who “leverage and exploit the gap between hope and knowledge.” Just because the FDA eased restrictions, he said, doesn’t make them any safer or more legitimate.

That said, any use of unapproved peptides should also be disclosed to a doctor. 

“A short, open and empathetic clinical conversation can turn invisible consumer use of products like BPC-157 into useful clinical information that can critically help inform current and future care for such patients that ensure the best possible outcomes,” Soundararajan said.

BPC-157 should not be combined with other experimental peptides until research determines the potential for adverse reactions or events. Long-term use of BPC-157 and other peptides marketed for wellness is not advised.

#5033. Cutting Back on Ultra-Processed Foods May Help Ease Depression Symptoms

  • A small study found that reducing ultra-processed food (UPF) intake was associated with improvements in depression symptoms.
  • The findings add to previous research linking high UPF consumption with an increased risk of depression.
  • Researchers are investigating how factors such as nutrition, inflammation, metabolic health, and the gut-brain axis may link UPFs with mood.

Emerging research has linked high consumption of ultra-processed foods (UPFs) with an increased risk of depression. Now, a small study published September 23 in JAMA Psychiatry suggests that reducing UPF intake may help ease depression symptoms.

There is no universally accepted definition of UPFs. However, a widely used classification system known as NOVA defines them as industrial formulations made primarily from substances extracted or derived from foods, often with additives not typically used in home cooking. Examples include soft drinks, packaged snacks, candy, sausages, hot dogs, and pastries. 

Previous research has linked UPFs to an increased risk of depression, anxiety, eating disorders, and other mental health conditions. In the new study, participants reduced their UPF intake by more than 85% and had significantly lower depression scores across three standard measures while following the low-UPF diet.

Lowering ultra-processed food intake may improve depression

This small study included 20 participants who:

  • were between 18 and 80 years old 
  • had a primary diagnosis of depression
  • had a Patient Health Questionnaire-9 (PHQ-9) score of at least 5
  • consumed high amounts of UPFs
  • had at least one self-reported metabolic abnormality, such as overweight or obesity, high blood pressure, high cholesterol or triglycerides, or insulin resistance

The participants also had to agree not to change medications, therapy, exercise, or sleep during the study. 

Of the 20 participants enrolled in the study, 19 completed it. The average age was 44. Participants followed both their usual diet and a low-UPF diet during separate phases of the study, with the order randomly assigned.

Participants generally found the low-UPF diet acceptable and manageable. They also had lower depression and anxiety scores during the low-UPF diet phase compared with their usual diet.

On one commonly used depression measure, the PHQ-9, average scores declined from 8.68 during the usual diet phase to 5.53 during the low-UPF phase. 

The study had several limitations, including its small sample size, reliance on self-reported dietary data, and uncertainty about which aspects of reducing UPFs may have contributed to the changes in symptoms.

The researchers also said that larger studies are needed to determine whether reducing UPF intake can improve depression symptoms.

Michelle Routhenstein, Preventive Cardiology Dietitian at Entirely Nourished, who wasn’t involved in the study, agreed, noting that more research is needed to understand whether reducing UPFs directly improves depression symptoms or whether other aspects of the overall diet may be contributing to the observed benefit.

Dara Houp, PsyD, a licensed clinical psychologist with Pathways Psychological Services in NYC, who wasn’t involved in the study, told Healthline that it’s important to distinguish ultra-processed foods from processed foods more broadly.

Routhenstein agreed that the distinction is important. “Processing is not inherently negative. Many nutritious foods are processed in some way.” 

“I would not encourage people to simply label a food as ‘processed’ or ‘unprocessed’ and make decisions based on that label alone. I prefer to look at the overall dietary pattern, the nutritional value of the food, and what role that food plays in the diet. A packaged food can still provide meaningful nutrients and be part of an overall nutritious eating pattern,” she said.

How can ultra-processed foods affect your mood?

There are various proposed mechanisms for how UPFs may affect mood.

For example, UPFs are often low in fiber, vitamins, and minerals, which can play important roles in brain health.

Houp noted that some ingredients found in UPFs, including artificial sweeteners such as aspartame, may affect the gut microbiome.

“Our gut is a primary location for producing neurotransmitters, responsible for manufacturing the vast majority – upward of 90% – of our body’s serotonin. When the gut microbiome is disrupted, it directly interferes with healthy neurotransmitter production,” she said. 

Houp added that diets high in UPFs may be lower in antioxidant-rich foods, such as fruits and vegetables, which could contribute to oxidative stress. Research suggests that people with depression may have lower antioxidant levels and higher markers of oxidative damage.

Routhenstein also noted that researchers are investigating how UPF consumption may influence inflammation, metabolic health, and the gut-brain axis, all of which may have implications for mood.

What are ways to reduce ultra-processed food intake?

Reducing your UPF intake can start with small, sustainable changes.

Routhenstein suggested starting by looking at the foods you eat most often rather than trying to overhaul your kitchen.

“Identify one or two foods that are frequent sources of UPFs and consider what you could realistically replace them with. For example, you might swap a daily sugary drink for water or sparkling water, or choose fruit, nuts, yogurt, or another minimally processed option in place of a packaged snack,” she said. 

Other ways you can help reduce UPFs include: 

  • Cook at home, using whole ingredients when possible.
  • When possible, choose foods with fewer highly processed ingredients.
  • Build meals around whole foods, like fruits, vegetables, grains, and lean proteins.

Routhenstein added that you don’t have to eliminate convenience from your diet. Frozen vegetables, canned beans, canned fish, oats, plain yogurt, tofu, and other minimally processed foods can make healthy eating much more practical.

“The goal is not to eat a perfectly unprocessed diet. It is to gradually shift the overall pattern toward more nutrient-dense foods that provide fiber, protein, healthy fats, vitamins, and minerals,” she said. 

#5034. FDA Issues Class II Blood Pressure Medication Recall, Over 13,000 Bottles Affected

  • 13,567 bottles of 25 mg chlorthalidone tablets were recalled after failing lab tests, according to the FDA.
  • It’s the manufacturer’s second recall of the tablets this year, all with the same expiration date.
  • Together, the two Class II recalls cover 25,027 bottles, according to FDA enforcement reports.

Some people taking a common blood pressure medication may have pills that don’t dissolve the way they should.

More than 13,500 bottles of 25 mg chlorthalidone tablets are under a nationwide recall after failing lab tests, according to the Food and Drug Administration (FDA).

Inventia Healthcare Limited voluntarily recalled the tablets on September 3, 2026, according to the FDA’s enforcement report.

The report lists the reason for the recall as “Failed Dissolution Specifications.” That means the tablets failed a test that measures how the medicine is released from the pill, according to the FDA.

“Chlorthalidone is a diuretic, or ‘water pill,’ commonly used to treat high blood pressure and reduce excess fluid retention in the body,” Cheng-Han Chen, MD, a board certified interventional cardiologist at MemorialCare Saddleback Medical Center in Laguna Hills, CA, told Healthline.

This is the second time this year Inventia has recalled its 25 mg chlorthalidone tablets.

The first recall was initiated on June 5 and covered 11,460 bottles. Both were for the same reason: failed dissolution specifications, according to FDA enforcement reports.

Together, the June and September recalls cover 25,027 bottles.

Which chlorthalidone tablets are being recalled?

The FDA classified both recalls as Class II.

The two recalls cover three batches of the chlorthalidone tablets, USP, 25 mg, according to the FDA enforcement reports. All three expire in April 2027.

The 25 mg tablets are light yellow, round and flat, with “103” stamped on one side and nothing on the other, according to the drug label.

September 2026 recall (recall number D-0852-2026)

Recall initiated on September 3, 2026; 13,567 bottles affected.

  • Batch: RISA24002
    • 100-tablet bottles
    • NDC 64980-599-01

June 2026 recall (recall number D-0610-2026)

Recall initiated on June 5, 2026; 11,460 bottles affected.

  • Batch RISA24001
    • 100-tablet bottles
    • NDC 64980-599-01
  • Batch RISB24002:
    • 1,000-tablet bottles
    • NDC 64980-599-10

How to check if your blood pressure medication is recalled

Patients whose supply is part of the recall “should contact their pharmacy to discuss obtaining a replacement and ask whether they should continue taking the medication in the meantime,” Chen said.

For Class II recalls, people can generally keep taking the medicine unless the recalling company gives other instructions, according to the FDA.

In some cases, stopping a medication may be more harmful than continuing to take the recalled medicine, the agency said.

If you’re concerned that your medication has been recalled, here’s what you can do:

  • Call the pharmacy where you last refilled your prescription.
  • Ask for the manufacturer, NDC, and batch number on file.
  • Compare those against the recall lists above. 

Are there health risks associated with a Class II recall?

A Class II recall means using the drug may cause temporary health problems, but the chance of serious harm is unlikely, according to the FDA.

“The voluntary recalls cited are classified as Class II, at the retail level,” an FDA Spokesperson told Healthline.

A retail-level recall means the company is “removing the product from retail stores and pharmacy shelves,” Ileana Elder, a branch chief in the FDA’s Office of Compliance, said in an FDA podcast. When a recall doesn’t extend to consumers, stopping a medication unnecessarily can be more harmful, and patients should contact their pharmacy with questions, Elder said.

Healthline reached out to the companies for more information about the September recall, but had not heard back by the time of publication. The FDA referred further questions to the companies

However, based on a preliminary assessment of the batches recalled in June, Rising Pharma Holdings, a distributor of one of the recalled lots, stated that “no impact on patient health and safety is anticipated.”

“If a medication does not dissolve at the expected rate, it may not be absorbed by the body as intended,” Chen said. “This can reduce the amount of medication that reaches the bloodstream and potentially decrease its effectiveness.”

What do ‘failed dissolution specifications’ mean for medications?

In a dissolution test, a lab places a tablet in a controlled liquid medium to determine whether and how quickly the active ingredient is released, according to FDA guidance on dissolution testing.

A failed result means the batch didn’t meet the release standard set for that product, according to FDA guidance on drug quality standards.

Manufacturers propose and justify these standards, and regulators approve them as part of drug approval, according to the same guidance.

In the United States, the official standards stay the same from the time a drug is released until it expires, the guidance said.

The September FDA enforcement report doesn’t say how the recalled tablets failed. A notice from Rising Pharma Holdings about the June recall provides more details.

Rising found two problems at the 18-month mark of ongoing quality testing of its 25 mg tablets, the company said in a notice posted by the California State Board of Pharmacy:

  • The tablets contained less chlorthalidone than the approved minimum: 91.5% of the labeled amount, when the minimum is 92%.
  • On average, 78% of the medication dissolved during the test. The standard requires at least 80%.

What is chlorthalidone used for?

According to the drug label, chlorthalidone treats:

  • High blood pressure (hypertension) — alone or added to other blood pressure medications, or in more severe cases.
  • Swelling linked to heart failure, liver scarring,  and corticosteroid or estrogen therapy, as an add-on treatment.
  • Swelling from some kidney conditions.

The label recommends that most people with high blood pressure start with one 25 mg dose per day. That’s the strength involved in both 2026 recalls.

#5035. Letter From the Editor-in-Chief: Is Worrying About Sleep Keeping Us Awake?

  • A new study analyzing wrist tracker data from 95,000 people found that higher amounts of REM and deep sleep were linked to a lower risk of 83 diseases combined, including dementia, hypertension, and Parkinson’s.
  • Nearly 70% of Americans say anxiety disrupts their sleep – and for some, the sleep trackers meant to help may be making it worse, a phenomenon researchers call orthosomnia.
  • Healthline’s Editor-in-Chief Robin Hough shares why he’s taking the data, leaving the anxiety, and still setting his alarm for a very insistent 8-year-old.

As a busy, sleep-deprived, middle-aged father of two young kids, including an irrepressible 8 year old ball of energy who is, without fail, up and ready to take on the world at 6 o’clock every morning (usually with a steady stream of questions about dinosaurs, the natural world and the origins of the universe in his back pocket), do I really need another set of headlines informing me that not only am I falling short in getting the requisite 40 winks a night but I’m also at risk of 83 diseases into the bargain? 

Apparently, I do. 

Another day, another headline-grabbing sleep study lands, this time linking a greater amount of sleep – specifically sleep of the rapid eye movement (REM) variety, also known as dream sleep – to a lower risk of dozens of diseases.

What a 95,000-Person Study Reveals About Sleep

The recent study published in PLOS Medicine found that REM sleep is linked to a lower risk of 83 diseases, including dementia, Parkinson’s, and multiple sclerosis (MS). As an added bonus, the study also found that a higher amount of deep sleep was associated with a lower risk of seven health conditions, including depression and type 2 diabetes. 

So far, so alarming for a self-confessed “bad sleeper”. 

Yet also fairly unsurprising. The accepted science, combined with anecdotal experience, has long told us that the right amount of good quality sleep corresponds directly with improved short and long-term health outcomes, both mental and physical. Evidence suggests that 7-9 hours of sleep per night is typically the sweet spot for a healthier life. Where this particular study differs from those that have come before is that scientists used wrist trackers to gather much more detailed information over a multi-year timeline about participants’ sleep stages, including irregular sleep patterns and variations in sleep duration. The findings were drawn from real-world data from more than 95,000 participants in the UK Biobank database.

Orthosomnia: When Tracking Your Sleep Causes Anxiety

Now, as the aforementioned and self-proclaimed VBP (very busy person – if you know, you know), who works long hours across UK and US timezones for the day job whilst serving as a sub-par encyclopedia for the real boss (the aforementioned 8 year old) outside of work, the general demands and pressures of daily life are more than sufficient to make consistently good sleep something that is all too often out of reach. 

I know this to be true because the wearable I use reminds me of the fact with cold, hard data on a daily, if not hourly, basis. One of the first things my wife and I do each morning (after fending off existential questions from a third grader) is hawkishly plug into our Oura app to trade sleep scores and discuss what did or didn’t contribute to our invariably bad or, very rarely, good night’s sleep. Deep sleep, REM sleep, resting heart rate, heart rate variability, latency, even sleep debt – a laundry list of data points that we pore over in a futile attempt to make our sleep consistency optimal whilst living a life that doesn’t readily allow for it. Scientists have even given this phenomenon a name – Orthosomnia – where the anxiety of analyzing and perfecting sleep scores actually disrupts rest and intensifies poor sleep, and in the most extreme cases, insomnia.

It turns out we’re not alone. Results of a Gallup poll from 2024 – with the rather on-the-nose headline “Americans sleeping less, more stressed” – showed Americans report averaging 6.5 hours of sleep per night, comfortably below the recommended baseline of 7+ hours. Perhaps more tellingly, in a survey from 2024, the American Academy of Sleep Medicine found that nearly 70% of Americans report losing sleep due to anxiety.

Which brings me back to my original point. Anxiety-induced stress makes for the perfect bedfellow (pun intended) for a bad night’s sleep. The kind of anxiety-induced stress that can be both caused and amplified by obsessing over wearables data and a steady torrent of sleep-related content that aims to scaremonger rather than inform. 

Why Empathy Matters in Health Information – and in Sleep

In fairness to the UK Biobank study, it is unequivocally important and unique research steeped in actionable real-world data and peer-reviewed medical science. And yet we live in a world increasingly awash with AI-generated content, sporting sensationalist headlines and powered by algorithms designed with an inherent imbalance to engage rather than empower. In that environment, gold-standard scientific work like this is all too often reduced to the lowest common denominator. 

If you’ll indulge a minor tooting of our own horn here, this is precisely the inverse of how the team of wonderfully talented editorial misfits at Healthline Media thinks about and creates health information. We take research-backed science and our embedded clinical rigor and apply it to every aspect of our work. But we never lose sight of the fact that behind every study, there’s someone lying awake in the early hours running the math on their own risk factors, and that what they need in that moment is recognition that we are all at different stages on that journey, and a helping hand along the path to better health. 

Life isn’t linear, sleep isn’t linear, and health is certainly not linear. Some nights I’ll get my seven hours. Plenty of others I won’t. And that is ok. Because either way, my alarm clock has a favorite dinosaur and a nonnegotiable 6 a.m. start time, and no peer-reviewed study or scary headline is going to change that. So I’ll take the data and the empathy, leave the anxiety, and see you all again at dawn.

If there’s something keeping you awake at night, or you just want to learn more about the wonderful world of sleep, we have a wealth of science-backed information at our dedicated Sleep Health hub. There you can also find Healthline’s Better Sleep Challenge – a three-week series designed to help you sleep better, feel more rested, and wake up ready to thrive each day.

Robin Hough is the Editor-in-Chief of Healthline, and in addition to this regular Letter from the Editor column, he can be found every other Saturday in our Healthline Daily newsletter. He’ll soon be launching a dedicated Letter from the Editor newsletter coming to an inbox near you later in the fall.

#5036. Low-Energy Diet Plus Structured Exercise Reverses Early Onset Diabetes

  • A new study found that a low-energy diet and exercise reversed early onset type 2 diabetes in half of the participants.
  • This is important because early onset diabetes can lead to earlier health decline and other complications.
  • Experts suggest eating a good-quality diet and reducing calories by about 500–700 daily to lose weight.
  • Aim for 150 minutes of moderate-intensity physical activity each week.

A new study has found that a combination of a low-energy diet and structured exercise reversed early onset type 2 diabetes in about half of participants.

The research focuses on adults under 45 years old who developed type 2 diabetes early in life. The authors presented their findings at the European Association for the Study of Diabetes (EASD) Annual Meeting, held September 28 to October 2. The study was published on October 1 in The Lancet Regional Health Americas.

Type 2 diabetes is a condition in which the body becomes less able to respond to insulin or has difficulty producing it, leading to elevated blood sugar and low energy. If untreated or not properly managed, it can lead to serious complications such as vision problems, kidney disease, and nerve damage.

These findings offer hope for better treatment options for people with this condition, which the authors say is known for causing faster health decline when compared to later-onset diabetes.

Effects of a low-energy diet plus exercise on early onset diabetes

The study was conducted at three centers in England and Canada, linked with the University of Leicester, McGill University, and the University of Alberta. 

Researchers selected adults ages 18 to 45 who had early onset type 2 diabetes and obesity but were not using insulin therapy. 

A total of 96 participants were randomly divided into two groups: one received usual care, and the other took part in a program combining a low-energy diet with supervised and independent exercise.

The intervention group followed a strict diet of 800-900 calories per day, consisting of 30% protein, 50% carbohydrates, and 20% fat. Participants were encouraged to drink at least 2 liters of calorie-free fluids daily and were given a fiber-based laxative if needed. 

For the first two weeks, the diet was entirely based on meal replacements (Optifast). From week 3 to week 12, the same calorie limit was maintained, with greater food variety, including one meal per day made from regular foods such as protein sources, one portion of fruit, and two portions of non-starchy vegetables.

The exercise program involved supervised aerobic and resistance training twice a week and one session of independent exercise per week during the first 12 weeks. After this, from weeks 13 to 24, participants followed a weight-maintenance diet and exercised independently.
