Participants stopped all glucose– and blood pressure-lowering medications at the start of the diet unless they had a specific kidney condition called albuminuria (high levels of a protein called albumin in the urine).

The main goal of the study was to determine how many participants achieved remission of their diabetes after 24 weeks. Remission was defined as having a blood sugar level below the diabetes threshold (measured by glycated hemoglobin or HbA1c less than 6.5%) and not taking any diabetes medication for at least 12 weeks. 

The study also examined secondary outcomes such as fitness levels, body composition, and heart health.

Diet-exercise combo led to remission in 54% of participants

After 24 weeks, 54% of participants in the diet and exercise group achieved remission, compared with only 4% in the usual care group.

They also lost weight. The diet and exercise group lost an average of 8.4 kilograms (about 18.5 pounds), while the usual care group lost only 1.2 kilograms (about 2.6 pounds). 

After adjusting for various factors, the diet-and-exercise group lost about 7.5 kilograms more than the control group. 

Additionally, most of this weight loss was fat, with about 6.7 kilograms of fat lost at 24 weeks, which the researchers note is important for improving diabetes.

According to the researchers, the intervention helped preserve muscle mass, which is usually lost during weight loss. This is significant because maintaining muscle is important for long-term health, strength, and metabolism.

They further highlighted that early onset type 2 diabetes can cause serious health problems at a young age. 

However, their study shows that a combined approach of diet and exercise can not only help people lose weight and improve health but can also lead to real remission of diabetes, without needing medication. 

This is important because early onset type 2 diabetes usually worsens quickly and is harder to manage than diabetes that starts later in life.

The researchers additionally believe their approach could be especially helpful for younger adults who may be planning to have a child, as managing diabetes early is critical for health during and after pregnancy.

The study’s success in a multi-ethnic population also adds to its significance, showing the program could work for diverse groups. 

The researchers noted that most people in the diet-and-exercise group experienced side effects early in the program, including constipation, headaches, dizziness, and fatigue. However, these were mostly mild and resolved over time.

Managing your health with type 2 diabetes

Avery Zenker, a registered dietitian and medical writer, explained the importance of this work. “We have evidence that type 2 diabetes doesn’t have to be an inevitably progressive disease,” Zenker told Healthline.

“This can give hope for people with diabetes and help empower them to make impactful lifestyle changes.”

Zenker, who was not involved in the study, added that type 2 diabetes remission is not only possible but more likely in younger people who tend to have a shorter disease duration and a less complicated health history.

Jennifer Pallian, BSc, a registered dietitian and founder of Foodess, who wasn’t involved in the research, said that you can put these findings into action right now.

First, you can start a lower-calorie diet. “A reasonable target used in the research was to reduce daily calorie intake by about 500–750 calories, with the goal of losing at least 5% of starting body weight,” she told Healthline.

Zenker noted there are other factors to consider besides the number of calories. “There’s a big difference between a low calorie diet that’s predominantly whole foods compared to one that’s mostly ultra-processed and nutrient-poor,” she said.

“It’s important to consider other recommendations like whole foods, high fiber, and reduced intake of ultra-processed foods.”

Along with making dietary changes, Pallian advised working toward getting at least 150 minutes of moderate-intensity activity per week. “It also helps to make the plan measurable,” she said. “Set specific nutrition and activity goals, keep track of what and how much you eat, and record your physical activity too.”

Pallian suggested taking note of situations where it’s harder to stay on track so you can spot patterns and make adjustments.

Zenker further noted the importance of starting small and not feeling as if going to the gym is the only way to get exercise.

“Some simple ways one might start to increase daily activity could include adding a short body weight routine in the morning, taking a short walk after a meal, or dancing to music at home,” she said. “Even activities like yard work or cleaning the house can count as physical activity.”

Finally, Pallian advised regular visits with a healthcare professional to monitor blood sugar and blood pressure. These values may improve with weight loss, which may require your doctor to adjust your medication regimen.

#5037. Wall Squats, Planks Once a Week May Help Lower Your Blood Pressure

  • Just one or two isometric exercise sessions per week may help lower blood pressure in the short term, according to a new study.
  • Performing isometric exercises, such as wall squats and planks, three times a week may provide long-term benefits.
  • Experts say people beginning an isometric exercise program should start slowly and build up their routine over time.

Isometric exercises, even if done only occasionally, could help improve blood pressure, according to a new study.

Researchers report that study participants who did these types of exercises once or twice a week saw reductions in blood pressure over a short period.

They added that participants who engaged in isometric exercises three times a week maintained improvements in blood pressure over a longer period.

The researchers said that previous studies had indicated that isometric exercise training reduces blood pressure more than other forms of exercise and even some drug treatments.

However, they said previous research had not established how often isometric exercise training needs to be performed to maintain these health benefits.

Isometric exercises involve a person holding a pose for a few minutes at a time. Examples include wall squats and floor planks.

The researchers said their study provides important information for people who don’t have time to exercise daily.

The researchers wrote that the study “offers evidence that lower frequency [isometric exercise training] can help maintain short-term [blood pressure] reductions, providing a pragmatic option for individuals with limited time or adherence challenges.”

They published their findings on September 29 in BMJ Open Sport and Exercise Medicine.

Experts not involved in the research said it provides helpful guidance for people who want to improve their blood pressure.

“The main takeaway is that you don’t need to exercise every day — or even several times a week — to experience significant blood pressure benefits from isometric exercise,” said Michael Fredericson, MD, a professor of orthopaedic surgery at Stanford Health Care.

“We already knew that performing isometric training for even just a few weeks can lower blood pressure,” added Amanda Paluch, PhD, an associate professor of kinesiology at the University of Massachusetts Amherst and chair of the American Heart Association’s Physical Activity Committee. “This study adds to the research by looking at how much training is needed to maintain those gains.”

“It is encouraging to see measurable improvements in blood pressure with a relatively brief and infrequent exercise routine,” said Jennifer Wong, MD, a cardiologist and medical director of Non-Invasive Cardiology at MemorialCare Heart and Vascular Institute at Orange Coast Medical Center in California. “These findings suggest that even small, consistent efforts can make a meaningful difference.”

Isometric exercise helps lower blood pressure

The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) estimates that nearly half of adults in the United States have high blood pressure. That’s about 120 million people.

The condition, also known as hypertension, increases the risk of heart disease and stroke.

High blood pressure occurs when the force of blood pushing through blood vessels is consistently too high.

Blood pressure readings consist of two numbers. One is the systolic pressure (top number), which measures pressure when your heart beats. The second is diastolic pressure (the bottom number), which measures the pressure between beats.

Treatment usually involves medications such as beta-blockers and ACE inhibitors, as well as lifestyle factors such as a healthy diet and exercise.

In their study, the researchers aimed to determine how much isometric exercise was needed to lower and maintain blood pressure.

The researchers recruited 100 adults with an average age of 37. Subjects included male and female participants with normal-to-high-normal systolic blood pressure (120–140 mm Hg) who engaged in less than 150 minutes per week of moderate physical activity.

The participants also had no injuries or illnesses, were not taking and had not previously taken blood pressure medication, were not smokers, and did not consume more than 14 units of alcohol per week.

The participants were divided into five groups. One group served as a control and did no isometric exercise, while the other four groups followed the same four-week home-based lower-body isometric training program.

The regimen consisted of four sessions of isometric wall squat exercise, separated by two minutes of seated rest, performed three times a week.

During the next four weeks, the participants were split into four training schedules.

One group continued to exercise three times a week. A second group exercised twice a week, while a third group exercised once a week. The remaining group stopped the exercises entirely.

Blood pressure, heart rate, and other cardiac measurements were taken at the start of the study and after four weeks and eight weeks.

The researchers reported that the four groups who completed the initial four-week exercise routine showed significant reductions in systolic and diastolic blood pressure compared to the control group.

Over the next four weeks, the researchers found no statistically significant difference in diastolic blood pressure among the groups.

However, the researchers reported that participants who continued isometric exercise training three times a week achieved the greatest reduction in systolic blood pressure compared with baseline. That group also maintained improvements in other cardiac measures.

The group that had stopped doing isometric exercises after the initial four-week period showed a reversal of their blood pressure improvements.

The researchers wrote that the study “demonstrates that while lower frequency [isometric exercise training] may be sufficient to maintain [blood pressure] reductions over the short term, higher frequencies are required to preserve these benefits and the associated vascular and cardiac autonomic adaptations.”

The researchers noted that the eight-week study period was limited and that further research is needed. They also acknowledged that the participants were from a relatively small cross-section of people.

Why isometric exercise may benefit hypertension

There are several ways isometric exercises may help lower blood pressure.

“Repeat isometric exercises (contracting a muscle and holding a position) seem to change how the blood vessels regulate pressure between workouts,” said Kevin Shah, MD, a cardiologist and program director of Heart Failure Outreach at MemorialCare Heart & Vascular Institute at Long Beach Medical Center in California.

“Blood pressure comes down to how hard the heart pumps and how much resistance the blood meets in the vessels,” added Paluch.

“Research suggests isometric training works mainly on that second part. It helps your blood vessels relax and open up, so blood flows through more easily.”

“Isometric exercise seems to promote cardiovascular adaptations that can lower blood pressure,” explained Fredericson. “Over time, this training can enhance the cardiovascular system’s ability to regulate blood pressure.”

Getting started with isometric exercise

Experts interviewed by Healthline say that the wall squats used in the study are one of the most beneficial isometric exercises.

They also recommend static leg extensions, handgrip exercises, planks, and exercises that involve sustained muscle contraction.

“Start slowly, with manageable goals,” Wong said. “Even one or two short sessions per week can be a starting point,” she said. “Begin with brief holds, allow adequate rest between sets and sessions, and gradually build up as strength and comfort improve. The goal is to establish a routine one can maintain consistently.”

Paluch recommended starting small and keeping it simple. She says 10 to 20 minutes per session is sufficient.

She suggested four 2-minute holds of a wall sit or handgrip squeeze with 2 minutes of rest between each hold. Those can be done two to three days a week.

“The message is that consistency matters,” she said. “If you’re able to maintain three times a week, that appears to be the best way to maintain the gain, but it’s not an all-or-nothing situation.”

“You don’t have to maintain the initial pace forever to get something out of the training, especially when life gets in the way and adherence or time becomes a challenge,” Paluch added. “Even getting in one day a week is better than nothing.”

For those just getting started with exercise, Shah recommended starting with a short walk before adding isometric exercise.

“A 10-minute walk on most days is a manageable first step,” Shah said. “Gradually increase the length of walks and then add strength training. Consistency matters more than an exhaustive routine that is not sustainable. Remember: Progress and not perfection.”

Fredericson advised starting slowly and building from there.

“For someone completely inactive, even 5 to 10 minutes of movement per day is a meaningful starting point,” he said. “Walking is an excellent option because it requires little to no equipment or preparation. From there, they can incorporate strength training two or three days a week.”

#5038. Insomnia Isn’t Just Exhausting — It May Raise Your Risk of Stroke, Dementia

  • A recent review has found that insomnia may be associated with an increased risk of stroke and hospitalization.
  • The researchers also found evidence of an association between insomnia and depression, suicidal behaviors, and dementia. 
  • These findings add to growing evidence that insomnia may be linked to health risks beyond poor sleep and impaired daytime functioning.

Insomnia may do more than leave you tired. A review published in Sleep Medicine Reviews on September 7 has linked it to a higher risk of stroke, depression, and hospitalization.

Insomnia is characterized by difficulty falling or staying asleep despite having an adequate opportunity to sleep and typically involves impaired daytime functioning.

“Sleep is such an important health factor when we think about overall well-being,” Joseph You, MD, cardiologist with Manhattan Cardiology in NYC, who wasn’t involved in the study, told Healthline.  

He noted that sleep deprivation and poor-quality sleep have long been linked to adverse cardiovascular events and other health problems.

Insomnia linked to 26% higher stroke risk

Insomnia is the most common sleep disorder in adults. Chronic insomnia affects approximately 22% of the general population.

“Chronic insomnia is generally characterized by ongoing sleep difficulty despite having an adequate opportunity to sleep, rather than simply having an occasional bad night,” noted Christopher Yi, MD, board certified vascular surgeon at MemorialCare Orange Coast Medical Center in Fountain Valley, CA, who wasn’t involved in the study. 

To examine the association between insomnia and stroke, the researchers identified nine studies involving more than 1.3 million people. Six were included in the meta-analysis, which found that insomnia was associated with a 26% higher risk of stroke.

It is important to keep in mind that association does not equal causation. However, several factors may help explain the link between insomnia and stroke.

Persistent sleep disruptions can trigger overactivation of the sympathetic nervous system. 

The sympathetic nervous system is part of the body’s involuntary nervous system that triggers your “fight or flight” response in stressful or dangerous situations.

“Being too wired can impair sleep, and impaired sleep, in turn, can make us too wired. A state of higher adrenaline and cortisol raises blood pressure, blood sugar, and increases inflammation, all of which have serious health effects,” said Alex Dimitriu, MD, double board certified in Psychiatry and Sleep Medicine, and founder of Menlo Park Psychiatry & Sleep Medicine, who wasn’t involved in the study. 

The review authors noted that these changes can not only raise blood pressure and heart rate but can also increase vascular tone while reducing heart rate variability. Continuous cortisol release can also promote hypertension, systemic inflammation, and disrupt glucose and lipid metabolism. 

Together, these changes may promote plaque formation and compromise vascular health, potentially contributing to a higher risk of stroke.

“I would emphasize that this study demonstrates an association and does not prove that insomnia directly causes stroke or other cardiovascular events,” cautioned Yi.

“There was significant variability among the studies, and factors such as underlying medical conditions and the use of sleep medications could influence the results. The authors themselves caution that an independent causal role for insomnia has not been established.”

Insomnia can increase the risk of hospitalization by 28%

The researchers also looked at five studies examining the association between insomnia and hospitalization. The studies included mostly older adults and ranged in size from 1,101 to 484,707 participants.

Three of the studies, involving 19,745 participants, were included in a meta-analysis. Researchers found that people with insomnia had a 28% higher likelihood of hospitalization for any reason.

The researchers noted that insomnia may be a key marker of poor health and may exacerbate the severity of underlying health conditions.

When to seek help for insomnia

Dimitriu said a good indication that it may be time to seek help is when sleep problems begin to affect your life, health, or happiness. If you are unable to sleep at least 6–7 hours when given the opportunity, or regularly feel tired or sleepy during the day, he recommends speaking with a healthcare professional.

You recommended seeking help as soon as you begin to suspect you may have insomnia. Doing so may also help identify other risk factors or lifestyle habits that could be affecting your sleep and overall health.

People with insomnia may be more likely to have depression

The researchers reviewed six meta-analyses examining the association between insomnia and depression and other mental health disorders.

Although most were rated as having low or critically low methodological quality, the findings consistently linked insomnia with a higher risk of depression.

According to the researchers, chronic insomnia can lead to daytime fatigue, attention issues, and reduced executive functioning. These factors can increase rumination, negative thinking, and maladaptive coping strategies, which are all risk factors for depression and anxiety.

Separately, two meta-analyses found that insomnia was associated with a higher risk of suicidal ideation, suicide attempts, and suicide death.

Sleep disturbances are among several risk factors for suicide, including independently of depression.

This isn’t to say that insomnia causes depression or suicidal behaviors, only that it may be one among various risk factors.

Insomnia linked to higher risk of dementia

The researchers reviewed five meta-analyses examining the association between insomnia and dementia. The analyses included between 5 and 39 studies and ranged from 36,005 to more than 3.1 million participants, with follow-up periods ranging from 2 to 40 years.

Overall, most of the analyses linked insomnia with a higher risk of dementia. Three also found that insomnia was associated with a higher risk of Alzheimer’s disease.

The researchers noted several possible explanations for the association. Sleep disturbances may contribute to inflammation and impaired clearance of substances such as amyloid-beta and tau from the brain, processes that could potentially contribute to neurodegeneration.

However, these findings show an association between insomnia and dementia and do not establish that insomnia causes dementia.

#5039. 8-Hour Time-Restricted Eating Window May Lead to Greater Weight Loss

  • An 8-hour eating window was linked to greater weight loss than a 12-hour window or no time restriction, but not significantly more than a 10-hour window.
  • Time-restricted eating (TRE) is a form of intermittent fasting that limits eating to a set number of hours each day without necessarily restricting calories.
  • Shorter eating windows were linked to modest weight loss, but researchers found limited effects on blood pressure, blood sugar, and cholesterol.

Limiting your eating to an 8-hour window may result in more weight loss than a 12-hour window, according to a new study published in the journal Nutrition & Diabetes on September 12.

Time-restricted eating (TRE) is a form of intermittent fasting that limits eating to a set daily window, such as 8, 10, or 12 hours, without necessarily changing what you eat.

The eating window is followed by fasting for the remaining hours. Common approaches include 16:8, which involves 16 hours of fasting and 8 hours of eating, and early TRE, which involves eating earlier in the day.

Shorter eating window linked to modest weight loss

The researchers used data from 124 adults across five TRE trials lasting between 3 and 6 months. At baseline, participants had an average eating window of about 15 hours and at least one component of metabolic syndrome.

Their food and beverage intake was tracked using an app. 

Participants were assigned either to a TRE group or to the control group. The control group participants maintained their habitual intake, received nutritional counseling, or were given guidance on healthy eating. 

Those in the TRE group limited their eating windows to 8, 10, or 12 hours, with no restrictions on dietary quality or energy intake. 

After 3–6 months, the researchers found that participants assigned to an 8-hour eating window lost about 3.7 pounds (1.7 kg) more than those assigned to a 12-hour window and about 5 pounds (2.3 kg) more than those in the control group.

However, there was no statistically significant difference in weight loss between the 8-hour and the 10-hour windows. The 8-hour window also showed no significant impact on blood pressure, glucose, or cholesterol.

“The findings are interesting and add to the evidence that shortening the daily eating window may help with weight loss, but I would be cautious about concluding that eight hours is the optimal window,” Dalia Perelman, MS, RDN, CDCES, research dietitian at Stanford Medicine, who wasn’t involved in the study, told Healthline. 

“One finding I find particularly interesting is that people who shortened their eating window more relative to their usual pattern tended to lose more weight. This suggests that the benefit may not be about everyone following exactly the same eight-hour window, but rather about meaningfully shortening one’s habitual eating period,” she added. 

How time-restricted eating may affect metabolic health

The researchers also found that the 10-hour eating window significantly reduced systolic blood pressure compared with the 12-hour window. There were no significant differences between the eating windows for glucose or lipids. 

However, a 2020 study found that a 10-hour eating window reduced weight, blood pressure, and cholesterol, among adults with metabolic syndrome who were already taking medication. 

“[These] effects are generally modest, and TRE should be viewed as one potential component of an overall lifestyle approach rather than a treatment on its own,” said Perelman.

“I think TRE is best viewed as one possible tool rather than a magic formula. When we eat appears to matter, but what and how much we eat still matter as well,” she added.

“A nutritious diet, limiting highly processed foods, avoiding eating late at night, and finding an eating pattern that is enjoyable and sustainable are probably more important than rigidly adhering to a specific number of hours.”

Is time-restricted eating right for everyone? 

Perelman noted that TRE may not be right for everyone, including: 

  • people who are pregnant or nursing
  • people with a history of eating disorders
  • people who are underweight or frail, are losing weight unintentionally, or have difficulty meeting their nutritional needs
  • children and adolescents who are still growing

Perelman also told Healthline that “Older adults at risk for muscle loss should also make sure they are consuming adequate calories and protein.” 

She added that people with diabetes who use insulin or medications that can cause hypoglycemia should discuss TRE with their healthcare professional because changing when they eat may require medication adjustments. 

What should people consider before trying time-restricted eating? 

As with any eating plan, time-restricted eating should be realistic and sustainable for your lifestyle.

Perelman noted that health is about more than what and when you eat. She said that sharing meals with family and friends, social connection, and enjoyment of food are important too. 

A rigid eating schedule that interferes with those things may not be beneficial overall.

“Rather than focusing on achieving a precise eight-hour window, I would prioritize a balanced, nutrient-rich diet low in highly processed foods and, when possible, finish eating earlier in the evening,” Perelman said.

#5041. Simone Biles Thought Her Itchy, Flaky Scalp Was Dandruff. It Was Eczema

  • Olympic gold medalist Simone Biles is opening up about living with eczema.
  • After experiencing itching and flaking on her skin and scalp, Biles saw a dermatologist who diagnosed her with atopic dermatitis and seborrheic dermatitis.
  • Biles hopes that sharing her experience will help others with eczema feel less ashamed and encourage them to seek care.

Olympic gold medalist Simone Biles is the most decorated gymnast in history. But the beloved icon is taking a break from talking about the balance beam and vault to discuss a personal health condition that she lives with alongside 31 million fellow Americans: eczema.

“A couple years ago, I started noticing flaking and itching on my scalp, especially on the crown of my head. And at first, I thought it was a lot of dandruff, and then I was worried it could be something contagious,” Biles told Healthline. “I’ve also dealt with patches of dry skin on my arm, but it comes and goes.”

Recently, she noticed her symptoms getting worse, especially when stressed from training.

“I would definitely be in the gym, finding myself waiting for bars or for beam and just scratching and scratching and [people] would be like, ‘Simone, can you stop scratching?’ and I’m like, ‘Oh, yeah, my bad, sorry,’” said Biles.

When her hairdresser noticed scaly patches on her scalp, Biles began searching online for answers and trying home remedies, including combs, shampoos, and oils.

“[Self-fixes] weren’t really helping, and I didn’t go to the dermatologist immediately because I was busy in the gym training,” said Biles. “I also felt ashamed and embarrassed, so I started speaking out about it at Paris Olympics.”

After making a TikTok video about her symptoms, she was inspired to see a dermatologist who diagnosed her with the skin conditions seborrheic dermatitis and atopic dermatitis (also known as eczema).

“[A] lot of what she is communicating really mirrors what we’re seeing in patients and what we know about these conditions,” Angela Lamb, MD, Bile’s dermatologist, told Healthline.

While eczema is a group of chronic inflammatory skin conditions, Lamb said stress can trigger flares, as well as extremes of weather, especially for those with atopic dermatitis.

“And then there’s just individual triggers, so patients really need to listen to their bodies, find out what moisturizer would work well for their skin, find out if you have seborrheic dermatitis on your scalp,” said Lamb. “[Know] what are the products that tend to be flaring it or what you do that makes it better or worse.”

How Biles found relief from her eczema symptoms

Biles says she found relief for her symptoms with Zoryve, a once-daily, steroid-free, topical prescription medication that comes in both foam and cream forms. Biles uses both forms: the foam for her scalp, and the cream for her skin.

“I feel like it fits in easily to my lifestyle…you can apply it to your scalp, and it’s really, really lightweight, and then for my eczema, it’s a cream, and it’s non-greasy,” said Biles.

In addition to helping her feel more comfortable, she said that receiving effective treatment eased the embarrassment she experienced around the appearance of her skin.

“[Going] on a lot of shoots and a lot of sets, [I was] definitely more self-conscious about my parts or if I was wearing braids — the flaking, what that looks like on camera — so I would just try to cover it up with oils and all of that stuff,” she said.

To share her journey, Biles partnered with Zoryve’s maker, Arcutis Biotherapeutics, to launch its Skin to Believe In campaign. The initiative aims to break the stigma around chronic skin conditions that can make people feel self-conscious, isolated, or embarrassed.

“I know people are going through similar things, thinking it’s dandruff or thinking it’s contagious,” Biles said. “[You] don’t have to silently suffer. Own it. Go see a trusted professional, and get the relief that you deserve.”

Because every medication has potential side effects, Lamb said it’s important to talk with your doctor about a medication’s potential risks and benefits.

“This one, the side effects are in a very low percent of patients, which can include some nausea, headache, as well as an allergic reaction, but you’ll discuss that with your physician, and in this case, dermatologists are the main ones writing for Zoryve,” said Lamb.

She said it’s encouraging that nonsteroidal medications exist as options for patients with skin conditions. She explained that Zoryve contains roflumilast, a PDE4 inhibitor that helps reduce inflammation involved in eczema.

“So I’m very excited [because] I think the landscape of how we’re treating many conditions is really changing as we understand the science and the core of what drives it better,” said Lamb. “I think that’s been the largest breakthrough in the last many years in healthcare…understanding root cause is better, which can lead us to better treatments.”

How Biles reduces stress to help manage her eczema

Because Biles believes stress can contribute to her eczema flares, she practices a wellness routine that includes spending time with her girlfriends to get their nails done or a massage, and taking baths at night to wind down.

“[But] honestly, without training every single day, I don’t feel as stressed,” she said. “[Everyone] can find different ways to alleviate their stress. And it looks different for everyone.”

As she approaches her 30th birthday, Biles says she’s learned to prioritize her physical and mental well-being.

“I feel like now I know that there is help out there if I do get stuck or I notice something, and I feel like maturity has helped a lot with that,” she said.

“And I just feel like being an ambassador and having awareness for so much has opened up the doors for me to say, ‘Hey, I’m not feeling okay. Can I please do this instead?’ Or, ‘Oh, I need to speak to my therapist twice a week because I feel like I’m stuck in a rut right now.’ So I feel like [aging] has given me space and… confidence to do that.”

#5042. Ultra-Processed Foods Linked to Higher Risk of Multiple Diseases, Death

  • A large study has found that eating more ultra-processed foods (UPFs) was linked to a higher risk of disease and early death.
  • The risk of chronic diseases increased with higher UPF consumption, but reducing intake may help mitigate risk.
  • Nutrition experts suggest making whole and minimally processed foods the basis of your diet.
  • Choosing healthier options, cooking for yourself, and avoiding fast food can all help reduce your UPF intake.

Eating more ultra-processed foods (UPFs) is associated with an increased risk of several serious health problems and even a greater chance of early death.

A large study published on September 22 in Family Medicine and Community Health found that those who ate the most UPFs were more likely to develop chronic illnesses, including:

  • heart disease
  • cancer
  • obesity
  • metabolic syndrome
  • diabetes
  • mental health conditions
  • digestive diseases

These individuals also had a higher overall risk of death. 

While there is no commonly accepted definition of UPFs, a classification system known as NOVA defines ultra-processed foods to be “industrial formulations with five or more ingredients” that contain substances that aren’t typically used in culinary preparations.

These additives are typically intended to make UPFs feel, taste, or look more appealing. According to the researchers in the new study, UPFs also tend to be high in sugar, salt, and unhealthy fats but low in essential nutrients such as fiber and vitamins.

UPFs are heavily altered from their original form and often contain additives intended to make them feel, taste, or look more appealing. They tend to be high in sugar, salt, and unhealthy fats but low in essential nutrients like fiber and vitamins, the researchers noted.

The researchers added that because many chronic diseases often occur together, reducing UPF intake could be an important step in preventing multiple health problems at once.

Effects of UPF intake on health outcomes

The researchers conducted their study by combining the results of several different studies to get a clearer overall picture.

Specifically, they looked at studies where groups of people were followed over time to see how their habits affected their health.

To be included, studies had to involve adults aged 18 or older who were initially free of specific diseases. The studies also needed to compare people who ate the most ultra-processed foods to those who ate the least. 

The NOVA classification system was used to identify ultra-processed foods, such as candies, packaged snacks, sugary drinks, and ready-to-eat meals.

The researchers gathered data on health outcomes, including:

  • high blood pressure
  • cardiovascular events
  • cancer
  • obesity
  • diabetes
  • mental health issues
  • digestive diseases
  • death from any cause

In total, the team analyzed 51 studies involving nearly 9 million participants from around the world. The follow-up periods ranged from 2 to 32 years. 

Dietary intake was measured using food frequency questionnaires, 24-hour recalls, or dietary histories.

The researchers used statistical methods to compare the highest consumers of UPFs with the lowest consumers and examined dose-response relationships — in other words, how the risk changed with increases in UPF consumption. 

The analysis took into account differences in how UPF intake was measured, whether by grams per day, servings per week, or as a percentage of total daily food intake.

Higher UPF intake associated with several health risks

The study found that consuming a large amount of ultra-processed foods is linked to an increased risk of many chronic health problems. 

People with the highest UPF consumption had a:

  • 24% higher risk of cardiovascular events like heart attacks and strokes
  • 23% higher risk of obesity or overweight
  • 24% higher risk of metabolic syndrome or diabetes
  • 27% higher risk of depression and anxiety
  • 31% higher risk of digestive diseases, including Crohn’s disease
  • 12% higher risk of cancer
  • 18% higher risk of death from any cause

These numbers were derived by comparing people who ate the most UPFs to those who ate the least.

The researchers also examined how risks changed with each additional amount of UPFs eaten. For every extra 100 grams (about 3.5 ounces) of UPFs consumed daily, the risks increased as follows:

  • 14% higher risk of cardiovascular events
  • 4% higher risk of cancer
  • 3% higher risk of death from any cause
  • 2% higher risk of metabolic syndrome or diabetes

Additionally, increasing UPFs by 10% of total daily food intake was linked to a 16% higher risk of metabolic syndrome or diabetes.

However, the link between UPFs and high blood pressure was less clear. While the overall comparison did not show a statistically significant connection, dose-response analysis suggested an 11% higher risk of high blood pressure for every additional 100 grams of UPFs consumed daily.

The results were consistent across different ways of measuring UPFs and across different populations. 

The researchers noted that these chronic diseases often co-occur in the same individuals, a situation known as multimorbidity, which complicates treatment and increases healthcare needs. Reducing UPF consumption could help reduce their risk.

How much is too much when it comes to UPFs

Johannah Katz, MA, a registered dietitian at Nourish, who was not involved in the study, told Healthline that there isn’t currently a cutoff point at which ultra-processed foods become harmful.

“What this new research suggests is more of a dose-response relationship; the more ultra-processed food in the diet, the higher the observed risk for several chronic diseases,” she said.

Katz said she wouldn’t advise people that they necessarily need to eliminate all UPFs.

“A more realistic goal is to keep them from becoming the foundation of the diet and prioritize whole or minimally processed foods most of the time,” she said.

Katz further noted that just because something is ultra-processed doesn’t necessarily mean it is unhealthy.

“[A] sugary soda and a fiber-rich packaged whole grain bread may both meet criteria for ultra-processing, but they’re nutritionally very different,” she said.

How to cut back on ultra-processed foods

Vahista Ussery, MS, MBA, a registered dietitian nutritionist and chef as well as the Co-Founder of To Taste, advocates for a step-wise approach to reducing UPF intake. She was not part of the study.

“The first step would be to try to swap your favorite ultra-processed foods with healthier versions,” she said, noting that while many products marketed as healthy are, in fact, still ultra-processed, they can be a stepping stone to making larger changes.

Ussery said the next step is to start cooking more at home using whole ingredients.

“When you cook from home, you are fully in control of what you put into your body,” she said. “Look for simple recipes that aren’t intimidating and that you can prepare with the equipment you have on hand.”

Next, work on reducing your fast food intake, which tends to be heavily processed and less nourishing.

“If you have to go to a fast food restaurant, try to order the fresher options or consider adding fresher options and shrinking portion sizes of ultra-processed foods,” Ussery advised. “For example, order a small milkshake versus [a] large and add a side salad to fill you up instead.”

Finally, she pointed out that ultra-processed foods are “designed to be addictive,” which can make it difficult to stop eating them.

“The more you do, though, the easier it gets. Try to resist the temptation!” Ussery urged.

#5043. NBA Star Steph Curry Didn’t Realize His Blurry Vision Was a Sign of an Eye Disease

  • NBA All-Star Stephen Curry is opening up about living with keratoconus, an eye condition that can cause blurry or distorted vision.
  • Curry was diagnosed after former Golden State Warriors general manager Bob Myers noticed him squinting during a game and encouraged him to get his eyes checked.
  • Keratoconus can worsen over time, but early diagnosis and treatment can help correct vision and may help prevent the condition from progressing.

Twelve-time NBA All-Star and two-time MVP Stephen Curry is known for leading the Golden State Warriors to four NBA championships.

In addition to basketball accolades, being part of the Warriors dynasty made him aware of an eye condition he was living with called keratoconus (KC).

The condition causes the cornea to thin and bulge outward into an irregular cone shape instead of staying round.

This can cause blurry or distorted vision, increased sensitivity to light, glare and nighttime halos, frequent or rapid changes in eyeglass prescription, and eye irritation or redness.

“I didn’t know anything about it before I went to the doctor, nor did I really know that I had truly blurry vision or, like, distorted images and stuff,” Curry told Healthline. “It was kind of just the way I saw things, and for whatever reason, I didn’t ask the questions or go have an eye screen and all that until our general manager for the Warriors, his name’s Bob Myers, [pointed it out].”

In 2018, Myers was watching Curry play on TV and noticed that when the camera zoomed in on Curry sitting on the bench, he was squinting as he watched his teammates and looked at the Jumbotron. Myers took a photo of Curry squinting on the TV and texted it to him after the game.

“He’s like, ‘Hey, you were doing this the whole time they had you on camera. Have you gotten your eyes checked?’” said Curry. “And then that was the first idea of like, ‘Wait, is there something off about the way, you know, my vision was?’ Thankfully, I had access to go to an eye doctor and got a screen. And that was the first time I heard the word keratoconus.”

How treatment for keratoconus changed Curry’s vision

At 29 years old, Curry was diagnosed with KC and learned that it’s a progressive eye disease that can worsen over time.

“[There’s] a certain time window of your life [when it becomes] an irreversible sight-threatening condition,” he said.

Randy McLaughlin, OD, associate professor of clinical ophthalmology at The Ohio State University Wexner Medical Center, said the condition can worsen if not treated and may make obtaining a driver’s license or working difficult.

He said people with the condition are usually fitted with rigid gas permeable contact lenses, which are firm lenses made of breathable plastic and silicone that allow oxygen to pass directly through to the cornea. Some people get larger scleral contact lenses, which are wide enough to vault over the cone-shaped cornea and rest on the white of the eye.

“Patients may be treated with a medical therapy called corneal cross-linking, which hopefully stops the progression of keratoconus,” McLaughlin told Healthline.

Once Curry figured out corrective lenses, he said, “I could actually see clearly and stop squinting, stop having those weird little headaches that would come, that I didn’t associate with my vision.”

While it took some trial and error to get his lens prescription correct and feel stable enough to play basketball, once he did, he couldn’t believe the difference in his vision.

“[I] was the most annoying person probably that first two weeks, ‘cause any time I walked in the room, either I’d be awkwardly staring at you, ‘cause I can see you clearly, or making a comment [like], ‘I can see all the dust in the air, like, this is incredible,” Curry said.

On the court, he could see his teammates better.

“[It] was just a whole new world, for sure,” he said. “[I] realized for the longest [time] I was just adapting and adjusting to what I saw.”

Curry continues to have eye screenings twice a year to monitor the condition.

Keratoconus symptoms and why early diagnosis matters

KC is most often diagnosed during puberty and the teenage years and progresses during a person’s twenties. Delayed diagnosis may increase the risk of irreversible vision loss.

McLaughlin said KC is easily diagnosed at an eye doctor’s appointment with an instrument called a keratometer. However, additional testing may be required.

Your eye doctor may test for it if they notice sudden or severe changes in your astigmatism or nearsightedness, if your vision hasn’t improved with standard eyeglasses, or if you have an irregular corneal reflection.

Although the condition is not inherited in most cases, it can occur in some families.

“Family history of every patient is important, including any family history of keratoconus,” said McLaughlin.

To spread awareness about keratoconus and the importance of annual eye exams, Curry partnered with Glaukos for its Could it be KC? campaign.

Because KC disproportionately affects Black and Hispanic populations, he especially hopes to reach these communities.

“[The] reach that I have and the authentic story of what this has kind of been like for me is the reason I’m doing it,” Curry said. “The work that me and my wife are doing in our foundation in Oakland — the kids that we’re serving — this is a message that they need to hear.”

To connect with more young people, he took part in an educational community event at Madison Park Primary in Oakland on Wednesday, September 23, 2026. At the event, he introduced a new picture book, “Eye on the Ball,” inspired by Curry’s own experience living with KC, and designed to make conversations about eye health and KC more approachable.

“You want to give a visual, especially for kids, to kind of destigmatize what it means to go to a doctor, ask uncomfortable questions, be in that environment where it can be overwhelming at times for sure,” said Curry.

He empathizes with children and people of all ages who are living with undiagnosed KC.

“I can only imagine [because] the way that I saw the court, sometimes struggling to see the screen to watch film, like, little things that you just adapt to and figure out, for younger kids, high school kids, college, [those] in the workforce, you realize…that’s energy exerted,” Curry said. “It’s a hurdle that shouldn’t necessarily be a thing that you have to deal with, but it’s only going to get corrected if you have the awareness to go get it checked.”

He encourages people to see an eye doctor if something doesn’t feel right.

“[There] were plenty of moments for me in that respect, where… things are designed a certain way to be able to see them, and if you can’t, you have to make adjustments to do it, or you have to ask somebody, ‘You know, what does that say up there?’”

Curry hopes that sharing his story encourages more people to schedule eye doctor visits and that more people with KC get the care they need.

“[Hopefully their] outcome is similar to mine, as well, where it’s just a matter of getting checked and getting the right answers for what your situation is,” he said.

#5044. Women With Enlarged Breasts More Likely to Experience Migraine, Sleep Apnea

  • A new study found that women with macromastia (enlarged breasts) had higher odds of chronic migraine, neck pain, and obstructive sleep apnea than women without the condition. 
  • The findings suggest that macromastia may lead to more than physical discomfort, potentially contributing to multiple health conditions that affect quality of life. 
  • Women with macromastia experiencing frequent headaches, neck pain, or poor sleep quality should talk with their doctor to learn about their treatment options. 
  • General macromastia is often treated with breast reduction surgery, but other strategies, such as a supportive bra and physical therapy, may help alleviate discomfort.

Enlarged breast tissue, known medically as macromastia or breast hypertrophy, is relatively common among women.

The exact prevalence of the condition is not fully understood, but some research estimates that macromastia affects around 1–5% of women.

This estimate climbs after menopause, with around 18.6% of women experiencing an increase in breast size, which may be linked to weight gain and hormonal shifts.

However, macromastia can occur during other life stages, such as puberty or pregnancy.

Women with disproportionately large breasts often experience discomfort and pain, and may turn to breast reduction surgery for relief. 

Now, new research suggests that macromastia is associated with more than just physical pain.

A cross-sectional study found that enlarged breasts are associated with higher rates of chronic migraine, neck pain, cervical radiculopathy (a pinched nerve in the neck), and obstructive sleep apnea (OSA).

The findings, published September 22 in Headache: The Journal of Head and Face Pain, add to a growing body of research examining how macromastia may affect overall health and quality of life. 

“Many people think of enlarged breast tissue primarily as a cosmetic issue, but our findings suggest it may be associated with a broader range of women’s health concerns, including chronic migraine, neck pain and sleep-related breathing disorders,” lead study author Kristyn Pocock, MD, assistant professor of neurology at Wake Forest University School of Medicine, said in a news release.

“By better understanding these connections, we can help ensure women receive more comprehensive evaluations and care for symptoms that may affect their daily lives.” 

Macromastia linked to 40% higher prevalence of chronic migraine

To better understand how macromastia may impact quality of life, researchers reviewed medical records from 687 females treated at headache and neurology clinics affiliated with Stanford Medical Center from 2017 to 2024. 

The cohort included 347 females with macromastia and 340 without.

After accounting for factors including age, body mass index (BMI), depression, and anxiety, researchers found that those with macromastia were:

  • 40% more likely to have chronic migraine (a headache on at least 15 days per month for more than 3 months)
  • roughly twice as likely to experience neck pain
  • 54% more likely to have obstructive sleep apnea (OSA)
  • more than twice as likely to have cervical radiculopathy, a condition caused by irritation or compression of nerves in the neck

The study was a retrospective, cross-sectional analysis, meaning researchers reviewed existing medical records rather than following participants over time.

This means the study does not prove that macromastia causes migraine, neck pain, or sleep apnea, warranting further investigation.

“These findings raise important questions about how enlarged breast tissue may contribute to painful symptoms for women,” Pocock said. “More research is needed to determine whether treating macromastia could help certain women find relief and enhance quality of life.” 

Why macromastia may affect the neck, head, and sleep

Researchers don’t yet have an explanation for why macromastia is associated with these health conditions.

One possibility, Pocock previously suggested, is mechanical strain. 

Larger breasts can place additional load on the neck, shoulders, and upper back, which may contribute to musculoskeletal pain and nerve irritation. 

Pocock has also discussed how other factors may play a role, such as:

  • inflammation
  • hormonal effects
  • mood changes
  • physical activity
  • sleep quality

Further studies are needed to determine the exact role these cofounding factors may play in the possible health effects of macromastia.

Neck pain may drive breast reduction treatment decisions

The researchers examined breast reduction surgery among people with macromastia. 

Of the symptoms studied, neck pain was significantly associated with undergoing breast reduction surgery, known as a reduction mammoplasty. 

Migraine, sleep apnea, and cervical radiculopathy were not significantly associated with this surgical treatment. 

This finding raises questions about whether some symptoms associated with macromastia may be overlooked when people seek treatment, suggesting another avenue for further research.

Could breast reduction improve symptoms?

Preliminary evidence indicates that breast reduction surgery may help some people with headaches, but the evidence is still limited.

In a small 2025 prospective pilot study, which was also co-authored by Pocock, 34 women with symptomatic macromastia and headaches underwent breast reduction. At follow-up, 79% reported at least a 50% reduction in monthly headache days. Headache frequency, severity, and related disability also improved. 

Another pilot study led by Pocock published in 2026 found improvements in headache frequency, severity, disability, and sleep apnea risk after surgery. The sample size for this study was also small, with 34 participants, highlighting the need for further research.

The researchers also emphasized that breast reduction should not yet be considered a treatment for headaches because larger studies are still needed. 

Sherry Ross, MD, co-founder of Oneself, Intimate Skin Care, and the Pair-a-Docs Podcast, said that women with macromastia experiencing chronic pain may wish to consider breast reduction surgery.

“In my 30+ years as a practicing OB-GYN, I have never met anyone who regretted having breast reduction surgery,” she told Healthline. 

Talking with your doctor about macromastia

If you live with macromastia and experience frequent headaches, persistent neck or shoulder pain, numbness or tingling, loud snoring, or significant daytime fatigue, it’s important to discuss all of your symptoms with your doctor. 

A comprehensive evaluation can help guide you to the treatment that you need, whether it’s breast reduction surgery or other methods. 

Your healthcare team may recommend a more supportive bra, physical therapy, or an NSAID or other pain reliever to help you find relief. 

“The most important step in improving your comfort and health is to get a properly fitted bra,” Ross said. 

“There are dedicated bra stores that can properly fit larger-breasted women with full-coverage, well-supported bras, with and without underwire… both for everyday wear and for exercise.”

Ross emphasized the importance of proper breast support for regular physical activity, a first-line defense against other health conditions like obesity, diabetes, and heart disease. 

She recommended exercises that are better suited to larger breasts to avoid excessive breast bouncing and discomfort, such as: 

  • swimming
  • cycling
  • walking
  • strength and resistance training

“Strength training that focuses on the upper body and core is helpful as well,” Ross said. “Select workout clothing that is comfortable, breathable, and moisture-wicking to avoid excessive sweat and moisture buildup.”

Lastly, Ross advised maintaining a healthy weight and eating a well-balanced, plant-forward diet to support overall health and longevity.

#5045. FDA Approves Lilly’s New Oral Combo Pill for Common Breast Cancer Mutation

  • The FDA approved a new oral combo drug for a common breast cancer mutation on September 18.
  • The treatment includes Inluriyo (imlunestrant) taken with Verzenio (abemaciclib) for adults with ER-positive, HER2-negative advanced or metastatic breast cancer with ESR1 mutation that has spread after hormone therapy.
  • Results from a trial show that among 159 participants with ESR1 mutations, those taking the combination went a median of 11.1 months before their cancer spread, or they died, compared with 5.5 months for those taking Inluriyo alone.
  • A blood test can show whether breast cancer has an ESR1 mutation, according to the FDA.

People with advanced breast cancer that has spread despite hormone therapy, and with tumors that carry a gene change called an ESR1 mutation, have a new treatment option. 

The Food and Drug Administration (FDA) approved the use of an all-oral combo pill on September 18: Inluriyo (imlunestrant) and Verzenio (abemaciclib), both manufactured by Eli Lilly and Company. 

Inluriyo blocks estrogen’s signal in certain breast cancer cells and helps break down the receptor that receives that signal, according to the drug’s FDA-approved prescribing information. Verzenio blocks two proteins, CDK4 and CDK6, that can help cancer cells grow and divide, according to its prescribing information.

Verzenio first won FDA approval in 2017, and Inluriyo was approved as a stand-alone treatment in September 2025.

The FDA approved Inluriyo and Verzenio for use together. Inluriyo shuts down the estrogen receptors that fuel the cancer, while Verzenio slows how fast cancer cells multiply.

Neil Iyengar, MD, a breast medical oncologist at Winship Cancer Institute of Emory University, told Healthline that, “the individual treatments are not necessarily new, but the combination is a new concept.”

Some cancer specialists already prescribed the combination, according to Komal Jhaveri, MD, a breast medical oncologist at Memorial Sloan Kettering Cancer Center in New York and lead researcher on the clinical trial that led to its approval.

“Now FDA has backed that with an official approval, so more patients can receive it without having any issues,” Jhaveri told Healthline.

Who is eligible for Inluriyo and Verzenio?

The combination is approved for adults whose breast cancer meets all of these conditions:

  • The cancer is locally advanced, meaning it has grown into tissue or lymph nodes near the breast, or metastatic, meaning it has spread to distant parts of the body.
  • The cancer is ER-positive, meaning estrogen helps drive its growth.
  • The cancer is HER2-negative.
  • An FDA-authorized test has confirmed an ESR1 mutation.
  • The cancer grew after at least one round of hormone therapy, also called endocrine therapy.

What is an ESR1 mutation in breast cancer?

In ER-positive breast cancer, estrogen attaches to proteins called receptors, which can lead to cancer growth, according to the National Cancer Institute.

Hormone therapy either prevents estrogen from binding to those receptors or lowers estrogen levels, according to the American Cancer Society (ACS).

An ESR1 mutation changes the estrogen receptor in a way that “makes it stay turned on,” Hope Rugo, MD, chief of breast medical oncology for City of Hope, told Healthline.

“And that mutation can develop while a person is receiving hormone therapy, and ultimately it makes the cancer able to resist the hormone therapy,” Iyengar said.

ESR1 mutations appear in about 1% of breast tumors at diagnosis but in 10% to 50% of metastatic cancers that have stopped responding to hormone therapy, according to the College of American Pathologists.

How the new drug combo differs from earlier treatments

People with metastatic ER-positive breast cancer usually start treatment with a hormone therapy paired with a CDK4/6 inhibitor, such as Verzenio, according to Iyengar.

The CDK4/6 inhibitor doesn’t work on its own, he said. When an ESR1 mutation makes the hormone therapy stop working, doctors can’t continue the CDK4/6 inhibitor by itself.

Until now, those patients typically switched to a newer hormone therapy pill, such as Inluriyo, alone, according to Iyengar.

The new approval allows patients to keep a targeted therapy as part of their treatment.

Pairing a hormone therapy with a targeted therapy helps overcome resistance and controls the cancer longer, according to Rugo.

“We don’t like giving single endocrine therapy all by itself anymore because we like to give patients combination therapy with targeted agents,” she said.

Pills instead of fulvestrant shots

Before the newer pills, the standard treatment after an ESR1 mutation was fulvestrant, a hormone therapy given as two shots per visit, according to Rugo.

Patients receive the shots every two weeks for the first three doses and then monthly, she said.

“But people hate the injection,” Rugo said. “Most patients will do better with the pill because they don’t have to have the injection.”

How effective is the Inluriyo-Verzenio combo?

Researchers added the drug combo to the clinical trial 6 months after it began, Jhaveri said.

To extend how long patients went before their cancer grew, “we understood that maybe we need a little bit more than one drug,” she said.

The team set out to answer the question, “Can we do a better blockade by giving them more than one drug that might target pathways better?” Jhaveri said.

The clinical trial that led to the approval, called EMBER-3, included 874 adults, according to the FDA.

Eli Lilly funded the clinical trial. The approval is based on results from a cohort of 159 participants with tumors that had ESR1 mutations.

Jhaveri said the study showed “that despite getting a CDK4/6 inhibitor in the first line, the combination of imlunestrant plus abemaciclib was effective.”

In that group, those taking the combination went a median of 11.1 months before their cancer grew or they died, compared with 5.5 months for those taking Inluriyo alone, according to the FDA.

Tumors shrank in 35% of people taking the combination and in 15% of those taking Inluriyo alone, the FDA reported. The FDA noted that these results come from a closer look at part of the trial, not from its main research question.

When the FDA reviewed the results, it was too early to tell whether the combination helps people live longer, according to the agency.

“There isn’t any survival data from any of these trials. The trials weren’t powered to look at survival,” Rugo said.

Side effects of Inluriyo and Verzenio

In the EMBER-3 clinical trial, 86% of people taking both Verzenio and Inluriyo had diarrhea, and 9% had severe cases, according to Verzenio’s prescribing information.

Diarrhea is a known side effect of Verzenio, according to Jhaveri. Adding Inluriyo didn’t increase the incidence of diarrhea compared with pairing Verzenio with fulvestrant, she said.

About 6% of people taking the combination stopped treatment due to side effects, according to a 2026 analysis of EMBER-3 published in npj Breast Cancer.

People taking Verzenio should start anti-diarrhea medication, drink more fluids, and contact their care team at the first sign of loose stools, according to the prescribing information for the drug.

The FDA’s safety warnings for the combination also include:

  • low white blood cell counts
  • liver problems
  • lung inflammation
  • blood clots
  • harm to a developing fetus during pregnancy

Doctors check blood counts and liver function before a patient starts Verzenio, then every 2 weeks for the first 2 months, monthly for the next 2 months, and as needed after that, according to the prescribing information.

Testing for an ESR1 mutation

A blood test that looks for tumor DNA can show whether breast cancer has an ESR1 mutation, according to the FDA. The FDA approved one such test, Guardant360 CDx, to identify people who may benefit from the combination.

“We would typically test for it when somebody was diagnosed with metastatic breast cancer, and then we would test for it again if their scans showed that the cancer was growing again,” Iyengar said.

Ask your doctor about Inluriyo and Verzenio

Verzenio and Inluriyo, plus an aromatase inhibitor, are currently listed as a first-line treatment option for hormone receptor-positive, HER2-negative metastatic breast cancer, according to the National Comprehensive Cancer Network (NCCN).

Breast cancer specialists are likely to be aware of the new two-drug combo, but those treated by general oncologists may need to bring it up with their healthcare professional, Rugo said.

Rugo suggested asking when it makes sense to check your blood for an ESR1 mutation, and whether the combination is a good option if your cancer is growing on your current treatment.

Questions to bring to your next oncology appointment

  • Has my cancer been tested for an ESR1 mutation, and when should I be retested?
  • Am I eligible for Inluriyo and Verzenio?
  • I’ve taken a CDK4/6 inhibitor before. Is this combination still a good fit for me?
  • How does this combination compare to my other treatment options?
  • What’s our plan if I get diarrhea?

What’s next for ESR1-mutated breast cancer treatment?

The FDA granted accelerated approval to Etcamah (camizestrant), developed by AstraZeneca, on September 4.

Etcamah is for people whose ESR1 mutation shows up during their first treatment with an aromatase inhibitor, a type of hormone therapy, and a CDK4/6 inhibitor.

Doctors start Etcamah and keep the CDK4/6 inhibitor at the same dose the patient was taking when the ESR1 mutation showed up, according to the FDA’s approval notice.

The agency granted Etcamah accelerated approval based on how long patients went without their cancer worsening, measured from the point when the mutation first appeared in the blood, according to the FDA.

The FDA has required follow-up studies because it isn’t yet confirmed that switching treatment at that point, rather than when scans show the cancer is spreading, gives patients a meaningful benefit.

Patients in the clinical trial that led to the approval had blood tests every 2 to 3 months, according to Iyengar.

“So technically speaking, what the FDA has approved is two different strategies,” Iyengar said.

Early results show a short-term benefit from switching treatment based on blood tests rather than waiting for scans, but researchers are waiting for long-term results, he said.

Researchers are also studying whether Inluriyo helps people with early stage breast cancer. That study, EMBER-4, has enrolled more than 8,000 patients, and initial results are expected in 2027, according to Eli Lilly.

Rugo said patients should watch for more drug approvals over the next six months.

“I would just emphasize here that it’s a really exciting time because people are living longer after a diagnosis of breast cancer,” Iyengar said.

#5046. Testosterone Prescriptions for Women Surge Amid Pharmacy Access Barriers

  • A recent analysis found that testosterone prescriptions for females increased 146% between January 2023 and July 2026.
  • There are currently no FDA-approved testosterone products specifically for females, which can create dosing and prescription access challenges.
  • Current clinical guidance supports testosterone therapy for hypoactive sexual desire disorder (HSDD) in postmenopausal females.
  • More research is needed to determine whether testosterone provides benefits for other menopause-related concerns, such as fatigue, mood, and muscle strength.

Over the past few years, there has been a surge in prescriptions of testosterone for females experiencing menopause, according to an analysis by the research analytics company Truveta for Reuters that was published on September 15. However, these results have not been published in a scientific journal.

The rise in prescriptions has also come amid reports of prescription delays and denials at pharmacies.

This may be due in part to the fact that there are currently no testosterone products approved by the Food and Drug Administration (FDA) specifically for females, despite years of off-label use and evidence supporting testosterone therapy for hypoactive sexual desire disorder (HSDD) in postmenopausal females.

However, on September 17, the FDA called for more data on testosterone therapy for females and encouraged manufacturers to conduct studies that could support future approval of female-specific products.

What can testosterone actually help with during menopause?

Organizations such as the Menopause Society support the use of testosterone for HSDD in postmenopausal females, but not for other menopause symptoms.

However, Stephanie Faubion, MD, MBA, Director of the Mayo Clinic Center for Women’s Health and Medical Director for The Menopause Society, told Healthline that evidence does not support the use of testosterone for most menopause symptoms.

“There is evidence for its use for hypoactive sexual desire disorder in postmenopausal women. Testosterone does not decline specifically with menopause, but declines as a function of aging,” she said. “It has not been shown to help with things like fatigue, well-being, bone density, muscle mass, or mood.” 

However, despite a lack of supporting evidence or guideline recommendations, some clinicians have been prescribing testosterone off-label to help with fatigue and loss of muscle strength.

Sherry Ross, MD, board certified OB/GYN and Women’s Health Expert at Providence Saint John’s Health Center in Santa Monica, CA, said that testosterone is much more important in the menopause conversation than people realize.

“This powerful hormone helps in promoting muscle strength, bone growth, libido, balancing mood and energy, and reducing anxiety. Since menopause negatively affects all these systems, giving it some consideration can be life changing in perimenopause and menopause,” she added. 

Testosterone has long been thought of as a “male” hormone. However, females also naturally produce testosterone. Testosterone levels decline with age, but this decline is not specifically caused by natural menopause.

Faubion also stated, “When postmenopausal or late perimenopausal women use testosterone, it’s very important that their levels are maintained in the normal range for a premenopausal woman.” 

How testosterone may help with hypoactive sexual desire disorder

HSDD is a common sexual health condition characterized by persistently low sexual desire that causes significant personal distress.

One study found that 52.4% of naturally menopausal females experienced low sexual desire, while 6.6% had HSDD.

HSDD also affects around 1 in 8 females ages 45–65. 

“Testosterone has been shown to increase sexual desire and improve other aspects of sexual functioning, including arousal and sexual satisfaction,” said Faubion. 

However, this is still considered an off-label use of testosterone. 

Why testosterone can be difficult for females to access

Ross noted that testosterone can be prescribed in various forms, such as lozenges, ointments, creams, and gels. She also noted that the dose you are given should be carefully discussed with your healthcare professional. 

This is especially important because there are currently no FDA-approved testosterone products specifically for females. As a result, clinicians may prescribe products formulated for males at much lower doses.

Because products formulated for males may need to be divided to provide a lower dose, some females may have difficulty getting their prescriptions filled.

Nurse practitioners told Reuters that they’d had prescriptions rejected by pharmacies, and many of their patients had trouble getting them filled. 

Amazon Pharmacy also told Reuters that it doesn’t dispense some testosterone prescriptions that require females to customize doses in order to ensure safe and compliant dispensing. It added that the policy is not specific to testosterone or based on a person’s ‌sex or ⁠gender identity.

More research on testosterone use in females is underway

Along with the FDA’s encouragement for more data and studies, there are more than 30 trials studying the use of testosterone for various health conditions in females underway or planned, according to ClinicalTrials.gov. 

For example, Avivo Bio is developing a testosterone therapy designed specifically for females and is studying its potential effects on breast tissue.

“Testosterone should no longer be an afterthought when managing the symptoms of perimenopause and menopause,” said Ross.  

“More research is needed to continue supporting its benefits beyond improving libido, but the train has left the station, and people are enjoying the ride. Its whole-body benefits should not be minimized or downplayed.”

Current clinical guidance supports testosterone therapy for HSDD in postmenopausal females, while more research is needed to determine whether it provides benefits for other menopause-related concerns.

#5047. Think You’re Too Young to Worry About Alzheimer’s? Maria Shriver Says Think Again

  • Author and journalist Maria Shriver is raising awareness about brain health.
  • When her father was diagnosed with Alzheimer’s disease, she made it her mission to inform others about what they can do to lower their risk of cognitive decline.
  • As a longtime advocate for Alzheimer’s, Shriver wants people to take agency over their brain health.

Maria Shriver wants women to know that they’re nearly twice as likely as men to develop Alzheimer’s disease.

As an author, journalist, and founder of the Women’s Alzheimer’s Movement at the Cleveland Clinic, Shriver has been spreading awareness about Alzheimer’s since her father was diagnosed with the disease in 2003.

Now, she’s teamed up with Eli Lilly’s Brain Health Matters campaign to raise awareness about the signs and symptoms of Alzheimer’s disease, and how early detection and intervention can make a big difference.

For Shriver, spreading awareness is a gift. “[Particularly] to women who say that once they find out that they’re vulnerable, they’ll change how they’re walking through their life, but they need the information,” she told Healthline.

She wants everyone to know there are proactive strategies to help support brain health.

“I’m really hopeful that people will actually take away from this, that their brain health matters, that there are things that they can do, and go from… feeling like there’s nothing I can do to feeling there are things I can do,” Shriver said.

Since she first started advocating for Alzheimer’s, the research on brain health has come a long way.

“[There] was no hope, and there was nothing you could do, and you just felt powerless, and you felt it was something that just happened to you as you aged,” said Shriver. “There’s been a sea change in that.”

The science, Shriver said, will only continue to advance. “I do believe that in my lifetime, there will be a cure, but I’m excited about what we know today. I’m excited that the conversation includes brain health,” she said.

Genetic factors that may influence Alzheimer’s risk

Genetics plays a complicated role in Alzheimer’s disease, said Heather Snyder, PhD, senior vice president of Medical & Scientific Operations at the Alzheimer’s Association.

“[There’s] still a lot that we don’t know and a lot that we’re understanding about risk and what may make somebody’s risk higher related to their genes, or lower,” Snyder told Healthline.

For example, having a first-degree relative with Alzheimer’s, such as a parent or sibling, can increase your risk of developing the disease, but does not guarantee it.

“Most people with a family history will not necessarily develop Alzheimer’s,” said Snyder.

Researchers have identified over 100 genetic variants linked to a greater chance of late-onset Alzheimer’s (age 65 and older), the most common form of the disease. The known genetic factors for Alzheimer’s have started influencing treatment and research.

The most well-studied risk gene is ApoE4 (apolipoprotein E4). Snyder shared how the gene may influence risk:

  • You inherit one copy of the gene from each parent.
  • One copy of ApoE4 raises risk; two copies raise it further (with some variation across populations).
  • DNA surrounding the gene may also modify a person’s risk level.
  • Having ApoE4 variants increases risk but doesn’t determine outcome one way or the other.

Another genetic mutation, autosomal dominant, accounts for fewer than 1% of Alzheimer’s cases. People with this gene:

  • are likely to develop Alzheimer’s
  • may develop Alzheimer’s at a similar age to when their affected parent developed it
  • may experience Alzheimer’s onset much younger

If you’re concerned about your own genetic risk, your doctor can connect you with a genetic counselor who can help guide you through the genetic testing process and explain the results.

You can also find a genetic counselor in your area by visiting the National Society of Genetic Counselors.

Can you lower your risk of Alzheimer’s?

While genetic factors are outside of your control, up to 45% of dementia risk could be prevented or delayed with modifiable risk factors.

Most studies on lifestyle modifications and how they affect brain health are about cognitive decline and dementia. “So all causes, not necessarily just Alzheimer’s disease, but other causes of cognitive decline in dementia,” said Snyder.

Conditions like cardiovascular disease and diabetes are linked to increased risk of cognitive decline and dementia, but not everyone with these conditions develops Alzheimer’s.

As with genetics, Snyder said this is a risk factor, not a guarantee, and there is variability in why one person progresses to cognitive decline and dementia while another with similar conditions doesn’t.

However, she noted that research has shown that getting good sleep, knowing and managing your heart and metabolic numbers, and eating a nutritious, balanced diet are beneficial to brain health.

“What we’re seeing now is more translating from those associations into specific recommendations or specific steps that we can take,” Snyder said.

Snyder cited the Latin American Initiative for Lifestyle Intervention to Prevent Cognitive Decline (LatAm-FINGERS) study, which builds on the results of the U.S. Study to Protect Brain Health Through Lifestyle Intervention to Reduce Risk (U.S. POINTER) study.

The findings indicate that lifestyle interventions improved memory, thinking, and overall cognitive function in older adults at risk of dementia across various Latin American countries. The strongest gains were seen in participants receiving structured support and coaching. These interventions included:

  • physical activity
  • healthy eating
  • cognitive training
  • social engagement

Shriver said when it comes to lifestyle factors, she takes what she can control to heart.

“I meditate every morning. I pray; that’s my go-to meditation, and I exercise kind of first thing every morning,” she said. “Those are my three kinds of non-negotiables — and coffee.”

She also prioritizes challenging her brain by learning new things at work and playing games.

“I’m now kind of deep in Mahjong,” Shriver said. “[That] brings me to social engagement. I’m in a Mahjong group with younger people, so that helps me keep and make new friends, which is also an important thing.”

Shriver continues her father’s mission to find a cure

Shriver believes her father would be amazed at how far science has come, but she said he would be more focused on pushing her to keep making a difference.

“[He’d] tell me to get on it and, you know, find the cure,” Shriver said. “So I think that’s kind of how I am, as well…let’s fund the research that will get us to the finish line.”

She feels both hope and urgency as she advocates for more answers and new treatments.

“That’s why I think it’s really important to continually talk about [the] information available to us today that did not exist five years ago,” said Shriver.

Informing adults about the importance of brain health during midlife, she said, is her top priority.
